PAGE 1
AccDSL
Company Histories,
Accounting Interpretations,
and Scenario Simulation
Product and method proposal
Version 5.28 (proposal candidate)
27 October 2026
This document specifies a system that translates company disclosures into traceable evidence,
represents several compatible economic and accounting histories, and simulates their future
journals and financial statements under explicit economic scenarios.
The proposed data contracts, methods and acceptance cases specify the intended implementation.
Current source and domain limitations remain explicit; this candidate is not a completed product
or a claim of full proposal readiness. Proposed libraries can be replaced while preserving those
contracts. Sector rule packages require their own accounting review before they can be described
as compliant with a reporting standard.
Research materials, source qualifications and review records accompany this document in
docs/design/research and docs/design/review.

PAGE 23
Part I
Company histories and scenario
simulation
1


PAGE 24
Chapter 1
The same statements can describe
different companies
A financial statement describes selected consequences of a company’s activities. A simulation
must explain how those consequences can change. These are different tasks. A balance of
borrowing tells us how much debt is reported under a particular measurement basis and date. It
does not, by itself, tell us when debt resets, who may demand repayment, which assets secure it,
or whether management can replace it. Two companies can report the same debt and interest
expense and respond very differently to the same increase in market rates. Recovering one
plausible journal does not solve this problem. Even a complete journal may omit the contractual
fact that determines the response.
This part develops a representation of companies for which evidence is incomplete. Its purpose
is useful conditional simulation without concealing what the evidence leaves unresolved. The
numerical examples are synthetic teaching cases, not estimates of actual company exposures.
They make a distinction visible: a calculation can be internally exact while its interpretation
remains conditional on an unverified assumption.
1.1
A rate shock exposes the missing information
Consider a company with cash of 95 and debt of 100 at the forecast origin. Its recent annual
interest expense was 5. Suppose debt was outstanding throughout that year, interest was paid
when accrued, there were no fees or derivatives, and carrying amount equalled principal. A five
per cent fixed-rate loan fits these observations. So does a floating-rate loan whose reference rate
plus spread happened to equal five per cent. A mixture fits as well.
Let α be the floating fraction of principal. Assume a two-percentage-point increase applies to
that portion for the entire next year, with no cap, floor, maturity or hedge. Next year’s interest
payment is
I(α) = 100(0.05)(1 −α) + 100(0.07)α
(1.1)
= 5 + 2α,
0 ≤α ≤1.
(1.2)
The two terms are payments on fixed and floating portions. Historical observations admit every
value of α in the interval. They imply interest between 5 and 7 under the shock, but supply no
frequency distribution within that range. Uniform weights would be an additional judgment.
If interest is the only cash payment, closing cash is
C(α) = 95 −I(α) = 90 −2α.
(1.3)
2


PAGE 25
CHAPTER 1.
DIFFERENT COMPANIES, IDENTICAL STATEMENTS
3
Every result satisfies C + I = 95. The marginal intervals I ∈[5, 7] and C ∈[88, 90] do not
retain this relationship by themselves. The pair (I, C) = (7, 90) lies in the rectangle formed by
these intervals but is impossible in the company described. An interface may display intervals;
the calculation must retain their common dependence.
Suppose a later note establishes α ∈[0.2, 0.4]. Then
I ∈[5.4, 5.8],
C ∈[89.2, 89.6],
C + I = 95.
(1.4)
The family has narrowed because evidence changed. This is not an economic event changing the
contract. A reproducible historical forecast needs the earlier evidence family, while a current
analysis may use the narrower one.
1.2
Journals and economic histories
For a simple interest payment of 5, a journal debits interest expense and credits cash by 5. Both
fixed and floating histories can contain that posting. Storing alternative journals while omitting
contracts can therefore collapse economically relevant uncertainty. The journal is essential for
accounting consequences, but is not a complete description of rights, obligations and exposure.
Depreciation of 10 may arise from one asset or several vintages with different remaining lives.
Equal inventory totals may conceal different expiry dates and replacement costs. Receivables of
50 may consist of five customers owing 10 each or one owing 50. A customer-specific disruption
distinguishes these histories even when their published totals agree.
Write an economic history as h, its journal under accounting interpretation b as Jb(h), and
its report as Rb(Jb(h), h). The second argument permits notes to reveal facts absent from the
journal. Compatibility with report y means
Rb(Jb(h), h) ∈O(y),
(1.5)
where O(y) includes precision and context. A representation should preserve a joint family of
histories, contracts, journals and interpretations. Independently choosing from lists of possible
cash, debt and policies can create a company no coherent history supports. Shared evidence
need not be copied for each alternative.
1.3
Agreement depends on the question
Two histories can agree with retained reports, agree under one simulation, or agree for every
permitted future decision. These are progressively stronger requirements. Equal next-year cash
under one rate path does not establish equal cash after refinancing or early redemption.
For query q, scenario s and policy π, write the requested result as Fq,s,π(h). Equality
Fq,s,π(h1) = Fq,s,π(h2)
(1.6)
permits shared calculation for that question if provenance remains distinct. It does not authorize
reuse for a different horizon, shock or policy. When learning changes decisions, agreement for
fixed action sequences may itself be insufficient. Later chapters include observation laws in the
equivalence condition.
The inverse-problem treatment in Stuart (2010) explains why inference requires an observation
relation and, for Bayesian conclusions, prior assumptions. We do not transfer its function-space
theory wholesale to accounting. The relevant lesson is that a forward relation need not have a


PAGE 26
CHAPTER 1.
DIFFERENT COMPANIES, IDENTICAL STATEMENTS
4
unique inverse. Possible-worlds approaches to incomplete data in Libkin (2014, 2016a) provide
language for answers holding across compatible interpretations. Neither identifies a company’s
actual transactions from totals alone.
1.4
Model completeness is a separate question
A model containing only fixed and floating unsecured loans may enumerate every feasible
assignment while omitting convertible debt, leases and guarantees. Completeness within its
variables does not establish completeness of the company description. An omitted instrument
may dominate the stress response.
A family therefore needs a declared domain: entities, dates, instruments, bases, documents and
event types. Its computational status must distinguish an exact characterization, a sampled
subset and an unfinished search. Ten witnesses establish ten compatible cases, not that only
ten cases exist.
Finite report compatibility also differs from statistical identification. Giacomini and Kitagawa
(2021, Sections 2.1–2.3) studies observational equivalence of probability laws and set-identified
parameters.
Two loan histories fitting supplied reports need not induce the same law of
every possible disclosure. Calling them globally observationally equivalent would overstate the
evidence. Here they are report-compatible.
1.5
Selecting a convenient history
A fit criterion may choose the smoothest transaction path or the fewest contracts among equally
accurate reconstructions. Such a selection can be useful for illustration. It does not turn
smoothness or sparsity into an observed fact. It can erase the maturity concentration that
makes a stress consequential.
If α = 0 and α = 1 both fit interest expense 5, a preference for fewer reset clauses may choose
zero. It then predicts interest 5 under the shock; the omitted compatible case predicts 7. No
historical residual selects the correct response. Repeating the fit does not resolve the missing
information.
Bayesian model averaging gives a probabilistic alternative after models, priors and likelihoods
are declared (Hoeting et al., 1999). Its weights are not counts of reconstruction rows. Dupli-
cating a fixed-rate record ten times must not increase its probability. This invariance under
representational duplication is a useful implementation test.
1.6
Mechanics and management responses
The rate illustration holds financing decisions fixed. A company may repay, refinance, hedge,
sell assets or issue equity. Every response needs resources, counterparties and information.
Automatically increasing debt to pay all obligations assumes financing capacity. Refusing all
adjustment is also a policy choice, even when it is useful for stress analysis.
Shahnazarian (2004) separates financial identities from behavioral relationships. Caiani et al.
(2016) specifies action sequences and financial counterparts in an agent-based economy. These ap-
proaches motivate separate accounting and behavior, but do not supply a universally transferable
issuer policy. Responses must be estimated, supplied, bounded or left unresolved.


PAGE 27
CHAPTER 1.
DIFFERENT COMPANIES, IDENTICAL STATEMENTS
5
An output can distinguish historical explanations from future policies. One result may assume
a floating share between 20 and 40 per cent and existing facilities only; another admits a stated
refinancing offer. Averaging these without justified weights erases the question being answered.
Conditional results remain informative even when an unconditional forecast is unavailable.
1.7
Trees are explanations, not compulsory storage
A tree displays mutually exclusive events, information arrival and subsequent decisions. It is
useful for a small example. The continuum α ∈[0, 1] already contains infinitely many compatible
companies, so a finite selection of branches is generally an approximation. An interval and
symbolic formula can represent the same uncertainty more faithfully and compactly.
Branches must not imply that a signed contract is redrawn each year. Fixed versus floating may
be a persistent unknown fact. Future demand may instead generate fresh realizations under a
declared stochastic process. A model must preserve the distinction.
Sometimes a formula suffices; sometimes a split at a covenant threshold is needed; sometimes
filtering a probability distribution is justified. In difficult cases the result must retain an
unresolved region or bounded approximation. None of these choices requires a separate stored
company for every imaginable world.
1.8
A useful simulation explains its dependence
For each result a reader should be able to identify its evidence, remaining histories, contractual
and behavioral assumptions, and computational status. Balancing a projected balance sheet is
weaker than this requirement. The model must explain why cash is available, why a payment
occurs and what changes the answer.
The remainder of this part develops reconstruction, learning, scenarios, financing, accounting
transitions and tractable computation. Failure cases accompany the mechanisms they test.
Their purpose is to expose a specific mistaken inference and its repair, not to imply that a finite
catalogue anticipates every future error.


PAGE 28
Chapter 2
Reconstructing joint admissible
histories
The useful starting point for simulation is a description of what the evidence permits. That
description must connect opening balances, transactions, closing balances, notes and contractual
facts across time. Without these links, individually plausible entries can be jointly impossible.
With unjustifiably strong links, the model can exclude real alternatives because an analyst
supplied a convenient default. Reconstruction is consequently both an accounting problem and
an inference problem.
2.1
An observation has a context
A reported amount comes with a concept, entity or consolidated group, period, units, mea-
surement basis and precision. Cash at a date differs from cash generated during a quarter.
Continuing operations differ from the whole group. Millions differ from currency units. These
distinctions determine which equations may use the number; they are not annotations that can
be restored after arithmetic.
Let observation oi contain value yi, context ci, publication time pi and source location ℓi. A
reconstruction predicts gi(h, b; ci) from history h and accounting interpretation b. If the source
rounds to increment di using a declared rule, compatibility may take the form
yi −di
2 ≤gi(h, b; ci) < yi + di
2 .
(2.1)
The half-open endpoint is justified only by the assumed rounding convention. Other tie-breaking
rules, signs and reporting practices require a different observation set. The printed absence of
decimal places does not establish an exact underlying value.
A debt balance reported as 100 million might therefore permit nearby amounts. A difference of
0.4 million may be immaterial for a broad exposure chart and decisive for a covenant lying inside
the reporting interval. Precision should survive extraction so that the query can determine
whether it matters. An arbitrary numerical tolerance chosen for solver convenience is different
from reporting precision. The first controls computation; the second describes evidence.
6


PAGE 29
CHAPTER 2.
RECONSTRUCTING JOINT HISTORIES
7
2.2
Stocks constrain net movement
For opening balance B0, signed movements mk and closing balance B1, the stock-flow identity
is
B1 = B0 +
n
X
k=1
mk.
(2.2)
It constrains the sum, not the individual events. Receivables increasing from 40 to 50 can reflect
credit sales 60 and collections 50, or sales 110 and collections 100. Revenue disclosures narrow
the alternatives, but returns, acquisition, currency, taxes and write-offs may add terms.
For an illustrative single-entity bridge, let A denote gross receivables, Sc credit sales, Tc tax
amounts included in receivables, K collections, W write-offs, X currency movements and G
acquired or transferred balances. Let L be the allowance and N net receivables. Then
A1 = A0 + Sc + Tc −K −W + X + G,
(2.3)
N1 = A1 −L1.
(2.4)
Each movement has a defined period and scope. This is a decomposition for the example, not
a universal reporting-standard prescription. If only net balances and revenue are observed,
interpreting the change in net receivables as uncollected sales silently sets several terms to zero.
A later credit stress then begins from a distorted exposure.
The repair is not to invent missing movements. Retain their domains, constrain them with
relevant notes, and identify the quantities the evidence does not determine. A zero can be a
verified amount, an explicit scenario assumption or an unsupported shortcut. The record must
distinguish these meanings.
2.3
A family of compatible histories
Let z collect unknown historical amounts and categorical choices: transaction dates, principals,
customers, asset vintages and accounting interpretations. Given evidence D and additional
assumptions A, define
H(D, A) = {z : Cobs(z; D) ∧Cacct(z) ∧Ccontract(z) ∧Ctime(z) ∧A(z)}.
(2.5)
Observation constraints reproduce the sources within precision. Accounting constraints preserve
valid postings under a specified basis. Contract constraints preserve promises and exercise
rights. Time constraints preserve sequence and persistence. Assumptions close parts of the
model that the evidence leaves open.
This separation makes contradictions interpretable. An empty family may arise from extraction
error, inconsistent reports, an overstrong assumption, an unsupported mapping or an impossible
contract interpretation. It does not establish misconduct by the company. The reconstruction
can be wrong.
Each constraint should retain its origin. A small conflicting subset may identify an annual
movement table, an overlapping quarterly flow and a mistaken sign mapping. That is a focused
repair task. Removing arbitrary constraints until a solver succeeds would make the result
untrustworthy. Relaxation needs a reason, a changed observation model and an explanation of
how answers depend on the relaxation.


PAGE 30
CHAPTER 2.
RECONSTRUCTING JOINT HISTORIES
8
2.4
Overlapping periods
If half-year revenue is 120 and first-quarter revenue is 50 under the same scope and basis,
second-quarter revenue is 70. Adding both reports as independent events counts the first quarter
twice. Reports constrain an underlying flow; they are not themselves transactions.
Let ft denote flow on elementary intervals. A report for period Pi gives
yi =
X
t∈Pi
ft + εi.
(2.6)
Here εi denotes admitted rounding or discrepancy and is not automatically stochastic noise.
Multiple reports can constrain the same ft. Their source identities remain distinct even when
their numerical information is redundant. The duplicate can independently confirm a value or
reveal a later revision.
A reconstruction must also distinguish year-to-date arithmetic from seasonal interpolation.
Deriving a quarter by subtraction is valid under aligned scopes. Dividing an annual flow by
four assumes an intra-year pattern. A cash stress during a seasonal trough is sensitive to
precisely that assumption. Even when annual totals are exact, the within-year timing may
remain unidentified.
2.5
Joint margins conceal concentrated exposure
Suppose total debt is 100. Foreign-currency and domestic-currency debt are each 50. Debt
maturing within a year and later debt are each 50. These two disclosures are different partitions
of one debt population. They do not give four independent amounts.
Let x denote foreign-currency debt maturing within a year. Compatibility requires
within a year
later
total
foreign
x
50 −x
50
domestic
50 −x
x
50
total
50
50
100
0 ≤x ≤50.
(2.7)
A 20 per cent rise in the domestic-currency price of the foreign currency raises the domestic-
currency value of foreign principal by 10. The additional repayment due within a year is 0.2x,
ranging from zero to 10. Matching both reported margins does not identify near-term cash
exposure.
Choosing x = 25 selects a midpoint. Assuming independence also yields 25 in this symmetric
example, but independence is an extra assumption about the joint exposure. A concentrated
currency maturity wall is a case that this assumption can conceal. One scalar with bounds
represents all compatible tables more faithfully than a list of arbitrary examples.
Additional dimensions increase the difficulty. Currency, maturity, collateral, lender and rate type
may each have published margins. Their joint table can be large, and feasible completions may
be numerous. Maintaining constraints on that table preserves the issue even when enumerating
it is impractical. Later query-driven elimination can remove dimensions irrelevant to a specified
result, provided it retains the relationships they induce.
2.6
Accounting and economic alternatives
Some uncertainty concerns events: whether a sale occurred, when collection happened or which
asset was pledged. Other uncertainty concerns recognition and measurement: applicable basis,


PAGE 31
CHAPTER 2.
RECONSTRUCTING JOINT HISTORIES
9
estimates and policy interpretation. Their alternatives cannot be combined freely when premises
conflict.
A debt cannot be both repaid and continuing to accrue its old contractual interest without
a further event explaining the continuation. A historical carrying amount computed under
one interpretation cannot be forecast under another for the unchanged instrument without an
explicit transition. A presentation difference need not imply a different cash obligation.
Attach interpretation b to its premises and consequences. Evolve economic state under the
scenario, then apply the selected accounting rules. Where the applicable interpretation remains
unresolved, report conditional statements for each admitted basis. An average of incompatible
book measurements is not automatically a valid financial statement under either basis.
2.7
Historical record and execution state
Future calculation rarely needs every historical event in active memory. It may need principal,
accrued interest, reset dates, tax basis, asset age and outstanding rights. The evidence history
remains important for provenance and later corrections. These are separate storage needs.
The evidentiary record should remain available and versioned. Execution state should contain
quantities sufficient for future transitions. Compressing the latter need not delete the former.
A derived state can point to supporting constraints and records, and many alternatives can
share common events.
An event log alone is not an inference representation. If an event’s occurrence is uncertain, a
definitive log entry invents certainty. Conversely, constraints without explainable event witnesses
make results difficult to inspect. The proposed representation retains symbolic conditions and
constructs concrete witnesses for explanations, regression fixtures and counterexamples.
2.8
Feasible, infeasible and undecided
A solver may find a witness, prove no witness exists, or stop without deciding. An adapter
may not support the instrument. These outcomes are economically different. A timeout cannot
become infeasibility, and unsupported debt cannot become a zero liability.
For the question whether cash can fall below 20, a compatible witness with cash 18 establishes
possibility. Failure to find one establishes safety only if the search is complete and successfully
proves the relevant bound. A lower bound above 20 applies to the declared family, horizon and
policies. Changing any of those premises can invalidate it.
Floating-point feasibility adds another distinction. A small residual is evidence about a numerical
calculation, not necessarily a logical certificate. Exact rational examples help detect semantic
errors. Larger models may require validated bounds, solver-specific certificates or an explicit
undecided status. The method used to establish a conclusion belongs with the conclusion.
2.9
Reconstruction as a source-research guide
The family can identify useful missing evidence. In the currency-maturity example, another
total-debt figure contributes little. A cross-classification can determine x and resolve near-term
depreciation exposure. The question is which fact changes the decision, not which source is
easiest to extract.


PAGE 32
CHAPTER 2.
RECONSTRUCTING JOINT HISTORIES
10
Without probabilities, candidate disclosures partition the family and give possible reductions
in the answer set. With an admitted observation model, one may compute expected value of
information. Assigning equal weights merely to obtain an expectation would confuse these two
exercises.
A practical sequence is to construct the important joint constraints, locate uncertainty crossing
a decision threshold, and inspect sources capable of resolving it. The source may not exist or
may remain inaccessible. The process still identifies the economic consequence of the gap and
prevents extraction volume from being mistaken for identification.
2.10
Provenance and multiplicity
Two records can support the same assertion, and one record can support several constraints.
Provenance needs to preserve these distinctions without treating duplicate records as additional
worlds.
The semiring treatment in Green et al. (2007) concerns algebraic provenance for
database queries; its value here is a disciplined account of how source contributions combine. It
is not a probability assignment to economic histories.
If a source is withdrawn, a derived claim supported by another independent source may survive.
If the withdrawn source supplied the only premise for a maturity date, the maturity should
become unresolved. Recomputing only displayed totals can miss the effect on future liquidity.
Dependencies should extend through contract state and policy eligibility to scenario results.
A stable identity for an economic fact is also different from the identity of a particular source
assertion. Two filings can refer to the same loan with different labels. Merging them solely
because the labels resemble each other risks combining different facilities; failing to merge verified
duplicates can double debt. Entity and instrument reconciliation therefore needs evidence and
reversible decisions.
2.11
A method proposal for AccDSL
The proposed reconstruction service accepts an evidence view, a declared domain and an
assumption set. It returns a persistent constraint family, its known contradictions or unresolved
mappings, and optional witnesses. Queries request possible outcomes, certain conclusions,
bounds or distributions under separately admitted measures. A request for one witness should
not trigger exhaustive enumeration.
Constraints should be grouped by their source and economic role. Observation intervals and
unit conversions can often be checked before expensive solving. Linear bridges and discrete
interpretation choices can then be combined, with nonlinear valuation isolated when possible.
If a nonlinear method is needed, its completion status remains distinct from the accounting
identities it evaluates.
The service should preserve both a stable source snapshot and a reusable symbolic family. A
later disclosure can produce a refined version without overwriting the earlier one. A scenario
uses a particular version and records which assumptions it adds. This is a concrete way to carry
multiple possible journals and economic histories without creating a full independent database
for each possibility.


PAGE 33
Chapter 3
Learning, persistent uncertainty, and
who knows what
A future disclosure may resolve an uncertainty about today’s company. It may also reveal
nothing relevant. Simulation must allow both outcomes. It must distinguish a change in the
company from a change in the analyst’s knowledge, and distinguish the analyst’s knowledge
from information available to managers, lenders and investors. These distinctions determine
which actions are possible and which apparent forecasting successes are merely uses of future
information.
3.1
Four dates attached to one fact
A loan can be signed on 15 January, take economic effect on 1 February, appear in a report
covering the quarter ending 31 March, and become public on 10 May. A system may ingest
the report on 12 May. The signature, economic, reporting, publication and ingestion dates
answer different questions. A forecast made on 30 April cannot use the 10 May disclosure
simply because it describes a March balance.
For a report-based forecast origin t, let Dt contain assertions available by t under the declared
availability rule. If the analysis uses public information, publication time is relevant. If it
models a lender with private contractual access, the information rule differs. Ingestion time
controls reproducibility of the system’s own operation; it need not equal the earliest public
availability.
A historical replay should therefore specify both the economic date and evidence cutoff. A
corrected extraction performed today may accurately recover a number that was publicly
available before the old cutoff. A new restatement published today was not available then.
The first can repair a data-processing mistake in an old evidence view; the second changes the
information set and must not be silently backfilled into the old forecast.
3.2
Persistent unknown facts
Let θ denote a contract characteristic fixed at inception, such as whether a loan is fixed or
floating. Let Xt denote changing economic state, including cash, accrued interest and remaining
principal. A simple transition is
Xt+1 = T(Xt, θ, at, εt+1),
θt+1 = θt.
(3.1)
11


PAGE 34
CHAPTER 3.
LEARNING AND PERSISTENT UNCERTAINTY
12
Here at is a decision and εt+1 a future innovation under the specified scenario or stochastic
process. The second equation is essential. Uncertainty about a fixed contract does not license
redrawing it each period. Refinancing or amendment can change the terms, but those are dated
events with their own permissions and cash consequences.
Suppose a loan is either fixed at five per cent or floating at the market rate, and the market
rate is seven per cent for each of two future years. The possible interest sequences are (5, 5) and
(7, 7). Independent yearly resampling adds (5, 7) and (7, 5). Those additional paths describe
contract changes that the model did not authorize. Their inclusion can alter liquidity minima,
covenant duration and refinancing behavior.
The same persistence issue affects customer identity, asset vintage, tax basis, priority and
collateral. A compact state must retain persistent variables even if current totals do not depend
on them. Eliminating a variable because it is irrelevant today is unsafe if it changes later
observations or events.
3.3
Set-valued evidence updates
When no probability measure is justified, retain a feasible family Ht. If a new compatible hard
observation y supplies condition Cy, updating the same historical scope gives
H+
t = {h ∈Ht : Cy(h)}.
(3.2)
This operation narrows the old family. It does not advance the economic clock. Forward
propagation then maps each remaining history into future possibilities under the transition
relation. A smaller historical family can still produce a wider future range when the horizon
lengthens or new shocks are admitted.
An empty intersection calls for investigation. A source may conflict with another source, the
observation mapping may be wrong, or a model assumption may be too narrow. Selecting one
convenient history to restore nonemptiness would hide the contradiction. The result should
identify the conflicting premises and preserve the previous view.
A restatement is not always an additional constraint on the old asserted amount. It may replace
that assertion in a current evidence view. The system should preserve the original source
and link the correction to it. Historical replay can then reproduce the original view, while
current reconstruction uses the revised one. Conjoining both as exact truths would manufacture
infeasibility.
3.4
Probabilistic filtering requires a model
If a prior measure µt and likelihood L(y | h) are admitted, Bayesian updating has the form
µ+

L(y | h)µt(dh)
R
L(y | u)µt(du).
(3.3)
The denominator must be finite and positive. Its role is normalization, not an opportunity to
substitute an arbitrary fallback when the observation has zero modeled probability. A zero
denominator can reveal a misspecified likelihood, an impossible observation under the model or
inappropriate handling of a continuous conditioning event.
For continuous exact observations, ordinary density multiplication must be understood relative
to the declared dominating measure and conditional distribution. Adding a tiny Gaussian error


PAGE 35
CHAPTER 3.
LEARNING AND PERSISTENT UNCERTAINTY
13
can make a computation run, but it changes the observation model. Its scale should reflect a
justified measurement or reporting process, or be explicitly treated as a sensitivity assumption.
Numerical convenience does not establish the company’s measurement error distribution.
The POMDP formulation in Kaelbling et al. (1998, Sections 3.1–3.4) provides a formal treatment
when transition and observation probabilities are known. Its belief state summarizes the history
needed for control under those assumptions. An unweighted feasible family is not a probabilistic
belief state merely because it is stored in the same data structure. Supporting both requires
distinct semantics.
3.5
A finite filter that preserves contract identity
Let θ be an unknown but fixed contract type, xt a changing hidden operating state, and yt the
analyst’s observation. A declared probabilistic model supplies transition probabilities Pθ(x′ | x)
and observation likelihoods ℓt(y | θ, x′). With joint posterior weights wt−1(θ, x), the prediction
and update are

There is no summation that changes θ into another contract type. A contract modification
needs an explicit event and a different transition model. If Zt = 0, the result is an evidence
conflict under the declared model; normalizing arbitrary fallback weights would answer another
question.
An uninformative observation has constant likelihood across states, so its update does not
change the predicted relative weights. A missing disclosure is uninformative only when the
observation model makes it so. If no prior is justified, propagate a feasible set of joint (θ, x) pairs
through the transition relation and evidence restrictions. That procedure produces support, not
posterior probabilities. A declared set of priors can instead be updated member by member;
zero-likelihood members need an explicit conditioning rule, and one must not replace the result
by independently chosen period marginals.
A source correction can remove an earlier restriction. Retaining only the last posterior may
then be insufficient: a zeroed type cannot be recovered by multiplying it by another likelihood.
Keep the original prior or feasible family, the evidence versions and the transition model,
and replay all currently active evidence. Checkpoints can accelerate replay only when their
evidence dependencies remain valid. The reference filter compares this recursion with direct path
enumeration for four priors and all 27 length-three observation sequences. It also checks rare
positive types, uninformative observations, conflict, and a correction that reopens a discarded
type. This is an exact finite method; estimating a defensible issuer likelihood and controlling
particle approximation remain separate problems.
3.6
An informative and an uninformative disclosure
Return to the fixed-versus-floating loan. Assume, only for this example, prior probabilities of
one half each. A report can say “floating” or “fixed.” Suppose the report identifies the correct
type with probability 0.9 and the wrong type with probability 0.1, independently of other


PAGE 36
CHAPTER 3.
LEARNING AND PERSISTENT UNCERTAINTY
14
modeled quantities conditional on the type. After a “floating” report, Bayes’ rule gives
Pr(F | y = F) =
0.9(0.5)
0.9(0.5) + 0.1(0.5) = 0.9.
(3.7)
These probabilities are supplied assumptions, not estimates from the loan balance. The example
isolates the mechanics of updating. If the report were equally likely under both types, its
likelihood would cancel and the prior would remain unchanged. More pages of disclosure need
not imply more information about the exposure of interest.
In the set-valued version, an exact contract note may eliminate one type. An ambiguous phrase
compatible with both eliminates neither. A probabilistic false-positive model is a different
treatment from a set of possible interpretations. The analyst should choose the treatment
supported by the evidence process and preserve its assumptions.
3.7
Why some ambiguity does not disappear
A long sequence of reports can remain uninformative about a parameter. In the rate example,
if fixed and floating rates coincide throughout the observed period, additional interest totals
may never distinguish the contract types. Sample size alone does not resolve an unidentified
direction.
The set-identification analysis of Giacomini and Kitagawa (2021, Sections 2.1–2.3) separates
parameters identified by the observable law from aspects of the prior that data cannot revise.
The accounting application is conditional: one must first establish which observations depend
on which hidden quantities. It is not enough to invoke a general consistency slogan and expect
all uncertainty to vanish.
Persistent ambiguity can also become irrelevant to one query without being resolved. After
final repayment, the old rate clause may no longer affect future cash. It may still matter for
explaining historical interest, estimating management behavior or evaluating a counterfactual
refinancing decision. Removing it from an active cash-flow state is different from asserting that
the evidence identified it.
3.8
Managers need not share the analyst’s ignorance
Public disclosures may leave θ unknown to an analyst even though management signed the
contract. A management policy can legitimately depend on that private fact. If managers
refinance only floating-rate debt after a rate shock, their actions differ across histories that the
analyst cannot distinguish. That is not foresight; it is a specified information advantage.
Let Ia
t denote actor a’s information history and let πa
t map it to a decision. Then
aa
t = πa
t (Ia
t ).
(3.8)
Two simulated worlds with the same Ia
t must receive the same action under a deterministic
policy. For a randomized policy, they must receive the same conditional action distribution.
The simulator’s access to hidden state does not entitle the actor to use it.
Different actors can have different information. A lender may observe covenant certificates before
public investors. A subsidiary may know its operating cash before a parent receives a report. A
regulator may receive nonpublic prudential data. The model should specify these information
channels where they affect behavior; assuming universal knowledge can make financing appear
available too early.


PAGE 37
CHAPTER 3.
LEARNING AND PERSISTENT UNCERTAINTY
15
3.9
A simple foresight counterexample
A manager must choose at date zero between two production plans. Plan A yields 10 if next
year’s demand is high and zero if low. Plan B yields 6 in either state. Assume high and low
demand are equally likely and the manager learns demand only after choosing. The expected
values are 5 and 6, so the expected-value policy selects B.
A simulator that first draws demand and then chooses the best plan in that realized world
obtains 10 in the high state and 6 in the low state, averaging 8. It has valued perfect advance
information. The arithmetic is correct for a different decision problem. The target problem
permits an average of 6.
The discrepancy is not repaired by drawing more scenarios. It is caused by an information
violation. A nonanticipativity check must require the same date-zero action across worlds
sharing date-zero information. In a tree formulation this often appears as equality constraints
on decisions at indistinguishable nodes. In a symbolic representation it can be enforced by
restricting the policy’s inputs.
3.10
Signals can have economic value
Suppose the manager can pay 1 at date zero for a perfectly informative demand signal before
selecting A or B. With the signal the gross expected payoff is 8 and the net payoff is 7. Without
it the payoff is 6. The signal is worth 2 gross and 1 net in this example. These calculations use
a declared distribution and feasible action set.
The same logic applies to obtaining a debt schedule before committing liquidity, but real
disclosures may be noisy, delayed or nonpublic. A signal’s value depends on whether the
actor can act after receiving it and whether the relevant action remains feasible. A maturity
note arriving after repayment cannot improve that repayment decision, even if it improves a
retrospective explanation.
Without probabilities, one can compare worst-case payoffs or regret before and after a partition
of the family. The result is not an expected value of information. An interface should name the
criterion because different criteria can recommend different research priorities.
3.11
Simulated learning does not revise actual evidence
A scenario may include a hypothetical disclosure at a future date. Inside that run, an actor
updates its information and changes its action. The hypothetical report must not enter the
analyst’s actual evidence store as a fact. Otherwise running a scenario can contaminate later
reconstructions with imagined information.
Separate identifiers should distinguish actual source assertions, simulated observations and
analyst assumptions. A scenario observation can refer to the source model that generated it
and to the hidden state of that run. It cannot acquire the authority of a filed report merely
because its text looks like one.
The distinction also matters for conversational systems. An explanation generated from a
selected witness should say that it is one compatible history. If the explanation is later ingested
as evidence without its conditional status, a possible event becomes a purported fact. Provenance
and type checks should prevent that feedback.


PAGE 38
CHAPTER 3.
LEARNING AND PERSISTENT UNCERTAINTY
16
3.12
Learning can change the appropriate compression
Two histories may have equal current cash and equal next-period payments, yet differ in the
chance of a revealing report. If the report affects a later refinancing choice, their future decision
values can differ. A merge based only on current accounting totals discards this difference.
Suppose two contract states both pay 5 next year. In one state, a scheduled certificate will
reveal a restrictive covenant before the next refinancing date; in the other, no such information
arrives. If management or lenders condition their offers on that certificate, equal immediate
cash does not imply equal future financing. The observation process belongs in the transition
model used for compression.
This is why exact aggregation later requires equality of the joint law of successor state, observa-
tions and requested outputs for each allowed action. Preserving a marginal cash distribution is
too weak. A graph may share current arithmetic while retaining separate information states for
future decisions.
3.13
Recursive ambiguity and persistent contracts
Bayesian conditioning and a fresh worst-case choice at every date answer different questions.
Suppose a persistent binary type θ is either zero or one, and tomorrow a fair binary signal X
unrelated to that type is observed. The admitted laws are Q0 and Q1: under Qi, θ = i with
certainty and X remains fair. For the loss Z = 1{X = θ}, either law gives expected loss 1/2, so
max
Q∈{Q0,Q1} EQ[Z] = 1
2,
EX

max
i∈{0,1} EQi[Z | X]

= 1.
(3.9)
The second expression selects type zero after signal zero and type one after signal one. It
therefore evaluates a new law with θ = X. That law is absent even from the convex hull of the
original two laws: every mixture retains independence between the signal and type. No type
has physically changed along a realized path, yet dependence has changed across possible paths.
Preserving path support alone would miss the error.
Shapiro defines rectangularity through equality between static worst-case evaluation and its
nested conditional counterpart (Shapiro, 2016, Definition 2.1, Theorem 2.1 and proof, Section 5).
His finite construction pastes conditional distributions across histories; it can enlarge the
family. A dynamic program for that enlarged family solves a changed robustness question. Its
convenience cannot justify claiming that it solved the original persistent-type problem.
Terminology also requires care. Pichler and Shapiro call a product family a rectangular setting,
but explicitly show that its original worst-case functional and its composite conditional functional
can differ (Pichler and Shapiro, 2021, Section 4.2, Example 4.7). Computing a conditional
law-based risk measure can also differ from conditioning an unconditional ambiguity set. Take
six equiprobable states and an observed three-state group with losses 0, 1, 2. Conditional
expected shortfall at level 1/2 within that group is 5/3. The unconditional six-state risk
envelope permits probabilities at most 1/3 per state. It can assign 1/6 to the loss-2 state,
zero to the other two group members, and 5/18 to each state outside the group. This is an
admissible probability law; after conditioning on the group it assigns probability one to loss
2. The conditional worst case over that envelope is therefore 2. The difference is between
two precise conditional risk definitions, not an arithmetic error (Pichler and Shapiro, 2021,
Example 3.2 and equations (3.1)–(3.5)).
Policy consistency and equality of risk values are distinct as well. Xin and Goldberg give
moment-constrained inventory examples in which an initially optimal policy ceases to be


PAGE 39
CHAPTER 3.
LEARNING AND PERSISTENT UNCERTAINTY
17
optimal after reoptimization, and another in which the optimal policy sets agree although the
two optimal values differ (Xin and Goldberg, 2021, Definitions 3.1–3.3, Observations 5–7, proofs
in Sections 6.3–6.5). The company model must specify whether management can commit to a
policy, what information each later decision uses, and which family of joint laws is retained.
For a finite reference problem, enumerate complete admissible laws and observation-measurable
policies before choosing an approximation. For larger problems, a set of beliefs over persistent
types or an explicit precommitment formulation can retain those restrictions, but its recursive
sufficiency and computational cost need a separate derivation. Repeated worst-case optimization
remains useful as a stress test when the changed family is stated explicitly.
3.14
Persistent parameters and particle approximation
A particle approximation can reduce computation after a probability model is chosen. It
does not create that model. A type with probability 0.01 has probability 0.99100 > 1/3 of
disappearing from 100 independent multinomial resampling draws. No new evidence is involved
in this loss. Rare but material types therefore need explicit support checks, stratification or
separate conditional computations, with the resulting approximation stated.
For state-space models with persistent unknown parameters, SMC2 combines parameter particles
and state filters, with particle-MCMC rejuvenation (Chopin et al., 2013, Section 3.2, equation (7),
Proposition 1, Appendix A). Its extended target has the desired parameter posterior as a marginal
under the source assumptions. Finite particles still approximate that posterior; exactness of a
target marginal does not mean that one finite run represents every possible type. The unbiased
full particle likelihood estimate used in this construction must be distinguished from an arbitrary
estimated likelihood ratio.
The inspected author-linked modern implementation creates a state filter for each parameter
particle, runs that filter through the current observation date when evaluating a proposed
parameter, and incorporates a likelihood correction when increasing the state-particle count.
These operations have real time and memory costs. Retaining only filtering arrays differs from
retaining complete genealogies. This is a candidate for explicitly probabilistic filtering, not a
replacement for symbolic unweighted historical ambiguity.
3.15
Changing particles without changing the target
Increasing the number of particles can change an inference result even before new evidence
arrives. The reason is that a likelihood estimate and its random construction belong to the
state used by particle MCMC. Replacing the estimate with another unbiased estimate does not
automatically preserve their joint distribution. A test that examines only current company-type
probabilities can miss the error.
Fix an evidence revision v and a persistent type θ. Write its prior as p(θ) and likelihood as
Lv(θ). A particle representation of size N uses random variables u with law mN(u | θ) and
produces a nonnegative unbiased estimate ZN,v(θ, u) of that likelihood. Assume the normalizing
constant Cv is finite and positive. The joint target weights the random construction by the
estimate it produces:
eπN,v(θ, u) = p(θ)mN(u | θ)ZN,v(θ, u)

3.16
A correction can restore an excluded history
Changing the particle representation preserves the likelihood being computed. Correcting
a historical disclosure can change that likelihood itself.
Suppose two histories have prior
probabilities (1/2, 1/2). An old hard observation has likelihoods (1, 0), producing posterior
(1, 0). Its replacement has likelihoods (1/4, 3/4). Recomputing from the prior gives
πreplacement = (1/4, 3/4).
(3.16)
Multiplying the restricted old posterior by the replacement likelihood and normalizing instead
gives (1, 0). The operation conjoins the old observation with the new one. It cannot recover the
history the old assertion excluded. The new-to-old likelihood ratio is also undefined on that
restored history, where the denominator was zero.
A replacement therefore requires replay from a sufficiently informative preserved checkpoint, or
a separately justified bridge with the required support. The inference request must identify the
evidence revision and which assertion is replaced. Merely attaching a corrected document to an
existing particle population does not establish the corrected posterior. This is the probabilistic
counterpart of retaining alternative histories that become admissible after a source correction.
The source’s quantitative guarantees require additional premises. Its Appendix B uses uniform
transition and observation-density ratio bounds and posterior regularity conditions, and its
refreshed-particle idealization is stronger than a finite PMCMC move (Chopin et al., 2013,
Proposition 2 and Appendix B). A permanent deterministic type, a hard zero-likelihood
accounting observation or an unidentified parameter direction can violate the intended transfer
assumptions. Exact finite target calculations establish the distinctions above; they do not
establish company-model mixing, scaling, likelihood identification or forecast accuracy.
3.17
Replaying a declared evidence vintage
The preceding correction becomes an executable calculation once the inference request says
exactly which information remains active. A document identifier alone is insufficient. Two
documents may repeat one observation, and a new document may replace an earlier assertion
rather than supply a second independent measurement. The model must retain these relations
before it multiplies likelihoods.
Consider a finite set of persistent company types h ∈H, with declared prior masses p(h). Each
active observation occupies a named slot and supplies a likelihood Lj(h). We assume that
these observation blocks are conditionally independent given h in this example. Under that
assumption, for a selected evidence vintage v with active slots Av,

Missing prior probabilities or likelihoods instead leave the calculation unresolved. Neither
circumstance supplies probabilities for a family that was specified only as a feasible set.
The selected reference, FiniteVintageInference/1, retains the named types, exact rational prior,
observation-model identity and factorization assumption. A request also names the actor
and information cutoff, the active observation versions and their replacement ancestry. Each
observation retains its source content, information identity, slot, likelihood vector, supplied


PAGE 42
CHAPTER 3.
LEARNING AND PERSISTENT UNCERTAINTY
20
actor-availability time and any superseded version. Its content identifier changes when any of
those fields changes. The semantic information identity answers a different question: whether
two records purport to describe the same informational contribution. A replacement keeps that
identity even when its likelihood or source changes.
The supplied availability time must be no later than the request’s cutoff. An unknown time
remains unresolved; a later time is ineligible for that request. The reference uses the existing
exact fixed-offset timestamp conversion, including fractional seconds, rather than rounding time
to a calendar day. Its convention excludes leap seconds and unspecified offsets. These checks
establish consistency with declared availability. Establishing when an actual investor, manager
or system could obtain the information still requires a source-selection procedure.
Replacement selects a new head for an existing slot and preserves the chain to the earlier
record. Retraction removes that slot’s likelihood contribution, equivalently replacing it by one
for every type. The request cannot select two heads for one slot, use a missing predecessor, or
treat a different information identity as a revision of the same observation. Several unresolved
candidate revisions therefore require several conditional requests. Selecting a head does not
establish that it is the authoritative version.
The numerical consequences are immediate in the two-type example. After the correction, the
posterior is (1/4, 3/4). Suppose a separately declared, conditionally independent signal has
likelihoods (1/2, 1/4). Multiplying gives unnormalized masses (1/8, 3/16), whose sum is 5/16,
and hence
πcorrected+signal = (2/5, 3/5).
(3.18)
Retracting the corrected observation leaves only the new signal; with the original equal prior
the resulting posterior is (2/3, 1/3). Retracting the only observation restores the prior. A type
assigned zero prior mass remains at zero unless the prior itself is changed explicitly. Restoring
support lost through an erroneous observation is distinct from inventing support absent from
the declared model.
Repackaging the corrected observation under another slot would produce a different answer.
With the equal prior, counting its likelihood twice gives masses proportional to (1/16, 9/16)
and posterior (1/10, 9/10). The reference rejects two active observations that declare the same
semantic information identity. An earlier implementation checked distinct slots but omitted
this condition: its arithmetic tests passed while the declared information contract remained
incomplete. The repaired example makes the resulting overconfidence visible. False distinct
identities can still conceal repeated information, just as a false independence assumption can
make a well-formed calculation substantively wrong.
A checkpoint retains its complete request and computed result. Reuse requires the same model
and prior, actor and exact cutoff, with the old active records forming an unchanged prefix
of the new sequence. New independent records can then update that prefix. A replacement
that changes the prefix instead causes replay from the preserved prior. This avoids the failed
operation that starts from (1, 0) and attempts to recover the excluded type by reweighting it.
Corrupt checkpoint contents or incompatible model and information definitions are rejected
rather than silently relabelled.
In this finite reference the old checkpoint calculation is itself recomputed before reuse, so the
test establishes the posterior relation without claiming a computational saving. Safe reuse from
a larger store would require a proved sufficient dependency set. Particle checkpoints would
additionally retain the likelihood estimate, proposal and ancestor state required by the extended
target. A vector of posterior weights alone is insufficient.
The reference compares complete masses and normalizers over a finite rational test grid and
explicit contrary cases, including a correction, retraction, duplicate information, unknown times


PAGE 43
CHAPTER 3.
LEARNING AND PERSISTENT UNCERTAINTY
21
and a later fractional-second timestamp. An uninformative observation leaves both types alive.
This illustrates a representation in which uncertainty can persist without enumerating a new
copy of every historical node. It establishes the exact finite calculation under its declared
premises; continuous inference, source completeness and the suitability of the likelihood for a
real company remain further questions.
3.18
A correction reaches different decision makers at different
times
Replaying the corrected history answers an inference question. It does not yet say which
decision maker could use the correction. Suppose the analyst learns on 1 February 2026 that the
earlier observation was wrong, while the manager receives that correction on 1 March. On 15
February they possess different information even though they are studying the same company.
A simulation of the manager’s choice must use the manager’s information.
Continue the hypothetical fixed-versus-floating example. Under the original observation the
posterior is (1, 0); under the correction it is (1/4, 3/4). Suppose an unhedged exposure costs
zero for the fixed type and 20 for the floating type. A perfect hedge costs 6 in either case.
These are supplied losses in the same units, and both actions are assumed feasible. They are
enough to define this comparison without inventing an observed hedging price or an accounting
transaction.
Let p denote the probability of the floating type under the particular actor’s available information.
The expected losses are therefore
L(unhedged | p) = 20p,
L(hedged | p) = 6.
(3.19)
The original posterior gives an unhedged loss of zero. The corrected posterior gives 15, so the
hedge has the smaller declared expected loss. At p = 3/10 the two losses both equal 6 and both
actions remain minimizers. An implementation must retain that tie unless a separate choice
rule is supplied. The manager on 15 February still uses the old posterior, while the analyst can
already select the hedge under the corrected one.
The selected information procedure takes an actor and an exact cutoff, selects the available
unreplaced observation in each information slot, and passes that request to the finite evaluator.
Adding the future correction does not change the manager’s stored 15 January request or its
decision. Later knowledge can motivate a new decision, but it must not silently replace the
information used to reproduce an earlier one.
Unknown availability cannot be treated as known absence. Two incomparable available revisions
also leave the request unresolved; choosing the newer file arbitrarily would add an authority
rule. The checked examples retain these distinctions, refuse corrupt observation content and
fabricated posterior results, and leave unknown action losses unresolved. They select actions
from the declared feasible set; they do not execute a hedge or infer its causal effect. Actual actor
access, registry completeness, instrument terms and feasible financing still need evidence.
3.19
A proposed information service
AccDSL should represent evidence views and actor information views separately. An evidence
view selects actual assertions by availability, revision and scope.
An actor view specifies
observable state variables, private contractual facts and signal channels at each decision date.
Policies receive only that view and permitted memory.


PAGE 44
CHAPTER 3.
LEARNING AND PERSISTENT UNCERTAINTY
22
Forward execution first applies events available at the current boundary, constructs observations,
updates the relevant information state, and then permits decisions according to the stated
timing. Alternative orderings are possible, but they must be explicit and tested. Moving a
decision across an observation boundary changes the economic problem.
The service should refuse ambiguous timing where it affects the answer. It can still return a
conditional comparison of both orderings. Such a comparison is preferable to silently selecting
whichever ordering makes the simulation easier. Exact timestamps, time zones and publication
lags matter when events occur on the same calendar date.
3.20
Tests that discriminate information errors
A useful test duplicates a world, changes an unobserved future shock and confirms that the
current action remains unchanged. Another changes a privately known contract term and
confirms that an informed manager’s policy may change while a public analyst’s evidence view
does not. A third inserts a later restatement and checks that an earlier frozen forecast remains
reproducible.
These are semantic tests. They do not require a fitted behavioral model. They establish that
information restrictions are implemented as specified. Whether a particular manager actually
follows the chosen policy is a separate empirical question. Keeping that distinction prevents a
passing information test from being presented as behavioral validation.
A final test removes all informative observations and confirms that persistent uncertainty
remains. A system that gradually collapses to a single history merely because a particle filter
resampled or an optimizer selected one witness has confused computational concentration
with evidence. The retained family and the approximation used for calculation must remain
distinguishable.


PAGE 45
Chapter 4
Scenarios, interventions, and feasible
decisions
A scenario is a specified change to a model, not merely a new column of forecast numbers. It
must say what changes, what remains structurally linked, what actors observe and how they
may respond. A rate increase imposed on a contract schedule differs from conditioning an
empirical model on periods when rates were high. A revenue decline with unchanged prices
differs from one caused by price reductions. These distinctions determine the cash flows and
the meaning of the result.
4.1
Describe the experiment before calculating it
A scenario request should identify the forecast origin, horizon, evidence view, shock variables,
shock timing, transition rules, management policy and requested outputs. It should also specify
whether the shock is a deterministic path, a random process or an ambiguity set. A phrase
such as “rates up 200 basis points” leaves reset dates, currency curves, spread behavior and
duration unresolved.
For a deterministic scenario s, let ut(s) be the imposed external path. The company evolves as
Xt+1 = Tt(Xt, θ, at, ut+1(s)),
at = πt(It).
(4.1)
The transition includes contractual events and operational behavior. The policy uses information
available at its decision time. A statement projection is then a reporting map of the resulting
events and states. Directly shocking a reported total can be a useful reduced-form approximation,
but it requires an explanation of how the corresponding events and balances are reconstructed.
A scenario can also specify a set S of paths. The outcome family becomes
Y = {F(h, s, π) : h ∈H, s ∈S, π ∈Π}.
(4.2)
This expression is not automatically a Cartesian product of arbitrary histories, shocks and
policies. Feasibility may couple them. A lender’s offer can depend on the company’s contract
state; a commodity shock can affect both sales and collateral; a policy may be admissible only
for certain information states. These dependencies must remain in the defining relation.
23


PAGE 46
CHAPTER 4.
SCENARIOS AND FEASIBLE DECISIONS
24
4.2
Conditioning and intervention
Suppose sales S and market rates R both respond to an economic factor U. Observing high
rates can reveal information about U and therefore about sales. Imposing a rate change while
holding the structural equation for sales fixed asks a different question. The distinction is
formalized in causal models by separating conditioning from intervention.
In a simple illustration, let R = U and S = 100 −10U, where U is either zero or one. Observing
R = 1 implies U = 1 and sales 90. An intervention setting R = 1 replaces the rate equation. It
does not, by itself, change U or the sales equation. If U = 0 in the starting world, sales remain
100 under that intervention.
This is a local derivation illustrating the distinction in Pearl (2009, Sections 3.2–3.4). It is not
an empirical claim that rates have no effect on sales. A richer structural model may include a
direct rate effect, feedback and expectations. The point is that the intervention must name
which mechanisms change. An observed correlation cannot decide that intervention on its own.
For financial stress work, a reduced-form scenario may intentionally impose several joint paths
without claiming a causal intervention. That is a valid conditional exercise if described accurately.
It should not be presented as the causal effect of one variable when the exercise changes several
variables simultaneously.
4.3
One shock can enter several contracts
A market-rate shock may affect floating debt, deposit income, derivative settlements, pension
valuation and refinancing prices. Applying it independently to each reported line can miss
offsets or count them twice. The shock should enter the relevant primitive variables, and the
affected contracts should generate their consequences.
Suppose floating debt of 100 and a receive-floating/pay-fixed swap of notional 60 share the same
reference rate and reset date. Ignoring basis, timing and credit effects, a two-point increase
raises debt interest by 2 and swap receipts by 1.2, leaving a net increase in cash interest cost
of 0.8. Applying the shock to net finance cost and again to the swap would double count the
hedge.
The simplification is material. A different reset date, reference index, currency, collateral
agreement or counterparty default changes the result. The correct representation preserves
separate contracts and links them through the common rate process. An accounting hedge
designation is another relationship; it does not itself create the economic offset.
4.4
Timing changes exposure
For principal N and rate increment ∆r applying over year fraction δ, the simple incremental
payment is N∆rδ. A full-year shock on 100 at two points gives 2. A half-year exposure gives 1.
If the debt resets only after maturity, the current contract’s payment may not change at all.
The year fraction must use the contract’s convention and relevant dates. Replacing actual reset
schedules with annual averages can preserve annual interest in a calm period while changing
a short-horizon liquidity stress. A payment on the last day of a quarter and one on the first
day of the next quarter have different reporting and liquidity consequences even if their annual
totals agree.
A simulation should state whether events at a boundary occur before or after a decision.


PAGE 47
CHAPTER 4.
SCENARIOS AND FEASIBLE DECISIONS
25
Refinancing proceeds received after an old debt payment cannot fund that payment unless
a bridge or intraday facility is included. Netting same-day totals can conceal this ordering
problem.
4.5
Behavioral closure
Accounting identities do not determine production, hiring, investment, dividends or borrowing.
A simulation needs rules for these choices. They may be supplied policies, estimated relationships,
optimization problems or bounded alternatives. Each has a different evidentiary status.
A fixed payout ratio is easy to calculate but can produce dividends when cash is unavailable or
legal restrictions bind. A cash-target policy can call for borrowing beyond committed facilities.
An optimal investment policy can require knowledge of demand the manager does not possess.
The policy must be evaluated together with feasibility and information, not appended after the
financial statements have been balanced.
Let At(Xt, It) be the feasible action set. A policy is admissible only if
πt(It) ∈At(Xt, It)
(4.3)
for the worlds it claims to govern. If the actor cannot observe Xt fully, feasibility may itself be
uncertain from that actor’s perspective. The model then needs a rule for attempted actions,
acceptance by counterparties and consequences of rejection. Assuming successful execution
whenever management requests funding is a substantive optimism assumption.
4.6
Attempts, commitments and settlements
An equity issue may be proposed, approved, subscribed, priced and settled at different dates. A
loan may be committed but subject to conditions before drawing. A sale of assets may require
a buyer and transfer completion. Treating intention as cash receipt collapses these stages.
The event model should distinguish an action request from acceptance, a binding commitment
from a contingent one, and settlement from recognition. A financing policy can request 20 while
the lender supplies 12. The shortfall remains an economic state requiring a further response. It
must not disappear because the request was entered into a forecast cell.
These distinctions also prevent duplicate events. If a commitment is recognized in a schedule and
the eventual settlement is recognized again as new borrowing without linking them, principal
or cash can be counted twice. Stable contract and event identities support reconciliation from
request through settlement.
4.7
Feedback and simultaneous determination
A company’s borrowing cost can depend on leverage, while leverage depends on borrowing
proceeds and operating decisions. This creates a fixed-point or equilibrium problem. Sequentially
evaluating one formula and then the other once is generally not a solution to the simultaneous
system.
For illustration, suppose required proceeds are P = 20 and face value satisfies F = P(1 + y),


PAGE 48
CHAPTER 4.
SCENARIOS AND FEASIBLE DECISIONS
26
while the yield rule is y = 0.05 + 0.001F. Substitution gives
F = 20(1.05 + 0.001F),
(4.4)
0.98F = 21,
F = 21/0.98.
(4.5)
Using the initial guess F = 20 in the yield rule and stopping gives a different face value. The
discrepancy is small here because the feedback coefficient is small. Near a financing threshold it
may change feasibility.
This rule is a teaching assumption, not a market-pricing model. Its purpose is to distinguish
simultaneous equations from an event sequence. A proper model must also consider whether a
solution exists, whether it is unique, whether the implied offer is feasible, and which equilibrium
is selected when several exist. Numerical convergence to one point does not answer all those
questions.
4.8
Joint shocks and dependence
A recession scenario can combine lower sales, slower collections, weaker collateral values and
tighter funding. Sampling these shocks independently may understate their joint effect. Con-
versely, forcing every adverse marginal outcome to occur together can describe an intentionally
severe stress that is not a probabilistic percentile.
If a joint probability model is used, its dependence structure needs evidence or an explicit
sensitivity range. Correlations alone may not determine tail dependence. If a set of scenarios is
used, the feasible combinations should be declared. A collection of marginal intervals does not
establish that every combination is economically possible.
Historical and future uncertainty can also be dependent. A company with short-term debt may
follow a different liquidity policy from one with long-term debt. Treating debt maturity and
cash management as independent can generate histories inconsistent with the assumed behavior.
The model should either retain the dependence or state that the policies are deliberately varied
independently for a conditional stress comparison.
4.9
Counterfactual histories
A counterfactual asks what would have happened under a different action or shock while
preserving specified features of an actual or hypothesized world. It requires a rule linking the
factual and counterfactual worlds. Independently drawing a new company for each scenario
does not preserve that link.
If two policies are compared using the same initial history and exogenous innovations, differ-
ences can be attributed to the policies within the model. If histories or shocks are redrawn
independently, the comparison includes sampling variation. Common random numbers can
reduce variance, but only when the coupling preserves the correct marginal model for each
policy.
For unidentified histories, a useful result is the range of policy differences F(h, s, π1)−F(h, s, π0)
over the same h. Subtracting an independently minimized baseline from a maximized alternative
can combine different companies and overstate the achievable difference. The paired relation
should be preserved.


PAGE 49
CHAPTER 4.
SCENARIOS AND FEASIBLE DECISIONS
27
4.10
A threshold example
Suppose cash after interest is 90 −2α and a covenant requires cash at least 89.4. Solving the
inequality gives
90 −2α ≥89.4
⇐⇒
α ≤0.3.
(4.6)
After evidence narrows α to [0.2, 0.4], both compliance and breach remain possible. The equality
point belongs to compliance under the inclusive covenant in this example. A strict covenant
would require a different boundary.
The model can retain two guarded regions without enumerating individual values. The breach
region then triggers whatever cure, waiver or acceleration rules the contract specifies. It does
not automatically trigger liquidation. An unresolved guard is a reason to preserve conditional
outcomes or refine the family, not to choose the midpoint and declare compliance.
4.11
Scenario comparison as a decision aid
A useful comparison reports which conclusions hold across histories, which depend on a particular
contract assumption and which depend on a policy. For instance, a rate increase may raise
interest for every floating exposure but reduce the value of a fixed-rate liability under a separate
valuation basis. These are different quantities and can move in different directions.
The result should separate cash liquidity, book income, book equity, market value and contractual
compliance. A positive accounting profit does not guarantee payment capacity; a market-value
loss does not necessarily cause an immediate cash outflow. Combining them into an unnamed
risk score makes the mechanism difficult to audit.
A proposed dashboard can show a conditional envelope with witness histories at important
boundaries and a list of assumptions that change the conclusion. The envelope should retain
joint outcomes and timing. A worst cash value at one date and a worst debt value at another
may come from different paths; presenting them as one path would mislead.
4.12
Scenario failures and repairs
A scenario can fail because its shock is underspecified, because the model omits an exposure,
because behavior uses future information, because financing is infeasible, because the numerical
calculation fails, or because the economic assumptions are outside their domain. These failures
require different remedies. More Monte Carlo paths cannot repair an omitted contract or
an information violation. A more detailed journal cannot repair an unidentified behavioral
response.
AccDSL should therefore return a typed result: completed conditional simulation, unresolved
history region, unsupported mechanism, infeasible action, economic distress event, or numerical
failure. A run may contain several statuses across different regions. Dropping failed regions and
renormalizing successful ones changes the target distribution and can systematically remove the
worst outcomes.
The next chapters make the financing mechanisms explicit. Debt and equity are especially
consequential because they can appear to solve every cash shortage if issuance is represented only
as a balancing entry. Their economic terms, counterparties, timing and rights must determine
whether the entry may occur.


PAGE 50
Chapter 5
Debt is a dated contract, not a
balancing amount
Debt issuance joins a cash transaction today to promises about future payments and creditor
rights. A journal can record proceeds and a liability without describing those promises. A
structural model can describe the promises without supplying the accounting measurement.
Scenario simulation needs both, together with a financing policy and a model of whether
counterparties accept the proposed issue. Confusing these layers can make a company appear
liquid while its funding equation is economically inconsistent.
5.1
Five amounts that should not share one symbol
For a debt instrument distinguish face or promised payment F, gross proceeds P, net cash
received P −f after issuance costs f, accounting carrying amount B, and market value VD.
They may coincide in a simple par loan at inception. They need not coincide for discounted
debt, transaction costs, accrued interest, modifications or subsequent market changes.
The gross contractual promise is relevant to redemption. Net cash is relevant to liquidity.
Carrying amount is relevant to the balance sheet under the selected basis. Market value is
relevant to valuation or repurchase. A debt-to-equity covenant may use yet another defined
measure. Reusing one variable called debt for all of these quantities creates errors that can
remain hidden in a par, no-fee example.
A debt record also needs currency, lender, borrower, issue and settlement dates, maturity,
coupon or reset rule, day count, amortization, seniority, collateral, covenants, options and
modification rights. Unknown terms remain unknown. A model can support a bounded profile
without implementing every clause, but its unsupported terms must not silently default to
benign values.
5.2
The one-period pricing equation
Consider a loan issued at date zero and settled at date one. Let D be the date-one default
indicator. If there is no default, the lender receives promised amount F. If default occurs, the
lender receives actual recovery R, measured in date-one currency units. Let r be a risk-free
one-period rate. Under the illustrative assumption that discounting the expected payoff at r is
28


PAGE 51
CHAPTER 5.
DEBT CONTRACTS AND FINANCING
29
appropriate, lender break-even is
P(1 + r) = E[(1 −D)F + DR].
(5.1)
This equation is a pricing assumption for the example. Under physical probabilities, discounting
at a risk-free rate requires an appropriate risk-neutrality or pricing justification. More generally,
with stochastic discount factor M,
P = E[M((1 −D)F + DR)].
(5.2)
An explicitly justified pricing measure can provide an equivalent formulation. A real-world
default probability is not automatically a risk-neutral probability.
Define the promised yield y by F = P(1 + y) for positive proceeds and a single final payment.
Then
P =
F
1 + y.
(5.3)
The yield is a ratio of the promised repayment to the price, not the realized return in default.
With coupons or multiple dates, a yield-to-maturity equation replaces this simple relation and
may have additional conventions. The cash schedule remains the primitive description.
5.3
The convention problem in the supplied note
The user-supplied lecture note by Chak Wong adapts the risky-debt model of Strebulaev and
Whited (2012, Section 3.6) to residual-based deep learning (Wong, 2026). It correctly recognizes
that equity value, default and debt pricing interact. Its inherited debt convention requires
repair before implementation.
The retained December 2012 source’s equation (3.26) raises b′/(1 + er) at issuance and carries
b′ into the next period’s inherited debt. That use makes b′ a promised repayment. Equation
(3.27), however, equates b′(1 + r) to an expected payoff whose nondefault leg is b′(1 + er). That
use makes b′ cash advanced. Both printed equations were inspected in the retained primary
source; the mismatch is not merely a transcription error in the supplied note. The publisher’s
current correction status has not been cleared.
The consistent face-value convention uses F for the next promised payment and P for issuance
cash, as in equations (5.1)–(5.3). Substituting gives
F(1 + r)
1 + y
= E[(1 −D)F + DR].
(5.4)
Multiplying by 1 + y yields
F(1 + r) = (1 + y)E[(1 −D)F + DR].
(5.5)
The multiplier applies to recovery as well as to the nondefault payoff. Omitting it on recovery
changes the equation when recovery is positive. Alternatively one can formulate the entire
model in proceeds units, but then future liability state, repayment and recovery arguments
must change consistently. Renaming one symbol in the pricing equation is insufficient.

are synthetic inputs chosen to expose the convention. The expected payoff is
0.9(100) + 0.1(40) = 94.

This is a lender break-even residual measured in date-one cash. It is not a rounding error.
Zero recovery or no default can conceal the mismatch, so a no-default smoke test is inadequate.
