5.5
Recovery needs its own definition
Recovery can mean a fraction of face, a fraction of market value before default, a share of realized
collateral, or a cash distribution after costs and delays. These meanings are not interchangeable.
If recovery is R = ρF, the one-period expected payoff is F[1 −p + pρ] when default probability
is p. If R is an absolute asset distribution, scaling face does not generally scale recovery.
For delayed recovery at date two, discounting it as a date-one payment is wrong. A stochastic
discount-factor formulation must use the payment’s actual date. Recovery uncertainty can be
correlated with default and aggregate conditions; replacing it by an unconditional mean can
misprice the claim.
Multiple creditors require an allocation rule. Suppose distributable assets after costs are 70,
senior claims are 60 and junior claims are 50. Under an assumed strict priority waterfall without
other rights, senior recovery is 60 and junior recovery 10. Paying both creditors 70 per cent would
distribute 77 and violate the available asset constraint. The example illustrates conservation
and priority, not a legal conclusion for a particular jurisdiction. Actual enforceability, security
and restructuring rules need admitted authority.
5.6
Default is a transition, not deletion
An inability or decision not to pay can lead to arrears, cure periods, waiver, acceleration,
restructuring, enforcement or liquidation. The contract and legal process determine which
transitions are available. A simulator that sets debt and equity to zero at a default flag loses
unpaid claims, assets, recovery timing and operating consequences.
A useful state includes scheduled obligation, amount paid, arrears, default status, acceleration
status and recovery process. A missed payment can create an arrears balance even if operations
continue. A covenant breach can restrict dividends without immediate acceleration. A waiver
can restore eligibility subject to fees and revised terms.
Limited liability concerns shareholders’ obligations and the value of their claim under the model.
It does not require book equity to remain nonnegative. Negative book equity, negative market
equity before applying limited liability, cash shortage and legal default are different concepts.
Their relationships must be modeled rather than inferred from a single sign test.


PAGE 53
CHAPTER 5.
DEBT CONTRACTS AND FINANCING
31
5.7
Refinancing creates a new problem
A maturing debt of 100 cannot be repaid by writing a new debt balance of 100 unless a financing
transaction supplies the cash in time. Suppose new face 100 sells for 90 and issuance fees are 2.
Net proceeds are 88, leaving a shortfall of 12 before considering cash reserves. Treating face as
cash hides that shortfall.
If the company requires net proceeds of 100 and the price is 0.9 per unit face with a fee of 2,
required face is (100 + 2)/0.9 = 113.333 . . .. Increasing face may itself worsen price or violate
capacity. The funding calculation is then coupled to the pricing and eligibility problem.
A committed facility may provide capacity but still have draw conditions, currency limits,
borrowing-base constraints or expiry dates. An uncommitted facility is not guaranteed funding.
A refinancing policy should distinguish request, offer, acceptance and settlement. Failure at any
stage leaves a residual funding need and possible distress transitions.
5.8
Cash, debt and net debt
Some structural models use a signed debt variable, with negative debt interpreted as cash. This
is economical in a stylized model, but cannot automatically represent a company with cash
and debt simultaneously. Restricted cash may not repay a particular obligation. Cash in one
subsidiary or currency may not be available to another. Gross debt can affect covenants and
refinancing even when net debt is small.
Consider cash 100 and debt 100. Net debt is zero. If cash is unrestricted in the borrowing entity
and repayment is allowed without cost, netting may be adequate for a particular valuation
question. If cash is pledged to another creditor or held in a subsidiary subject to transfer
restrictions, netting destroys liquidity information. The same net balance describes different
payment capacity.
AccDSL should therefore preserve gross instruments and cash accounts in the general repre-
sentation. A net-debt reduction is a query-specific abstraction requiring explicit conditions. It
should not become the universal state merely because a numerical paper uses it successfully in
a simpler economic model.
5.9
Coupons, accrual and effective interest
Contractual coupon determines a payment schedule. Accounting interest expense can differ
because of discount, premium, fees or measurement rules. For a simple illustrative amortized-cost
schedule with effective rate i, carrying amount evolves as
Bt+1 = Bt + iBt −cash coupont+1 −principal paidt+1,
(5.10)
subject to the selected accounting treatment and any additional events. The effective-interest
term is not automatically a cash payment.
Suppose a zero-coupon promise of 100 is issued for 90 for one year. With no fees, an effective
rate 100/90 −1 accretes carrying amount from 90 to 100. Cash is received at issuance and paid
at maturity. Charging 10 as both accretion and a coupon payment would double count the
financing cost.
Conversely, a floating coupon can reset while an accounting measurement follows its own
applicable rules. A simulation should generate contractual cash first from terms, then calculate

CHAPTER 5.
DEBT CONTRACTS AND FINANCING
32
accounting consequences under an admitted basis. A generic rate shock on interest expense
cannot substitute for these two mechanisms in every instrument.
5.10
Issuance costs and tax timing
A fee can be paid to an intermediary at issuance, deducted from proceeds, financed separately
or incurred conditionally. Its cash timing should be explicit. The accounting allocation between
expense and carrying amount depends on the applicable standard and facts. A model cannot
infer that allocation merely from the fact that cash was paid.
The retained structural source and supplied lecture include a simplifying treatment that credits
the present value of a future interest tax benefit at issuance. That can be part of an economic
valuation convention. It must not be posted as an actual contemporaneous tax receipt without
a separate tax mechanism. A present value is not a cash settlement.
Tax losses, deduction limits, jurisdiction, timing and the availability of taxable income can all
affect realization. A scenario that assumes every interest expense produces immediate cash
tax savings may overstate liquidity in distress. The proposed design keeps tax cash payments,
tax expense, deferred tax and economic tax-benefit valuation separate, then links them under
explicit rules.
5.11
Options and amendments
A callable bond gives the issuer an exercise right; a puttable bond gives a holder a right.
Their exercise can depend on rates, liquidity, transaction costs and information. Replacing the
schedule with the contractual final maturity ignores these options. Exercising automatically
whenever it appears favorable assumes a policy and ignores possible constraints.
An amendment can change coupon, maturity, collateral or covenants. It needs an effective date,
consent requirements, fees and accounting treatment. A revised schedule should supersede future
obligations consistently while retaining the original agreement and past payments. Applying
both old and new coupons after amendment creates duplicate obligations; erasing the old
contract destroys provenance.
Where a clause is unknown, the family can contain alternative exercise rights or amendment
paths. Those alternatives should be activated only by their premises. A model should not
give the borrower an option that belongs to the lender, nor assume the lender consents to a
concession because it improves borrower liquidity.
5.12
Pricing and policy interact
A debt price can depend on future investment, payouts and refinancing because these affect
repayment. The issuer’s policy can depend on the price offered. Solving the two separately while
holding inconsistent policies fixed can produce a price that is correct for a different company
behavior.
The structural-finance survey Strebulaev and Whited (2012, Sections 3.4–3.6) is useful for this
interaction. The newer working paper Duarte and Fonseca (2026, Sections 2.1.3–2.1.5 and
3) also solves bond pricing and equity value jointly under its own timing, recovery and tax
assumptions. Those assumptions differ from the lecture’s model; it is not a drop-in correction.


PAGE 55
CHAPTER 5.
DEBT CONTRACTS AND FINANCING
33
A general AccDSL financing service can accept a declared price or offer as an input. That
supports conditional simulation without claiming equilibrium. An optional equilibrium service
may solve pricing and policy together, with separate numerical evidence. The interface should
identify which service supplied the offer and what assumptions support it.
5.13
Credit supply and rationing
A fair price does not imply an unlimited quantity is available. Lenders can face capital, liquidity,
concentration or mandate constraints. A borrower may be unable to issue enough securities at
any admissible terms. A model that solves only lender break-even can miss rationing.
Bank stress and balance-sheet models such as Halaj (2016); Behn et al. (2019) provide examples
where financing and adjustment depend on constraints. Their institutional settings should
not be transferred mechanically to a corporate issuer. The general lesson is that a financing
transaction needs a counterparty and feasible quantity, not only an interest rate.
An offer curve can therefore specify price, maximum quantity, conditions and expiry. A policy
chooses among feasible offers or records no acceptable offer. If the curve is uncertain, results
remain conditional on alternative credit-supply assumptions. A scenario can deliberately freeze
refinancing, but that restriction should be visible as a stress premise.
5.14
What dynamic financing models establish
For longer-lived debt, lender receipts depend on future issuance, investment, priority changes
and default choices. A candidate policy π produces a price qπ. If management optimizes while
holding an incompatible price fixed, the resulting policy may not be the one lenders priced. A
simultaneous solution must make the chosen policy optimal under its price and make the price
value that policy’s promised and default-state payments. A constrained contract-price input is
a useful alternative for mechanical stress testing, but its interpretation is conditional on that
price and availability.
Benzoni and coauthors make this distinction explicit in a dynamic model with fixed issuance
costs (Benzoni et al., 2023, Section 2, Propositions 1–2, Appendix A). Their debt quantity is face
value, its price is per unit of face, and shareholders choose default, issuance or inaction. Value
matching and smooth pasting generate a candidate barrier policy; off-policy deviations and a
supermartingale condition are required for equilibrium verification. The retained model assumes
zero default recovery and risk-neutral agents. It therefore illustrates the policy-price consistency
problem, but is not the positive-recovery solution required by the preceding example.
The alternative managerial model of Lambrecht and Myers places investment, borrowing, payout
and rents in a dated sources-and-uses constraint and imposes a no-Ponzi restriction (Lambrecht
and Myers, 2017, Sections 2–4). Its safe-debt solutions depend strongly on managerial preferences
and governance. In its exponential-utility example, the liquidation covenant protects lenders
under the stated diffusion dynamics. A jump across the boundary, delayed liquidation or costly
asset disposal would require a different recovery analysis. A closed-form policy derived under
those restrictions is a competitor to consider, not an identified default policy for every issuer.
Dewatripont, Legros and Matthews provide a primary contracting comparison (Dewatripont
et al., 2003, Sections 2–6 and Appendix B). Their environment has observable but noncon-
tractible effort, limited liability, monotone investor receipts and efficient renegotiation. Under
entrepreneur-offer renegotiation, a debt contract can dominate a more general contract in the
stated pure-strategy class. Their appendix also supplies cases in which changed bargaining

power, third parties or mixed messages change that conclusion. Even their investor conversion
option need not be an ordinary equity security, as footnote 16 explains. Contract labels cannot
replace the exact contingent payments and exercise rights in the company model.
5.15
Equal balances after unequal payment histories
A borrower can recover from a cash shortage before the reporting date. Its closing statements
then need not reveal that it failed to pay on time. To isolate the timing, consider a synthetic
company with cash 60, inventory 100, debt carrying amount 80 and book equity 80. The
contractual promise is 100, payable on 30 June 2027.
Assume a zero coupon, no fees or
taxes, and one remaining measurement interval that accretes the discount of 20 at maturity.
This deliberately simple interval makes the cash and contract comparisons exact; a general
effective-interest schedule needs its own measurement rules.
The company issues 40 new shares for one unit of cash each. In the first history, the subscription
settles on 29 June. Cash rises from 60 to 100, the discount accretes on 30 June, and the borrower
pays the complete promise that day. In the second history, the shares settle on 2 July. The
borrower can pay only 60 at maturity. On 1 July an explicit observation records the unpaid 40;
the new cash arrives on 2 July and pays the remaining claim on 3 July. No overdraft, emergency
loan or retroactive receipt funds the earlier payment.
The following comparison follows the money and claim in the second history. The liability
column is positive for presentation, although journal credits use the implementation’s signed
convention.
