The zero in the first two historical-miss cells records that the observation has not yet been
made. It is not a finding that a payment was timely. The observation changes contract memory
without changing cash or posting another expense. The final payment clears the current claim;
it leaves the history of the earlier shortfall intact.
Both histories end with the same financial statements under the supplied treatment. Inventory
of 100 is the only remaining asset, the debt is zero, and equity is opening equity plus the
subscription less the discount expense:
A = 100,
L = 0,
E = 80 + 40 −20 = 100.
(5.11)
Profit for the illustrated movements is −20. Net financing cash flow is 40 −100 = −60 in either
history. The late path divides the repayment into 60 + 40, which changes its timing but not its
total. Even agreement across the closing balance sheet, income and cash-flow statements does
not answer whether the maturity was paid on time. A simulation query about missed payments
must retain the distinguishing history.
The difference also explains why balancing the journal is insufficient. Share proceeds can debit
cash and credit capital correctly while a faulty method fails to increase the recorded shares. A
missed-payment observation can omit its history update without unbalancing any account. In
the checked reference, both omissions pass generic accounting execution and fail the independent


PAGE 57
CHAPTER 5.
DEBT CONTRACTS AND FINANCING
35
complete-state comparison. Explicit financing conditions subsequently reject these proposed
transitions before accepting their successors.
Those conditions have two roles. Snapshot conditions constrain current quantities: claims and
arrears are nonnegative, arrears cannot exceed the remaining claim, and under this matured
zero-coupon convention the carrying liability equals that claim. Transition conditions constrain
how an event changes them. Payment of p gives remaining claim q′ = q −p, reduces cash by p,
and leaves the historical missed amount unchanged. A share subscription must increase both
the monetary capital account and the separate share quantity under the supplied issue terms.
An internally plausible rewrite of historical miss from 40 to 50 can satisfy snapshot inequalities
but violates the transition condition. This is why both kinds of condition are needed.
Opening history creates another distinction. A supplied fiscal opening must satisfy the declared
period-start, temporary-balance and flow restrictions; an intermediate state containing this
year’s payments is not such an opening. Importing a consistent snapshot does not establish that
it includes every earlier missed payment. The selected opening method therefore compares it
with a separately supplied history premise and preserves unknown completeness. Actual source
evidence is still needed to establish that premise.
5.16
A missed payment and the available remedies
The amount unpaid does not by itself identify the creditor’s remedy. Apple’s 2024 annual
report provides a concrete historical description of why the distinction matters. Exhibit 4.1
expressly calls its description incomplete and qualifies it by the 2013 and 2018 indentures; its
issuer references concern Apple Inc., rather than automatically every subsidiary (Apple Inc.,
2024a, Exhibit 4.1, printed p. 3). We use the summary to identify conditions a simulation
must represent. It does not establish an actual issuer default, complete operative terms, or the
applicability of those terms to the synthetic company.
The described interest-payment event requires default continuing for 30 days after the installment
becomes due. Principal and premium use a different condition: nonpayment when due and
payable, including maturity, redemption or declaration. Importing the interest grace period into
principal repayment would therefore apply the wrong described condition. The other-covenant
route has its own exclusions, a continuing breach and 90 days after qualifying written notice.
Holder notice uses at least 25% of outstanding principal under the 2013 indenture and at least
33% under the 2018 indenture; the trustee has a separately described notice route (Apple Inc.,
2024a, Exhibit 4.1, printed p. 9).
The thresholds concern the relevant outstanding series, not a fraction of consolidated debt or a
count of creditors. Series mapping, denominator, delivery to the required recipients and validity
of notice are substantive inputs. No notice and no evidence about notice lead to different
answers. Likewise, day-level data at exactly the 30- or 90-day boundary may leave event order
unresolved. The conditional reference retains that uncertainty; it does not assert that the
complete contract has no answer at the boundary.
The summary next distinguishes declaration from automatic acceleration. Certain continuing
nonbankruptcy events permit a declaration by the specified trustee or holder route. Certain
bankruptcy events make the obligations due without that declaration. A negative equity balance
establishes neither the specified bankruptcy event nor the required category. Moreover, an event
for one series does not necessarily constitute an event for another. Neither automatic contagion
nor universal independence follows (Apple Inc., 2024a, Exhibit 4.1, printed pp. 9–10).
Cure and rescission must also remain separate. The described rescission of a declaration requires
holder action and specified deposits and cure or waiver conditions, with an exception concerning


PAGE 58
CHAPTER 5.
DEBT CONTRACTS AND FINANCING
36
amounts due solely because of acceleration. Payment can remove current arrears without
by itself proving that an earlier acceleration has been rescinded. In the selected synthetic
continuation, payment changes the economic claim, reassessment changes the current clause
finding, and a separate supported rescission assessment changes the recorded acceleration state.
The historical miss remains 40 throughout.
Consent cannot be supplied by a numerical convenience either. The summary requires each
affected holder’s consent for specified protected changes, including changes to stated maturity
and reductions of principal or interest. Its general majority-waiver provision excludes payment
defaults and certain individually protected provisions. A waiver concerns the specified past
default; it does not license a future one (Apple Inc., 2024a, Exhibit 4.1, printed pp. 11–12).
A current contractual default may cease under a valid waiver while the economic record still
shows that payment arrived late. Keeping those two statements is consistent.
The surrounding qualifications matter economically. Individual payment enforcement differs
from collective proceedings; legal discharge differs from covenant defeasance. The description
warns that assets sufficient to fund scheduled maturity may be insufficient after acceleration
(Apple Inc., 2024a, Exhibit 4.1, printed pp. 10–13). A simulator must therefore retain the
relevant rights, legal owner, timing and usable funding, even when a consolidated closing
statement appears fully funded.
The typed conditional implementation translates the retained examples and records selected
findings through the common monetary and memory engine. An unresolved finding produces
no successor state. Recording a known finding changes no cash, carrying amount, shares
or historical missed amount. This establishes a limited representation and execution result.
Multiple concurrent clauses, the full instruments, actual notices and amendments, and the
economic consequences of acceleration still require their own admitted methods.
5.17
Implementation proposal
The debt module should maintain immutable contract versions, a dated obligation schedule,
current economic state and separate accounting measurements. Events include issue, draw, reset,
accrue, coupon, amortize, redeem, amend, breach, cure, accelerate, restructure and recover.
Each event has preconditions, amounts, dates and successor state.
Existing repository methods already cover bounded issue, accrual, coupon, redemption and
conversion calculations, and newer declared methods preserve timing and debt memory. They
are useful foundations. They do not by themselves establish a general pricing model, market
capacity, legal default process or complete instrument admission. The extension should compose
with their checked boundaries rather than describe those capabilities as absent.
The smallest discriminating financing fixture should include proceeds different from face, positive
recovery, a fee paid at issuance, a maturity shortfall and an unavailable refinancing offer. A
second fixture should add two creditors with priority and delayed recovery. A third should test
unknown rate terms and a covenant boundary. Passing these checks establishes the specified
mechanics; empirical pricing validity and legal applicability require separate evidence.


PAGE 59
Chapter 6
Equity funding, ownership, and
dilution
An equity issue can provide cash while changing ownership and future claims. A model that
records only an increase in cash and book equity captures one accounting consequence but misses
the allocation of control and value. A structural model that charges an issuance cost captures a
financing friction but does not automatically create a share register. Scenario simulation needs
quantities, prices, rights, holders and dates, together with an explanation of who is willing to
subscribe.
6.1
Book equity, market value and share capital
Book equity is the residual of recognized assets and liabilities under an accounting basis. Market
equity value is the value assigned to owners’ economic claims. Legal share capital is a legally
defined component of capital that may be related to nominal share values. These quantities
have different meanings. Dividing book equity by shares produces book value per share, not
automatically the market subscription price.
A company can have positive book equity and insufficient cash to pay a maturity. It can have
negative book equity and valuable future opportunities. Limited liability can bound the value of
a shareholder’s claim below by zero in a model without forcing the accounting residual to zero.
The simulator should retain these quantities separately and define any relation between them.
Let N be the number of existing ordinary shares, VE the total economic value assigned to their
claim, and p = VE/N a per-share value when shares are homogeneous and N > 0. This division
presumes that VE refers to exactly that class. If preferred shares, options or noncontrolling
interests are included in total equity value, allocating all of it to ordinary shares is wrong.
6.2
A simple cash issue
Suppose the company issues n new homogeneous ordinary shares at subscription price ps > 0.
Gross proceeds are G = nps. If cash issuance costs are f, net cash is G−f. With no simultaneous
repurchase or conversion, the post-issue count is
N+ = N + n = N + G
ps
.
(6.1)
37


PAGE 60
CHAPTER 6.
EQUITY AND OWNERSHIP
38
An existing holder with ni shares has ownership fraction ni/N before and ni/(N + n) after
the issue, assuming that holder does not subscribe. This is percentage dilution. Its economic
consequence depends on the price received and the use of the proceeds.
For example, let N = 100, pre-issue equity value be 1,000, and issue 20 shares at 10. If the
cash adds 200 of value and there are no costs or other effects, post-issue value is 1,200 and each
share remains worth 10. The old holders’ percentage falls from 100 per cent to 100/120, but
their combined value remains 1,000. Percentage dilution is not necessarily a wealth loss.
If the same 20 shares are issued at 8 while pre-issue assets remain worth 1,000, post-issue value
is 1,160 and each share is worth 1, 160/120. Old holders then own 100(1, 160/120) = 966.666 . . ..
The transfer arises from the discounted subscription price under these assumptions. A model
that equates all share-count dilution with value destruction misses the first case; one that
ignores issue price misses the second.
6.3
Raising a target amount after a price shock
Suppose net funding need is K and a fixed cash fee is f. At a supplied price ps, required shares
are (K + f)/ps. A price decline therefore increases the share count required to raise the same
cash. If authorized issuance or investor demand limits n, the funding target may be infeasible.
Take K = 90, f = 10 and an initial subscription price 10. The company needs ten shares.
After a price shock to 5 it needs twenty.
If only fifteen shares may be issued under the
assumed authorization, maximum net proceeds become 15(5) −10 = 65, leaving a shortfall of
25. Increasing book equity by 90 would conceal both the share constraint and the cash shortfall.
The fee may instead be proportional, for example f = λG. Net proceeds are G(1 −λ) and
required gross proceeds are K/(1 −λ) for 0 ≤λ < 1. A fixed plus proportional fee gives
G = (K + f0)/(1 −λ). These equations specify different cost structures. The choice should
come from a declared offer or empirical assumption, not a silent generic parameter.
6.4
Pricing is not supplied by an accounting identity
The equation G = nps is an identity once shares and price are specified. It does not determine
investor willingness to pay. A subscription price can be an external offer, an assumed discount
to an observed market price, or the result of a valuation and demand model. Each supports a
different claim.
A public market quote can be stale, illiquid or refer to a different class. A large issue can affect
price. A distressed company may face a discontinuous demand schedule or no feasible offer. A
model that uses yesterday’s price for unlimited new shares assumes unlimited demand at that
price. That may be useful in a deliberately simplified scenario, but should not be presented as
a financing solution established by the statements.
The same caution applies to an endogenous equity value function.
It may value existing
shareholders’ future payouts under a particular policy and financing friction. Converting that
value into a new issue price requires assumptions about how new investors share those payouts
and how issuance changes the state. A scalar cost function η(e) for external equity is not a
complete security issuance model.


PAGE 61
CHAPTER 6.
EQUITY AND OWNERSHIP
39
6.5
The rights attached to a share
Shares can differ in voting rights, dividends, liquidation preference, conversion rights and
redemption terms.
An aggregate share count is sufficient only when the distinctions are
irrelevant to the question or explicitly normalized. A dollar of preferred funding is not generally
equivalent to a dollar of ordinary funding for future payouts.
A simple preference waterfall illustrates the point. Suppose new investors contribute 30 for a
nonparticipating preference claim entitled to 30 before ordinary holders, with the option to
convert into 25 per cent of ordinary equity. If distributable value is 80, the preference payment
30 exceeds conversion value 20. If value is 200, conversion value 50 exceeds the preference. The
exercise threshold is 120 under these assumptions.
This example requires a declared contract: no accrued preference dividend, no senior claims,
no participation after the preference and no other options. Adding any of these changes the
waterfall. A flat share-percentage allocation would misallocate value below the conversion
threshold. The model should retain class-specific rights and exercise policy.
6.6
Options, warrants and contingent issuance
An option can create future shares when exercised. Exercise can bring cash to the company,
transfer existing shares or settle in cash, depending on terms. Counting all potential shares as
already issued can distort current ownership; ignoring them can distort future dilution.
Let an option cover m shares at exercise price k. In a simplified physical exercise, the company
receives mk and issues m shares. The holder’s decision depends on share value, expiry, constraints
and possibly tax or employment conditions. A purely intrinsic-value rule is a modeling policy,
not a universal description of exercise behavior.
Contingent performance awards add dependence on future operating outcomes. A scenario
changing revenue can affect both the company’s value and the number of shares delivered.
Treating share count as independent of the operating shock loses that dependence. Historical
statement totals may not identify all award terms, so the family must preserve unresolved
conditions.
6.7
Conversion joins debt and equity
A convertible instrument links a liability schedule to ownership changes. At conversion, principal
or another contractual amount may be exchanged for shares according to a fixed ratio, a variable
price or a contingent formula. Cash settlement alternatives can change liquidity materially.
Suppose debt face 100 converts into ten ordinary shares. Physical conversion removes the
specified debt obligation and increases shares by ten under the assumed terms. It does not
also pay cash principal 100 unless the contract requires a separate payment. A simulator that
schedules both redemption and conversion double settles the same obligation.
Accounting derecognition and allocation can involve carrying amounts different from face and
separate components. Those postings need the applicable basis and facts. The economic
event should identify exactly which obligations and rights are extinguished or created before
the accounting module measures the consequences. Existing bounded conversion methods
in the repository can support this event discipline without claiming a universal convertible
classification.


PAGE 62
CHAPTER 6.
EQUITY AND OWNERSHIP
40
6.8
Repurchases and treasury shares
A repurchase uses cash and changes outstanding shares or treasury holdings according to the
legal and accounting treatment. A net increase in equity during a period can conceal both gross
issuance and gross repurchase. Their timing and prices matter for liquidity and ownership.
Suppose 20 shares are issued at 10 and ten are repurchased at 12. Net cash inflow before costs
is 80, while outstanding shares increase by ten if repurchased shares cease to be outstanding.
Reconstructing the period as a simple issue of ten shares for 80 uses an implied price of 8
and loses the actual transactions. A future policy inferred from that synthetic price can be
misleading.
Repurchase authorization is not settlement. A board may authorize up to an amount while
management executes only part. A scenario should not consume cash for the entire authorization
unless the policy calls for it and execution is feasible. Conversely, an already binding purchase
commitment may create obligations before the cash settlement date.
6.9
Dividends require both claims and capacity
A dividend policy can specify a target payout, but payment depends on declaration, entitlement
dates, cash, legal restrictions, covenants and class rights. Retained earnings are not a cash
account. A positive accumulated balance does not establish that funds are available in the
paying entity.
A declared dividend can create a payable before payment. The event sequence must avoid
expensing it as an operating cost in an illustrative ordinary distribution model, while still
recognizing the liability and equity movement under the selected basis. Payment reduces cash
and the payable. The exact legal and accounting treatment requires its admitted rules.
A cash-constrained policy may reduce or suspend a prospective dividend. It cannot necessarily
cancel an already enforceable obligation. Distinguishing intended, declared and paid distributions
is therefore important in stress scenarios. A single annual dividend line cannot capture all of
these states.
6.10
Ownership and control are related but distinct
Economic ownership percentages do not always determine control. Voting rights, contractual
arrangements, dispersed holdings and substantive options can matter. A scenario that changes
ownership may alter the consolidation perimeter, but the threshold and assessment are not
supplied by a generic percentage rule.
The security register should record holders and rights, while a separate control assessment uses
the applicable facts and accounting basis. When control changes, group reporting can change
even without a large contemporaneous cash flow. Conversely, a minority issue can raise cash
without changing control. The two calculations should communicate without being collapsed.
Historical disclosures may report total shares but not every beneficial holder. A model can
represent ownership categories and unknown allocations where sufficient for the query. It must
not invent holder identities or treat an unknown allocation as evenly spread. Concentration can
matter for approval, takeover and financing decisions.


PAGE 63
CHAPTER 6.
EQUITY AND OWNERSHIP
41
6.11
Earnings per share is not the financing price
Basic and diluted earnings-per-share calculations use accounting earnings, weighted share counts
and rules for potential ordinary shares. They are reporting calculations. They do not determine
market value per share or subscription price. A price-to-earnings heuristic is a separate valuation
assumption.
Timing matters even in a simple weighted count. If 100 shares are outstanding for half a year
and 120 for the other half, the simple time-weighted average is 110. Using closing count 120
understates basic earnings per share relative to that assumed timing. Actual calculations require
the applicable conventions, participation rights and events.
Diluted measures may exclude antidilutive instruments under the reporting rules. Exclusion
from diluted earnings per share does not mean the contract cannot create future cash or
ownership consequences under another scenario. The scenario engine must retain the instrument
even when one reporting calculation excludes it.
6.12
An issuance changes future incentives
Financing can change not only cash but the distribution of gains and losses. Existing shareholders,
new investors and creditors may prefer different policies after an issue. A model holding behavior
fixed is a conditional mechanical simulation. A model claiming an optimal response must specify
whose objective is optimized and what constraints apply.
For example, a deeply subordinated new equity contribution can protect creditors while diluting
existing owners. Existing owners may refuse it even when total enterprise value rises. A policy
maximizing total firm value can therefore differ from one maximizing existing shareholders’
value. Treating these objectives as interchangeable can change default or investment decisions.
The structural literature surveyed by Strebulaev and Whited (2012) makes financing frictions
and shareholder objectives explicit in stylized models. Transfer to AccDSL requires matching the
actor, security rights and timing. A convenient objective function should remain a hypothesis
until its application is justified.
6.13
A complete small financing comparison
Consider an immediate cash need of 40, current cash 10 and no available debt facility. Net new
funding need is 30. Existing shares are 100 and an admissible offer supplies ordinary shares at
price 5 with a fixed cash cost of 5. Raising 35 gross requires seven shares, gives 30 net cash and
permits the payment. Closing cash is zero and shares are 107.
If the offer instead caps issuance at six shares, gross proceeds are 30 and net proceeds 25.
Available cash is 35, leaving a payment shortfall of 5. The difference is an economic feasibility
result. The journal cannot repair it by crediting equity for another 5 without a transaction.
If management can defer 10 of the payment at a cost, the capped issue may become feasible. That
is a different policy and contract path, with future obligations. If a shareholder loan supplies
the missing 5, it creates debt and related rights. The model should show these alternatives
explicitly rather than use a generic balancing source.


PAGE 64
CHAPTER 6.
EQUITY AND OWNERSHIP
42
6.14
Proposed register and execution methods
A dated security register should identify issuer, class, units, holder or holder category, rights, issue
price where relevant, subscription and settlement state, and links to conversion or cancellation
events. Outstanding, authorized, treasury and potentially issuable units should be distinct.
Changes should reconcile through events.
An equity-financing method should accept a funding request, eligible offers, authorization limits
and actor policy. It returns an accepted transaction or a shortfall. The transaction then
produces cash, ownership and accounting events. Price formation may be supplied or solved by
a separate model; the result records which.
The repository’s simple shares-times-price postings and bounded capital events are useful
components. The proposed extension adds offer feasibility, class rights and ownership evolution.
It should reuse existing atomic posting and timing checks while adding negative cases: zero or
negative subscription price, unsupported class, duplicate settlement, insufficient authorization,
failed subscription and inconsistent conversion.
6.15
What the equity checks establish
Conservation checks can establish that cash equals settled subscriptions less costs and that
share movements reconcile. Waterfall checks can establish that distributions do not exceed
available value under the assumed priority rules. Timing checks can establish that ownership
changes occur at the declared event date.
These checks do not establish that an issue price is fair, that investors will subscribe, that a
legal approval is valid or that management will choose the modeled action. Those claims need
their own evidence. The simulator should be able to give useful conditional calculations while
keeping these unresolved questions visible.
A useful adversarial fixture issues shares below an assumed pre-issue value, then compares
percentage dilution with wealth transfer. Another changes the price while holding funding need
fixed and tests an authorization cap. A third combines conversion and redemption to ensure
that the same obligation is settled once. These examples expose mistakes hidden by a simple
par issuance with unlimited demand.


PAGE 65
Chapter 7
From economic events to coherent
statements
A scenario produces a sequence of events. Financial statements summarize selected consequences
of those events under a reporting basis. Keeping this direction explicit makes it possible to
ask whether an output follows from a plausible company history. Starting with independently
forecast statement lines and inserting a balancing amount reverses the logic: it can make the
accounts add up while leaving the economic transaction unexplained.
7.1
An event carries more than an amount
An event identifies actors, instrument or resource, effective time, settlement time, currency,
quantity, conditions and provenance. A sale can transfer goods today, be recognized under a
specified rule today, and be collected next month. A loan draw can be approved today and
settle tomorrow. A derivative can change value without an immediate cash transfer.
The event layer should therefore represent economic occurrence separately from recognition,
measurement and payment. The accounting layer maps admitted events and estimates to
postings. The reporting layer aggregates postings into statements. The mapping may create
noncash movements, and some economically relevant commitments may be disclosed without a
current journal amount.
Pierson (2020, Sections 3.3–3.5) explicitly separates accrual, payment and reporting accumulation
in a system-dynamics setting. Its simple debt-delay mechanism is acknowledged as limited.
The lesson for AccDSL is the separation of clocks, not the adoption of that simplified debt
mechanism as a general contract engine.
7.2
A credit sale and collection
Consider a synthetic sale of goods for 30, with inventory cost 18, recognized at delivery and
collected later. Ignore tax, returns and credit impairment for this illustration. At delivery, the
postings are a debit to receivables and credit to revenue of 30, and a debit to cost of sales and
credit to inventory of 18. Cash has not increased.
At collection, cash increases by 30 and receivables decrease by 30. Revenue is not recognized
again. The two events explain why profit and operating cash can differ during the interval. A
scenario that delays collection changes receivables and liquidity even if recognized revenue is
43


PAGE 66
CHAPTER 7.
EVENTS AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
44
unchanged.
If the sale is instead prepaid, receipt creates a different sequence under the selected recognition
rule. The same annual revenue and cash collection can conceal different within-year funding
needs. An annual-only model can be adequate for some questions, but it cannot claim to resolve
a monthly liquidity trough that its time aggregation removes.
7.3
Balanced entries are necessary, not sufficient
Double-entry balance verifies a conservation relation in the accounting representation.
It
does not establish that the event occurred, that the account mapping is correct or that the
measurement basis is appropriate. Debiting cash and crediting revenue balances even when the
money was borrowed.
Let je be the vector of signed account movements for event e, under a declared debit-credit
convention. A balancing check can be written 1Tje = 0. This is one invariant. Additional
invariants depend on the event: a loan settlement should reconcile cash to proceeds and fees; a
physical inventory sale should not consume more units than available unless backorders or short
sales are explicitly allowed; a share issue should reconcile units and ownership.
The event’s economic and accounting checks should remain separate. A balanced but eco-
nomically impossible issue must fail transaction admission. An economically valid event with
an unresolved accounting treatment can remain in the economic state while its statement
consequences are conditional or unsupported. Forcing one layer to masquerade as the other
hides useful information.
7.4
Quantity and valuation
Inventory value can change because quantities change, unit costs change, measurement rules
change or currency changes. A model using only a monetary stock can confuse these mechanisms.
Production capacity and physical shortages depend on quantities, while reported carrying values
depend on measurement.
Suppose ten units cost 5 each. Inventory carrying value is 50 under the illustrative cost basis.
A replacement-price increase to 8 does not automatically increase that carrying value to 80. If
five units are sold and replaced, cash purchases, cost allocation and ending stock depend on
the chosen inventory-flow assumptions. A future demand shock can expose differences among
vintages with the same initial total value.
The economic state should retain the granularity needed for production and sale decisions:
units, location, quality, expiry and ownership where relevant. Accounting state can aggregate
or measure those units under its basis. An aggregate sufficient for valuation may be insufficient
for a supply interruption.
7.5
Asset vintages and depreciation
Two firms can report the same property carrying amount and depreciation while holding assets
of different ages. The remaining useful lives, maintenance needs and replacement dates can
differ. A capital-expenditure scenario therefore requires more than a depreciation ratio.
For a simple straight-line asset with cost K, residual value v and life L, annual depreciation is
(K −v)/L under the assumed full-year convention. Several combinations of K, v and L can


PAGE 67
CHAPTER 7.
EVENTS AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
45
yield the same charge. The historical charge does not identify replacement timing.
An asset-vintage representation retains acquisition date, cost, accumulated depreciation, remain-
ing life, capacity and relevant impairment or disposal events. If only aggregates are observed,
the reconstruction should preserve a family of vintage allocations. A query about next year’s
depreciation may admit a compact aggregate; a query about replacement cash over five years
may require finer structure.
7.6
Working capital is a set of contracts
Receivables and payables are often forecast using days sales outstanding and days payable
outstanding. These ratios summarize historical relationships but do not identify individual due
dates or counterparties. A ratio-based projection can be a useful approximation when its scope
is stated. It cannot automatically describe payment priority or concentration under stress.
A customer delay can raise receivables while the firm still owes suppliers on fixed dates. If the
model shifts payables proportionally with sales without checking contracts, it may manufacture
supplier financing. Conversely, a model that fixes all payment dates may omit renegotiation
that management could pursue. Both are policy choices.
The proposed design permits ratio methods as explicitly reduced models alongside contract
schedules. Their outputs should carry the aggregation assumptions, horizon and validation
domain. A breach-sensitive liquidity query can require refinement to invoices or cohorts whose
due dates cross the critical boundary.
7.7
Currency conversion and cash availability
A foreign-currency account has both native units and a reporting-currency value. Exchange
movements can change reported amounts without changing native cash. A debt payment
in foreign currency requires the appropriate currency at settlement, not merely sufficient
consolidated reporting-currency cash.
Suppose the company holds 100 domestic units and owes 10 foreign units. At exchange rate 8
domestic per foreign, repayment costs 80; at 12 it costs 120. A consolidated cash total of 100
does not establish payment capacity after the depreciation. A hedge, foreign cash balance or
exchange facility may alter the result, but each must be represented.
Translation for group reporting differs from converting cash for settlement. The accounting basis
determines exchange differences in profit, other comprehensive income or other components.
The economic layer tracks actual currencies and conversions. A rate applied for presentation
cannot automatically be used as an executable market price for a large transaction.
7.8
Group boundaries and internal funding
A consolidated group can report cash in one entity and debt in another. Consolidation removes
some internal balances but does not necessarily make all cash freely transferable. Legal, tax,
contractual, regulatory or operational constraints may restrict movement. A group-level cash
plug can conceal an entity-level default.
Represent cash accounts and obligations by legal entity, then model permitted transfers as events
with dates, costs and approvals. Intercompany loans create claims in both entities. Dividends,


PAGE 68
CHAPTER 7.
EVENTS AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
46
capital contributions and service payments have different legal and accounting consequences.
They cannot be substituted solely because each moves cash.
For consolidated reporting, internal claims may be eliminated under the applicable perimeter
and basis. The underlying entity records remain necessary for liquidity and counterparty
analysis. Eliminating the records themselves would prevent the simulator from identifying which
entity can pay which obligation.
7.9
A two-entity liquidity example
Parent P has cash 5 and external debt due 20. Subsidiary S has cash 30 and no current external
debt. Consolidated cash is 35, apparently enough for the maturity. Suppose only 10 can legally
and operationally transfer from S before the due date under the declared facts. P can then
assemble 15 and has a shortfall of 5.
If a permitted intercompany loan of 15 becomes available in time, P can pay. If a dividend of
15 is declared but settles after maturity, it cannot fund that payment without a bridge. If the
subsidiary cash is restricted, even the first 10 may be unavailable. The scenario result depends
on transfer rights and timing, not just the consolidated total.
A correct consolidated statement after payment can coexist with an incorrect event sequence
if the model silently borrowed the cash before it was available. Tests must therefore examine
entity cash at event boundaries, not only the final group balance sheet.
7.10
Taxes and losses
Tax expense, cash tax and deferred tax are different quantities. A profit forecast cannot generally
multiply pretax income by one rate and use the result simultaneously for expense and immediate
payment. Loss carryforwards, payment lags, jurisdiction, interest limitations and temporary
differences can separate them.
A simple teaching model may assume immediate tax payment at rate τ on positive taxable
income, giving cash tax τ max(T, 0). That is a declared simplification. If losses can offset future
income, the state must retain the remaining loss balance and expiry or utilization conditions.
Forgetting that state can overstate future tax; treating all losses as immediately refundable can
overstate current cash.
Accounting recognition of deferred tax assets requires its own admitted rules and evidence.
Their carrying amount is not spendable cash. A stress scenario that turns a deferred tax asset
into a funding source without an actual refund or financing transaction is economically wrong.
7.11
Estimates, errors and changes of policy
An estimate revision changes the model’s measurement of an uncertain quantity. An error
correction revises a prior assertion or application. An accounting-policy change can alter
recognition or measurement according to the applicable rules. Their timing and comparative
effects differ.
The scenario engine should distinguish an economic shock that changes expected credit losses
from a correction showing that the old receivables data were wrong. Both can change reported
equity, but only one necessarily describes new economic information at the forecast date. The
evidence and accounting version relations should make the distinction explicit.


PAGE 69
CHAPTER 7.
EVENTS AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
47
A correction should not silently rewrite every frozen forecast. A current restated view and an
as-published historical view can coexist. Their differences are analytically useful: they separate
errors in data available at the time from errors in the forecasting model.
7.12
Consolidation and scenario-dependent perimeter
An acquisition, disposal or change of control can alter the reporting perimeter. The group total
before and after the event may not be comparable without a bridge. A simulation that treats
acquired revenue as organic growth can distort future behavior and validation.
Perimeter state should identify included entities and the relevant control assessment over time.
Acquisition events introduce assets, liabilities and consideration under the selected accounting
treatment. Disposal events remove or reclassify them and may create gains, losses or continuing
interests. These are not ordinary sales receipts applied to an unchanged company.
Unknown deal terms can produce alternative histories. Earn-outs, guarantees and retained
interests may affect future cash after apparent disposal. A compact representation that keeps
only closing consolidated balances can lose these rights. The source-to-contract-to-event chain
should survive even when the initial reporting effect is aggregated.
7.13
Atomic posting and partial failure
A multi-leg event should either apply all admitted accounting movements consistently or remain
unapplied with a recorded failure. Posting cash but failing to create debt because a later
validation step failed corrupts the state. Similar risks arise when conversion removes debt but
does not issue shares.
Atomicity is an engineering property that supports accounting consistency. It does not certify
the event’s economic validity. Preconditions should be checked before mutation where possible,
and the event should retain an immutable input snapshot. Replay should reproduce the same
movements under the same rule version.
A failed accounting mapping should not erase a successfully observed economic event from the
evidence store. Instead, the system can retain the event and mark its accounting consequences
unresolved. This separation permits later repair without inventing or deleting transactions.
7.14
A finding can become stale within one day
Consider the late-funded borrower in Section 5.15. An observation records an unpaid principal
clause as satisfied on 2 July. The company then pays the remaining 40 on that same date.
A later command asks whether a continuing event permits a new acceleration. Matching the
observation’s date to the command’s date would accept the old unpaid finding, even though
the intervening payment changed its premise. Calendar equality does not establish that the
financial state is unchanged.
This is a concrete failure in the earlier selected adapter. Its original trace, with payment on the
following day, passed. Moving payment to the same day exposed the defect: the stale satisfied
finding remained usable. The successor retains the old observation as history but requires
reassessment before using it as a current condition. A fresh paid assessment then supports the
corresponding absence of a new trigger. It does not retroactively revoke an acceleration already
recorded under earlier valid facts.


PAGE 70
CHAPTER 7.
EVENTS AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
48
To express the dependency, let B be the complete typed book and let P(B) retain its economic
contents: balances, cash-flow history, nonremedy memory, context, account and type definitions,
reporting period and schema. The projection omits the remedy-status record, the list of seen
event identifiers and the current observation date. The date is checked separately. The first
omission is essential: recording a finding changes remedy status and must not, by that act alone,
invalidate the finding it records. Event identifiers alone do not establish an economic change.
Other dependencies are retained conservatively, even when a particular clause might use fewer
of them.
An observation stores its clause kind ko, date do, evidence revision vo, and the projected
economic state P(Bo), represented by a digest in the implementation. A proposed use at book
B and date d, under revision v, requires
d = do,
P(B) = P(Bo),
v = vo,
(7.1)
together with a compatible clause kind and the separately supplied continuing event finding.
The same-day payment violates the middle equality. A status recording with no economic effect
does not. A later evidence correction can violate the last equality while leaving every balance
unchanged.
The evidence revision identifies a supplied immutable view; it is not a certificate that the
view contains all relevant facts. A caller that omits a new notice or supplies a false old view
cannot be corrected by comparing digests. Source acquisition, availability and revision selection
remain separate requirements. Similarly, checking a conservative whole-economic projection can
demand reassessment after an unrelated change. Minimal clause-specific dependency analysis
would be a further optimization needing its own correctness argument.
Clause kind prevents a different misuse. A satisfied principal-payment clause cannot supply the
missing premise for automatic bankruptcy acceleration. The selected implementation rejects
that mismatch; a separately supplied qualifying bankruptcy observation can support its own
automatic route. Its truth remains conditional on the applicable terms and supplied event
finding. The system preserves unresolved applicability rather than turning an unsupported
factual inference into a remedy.
This repair illustrates a broader simulation requirement. Assertions about current state need
the dependencies under which they were observed, not only an observation timestamp. Reusing
a cached covenant finding after a cash transfer, or a borrowing-capacity calculation after a
collateral release, has the same risk. Keeping the previous assertion permits historical replay;
withholding its current use prevents a past result from deciding a different present problem.
Neither operation requires enumerating a separate tree node for every stored copy. Shared data
can retain the dependence explicitly.
The checked continuation preserves complete states and the prior missed amount through
payment, reassessment and rescission. Unknown or stale findings return without an accepted
successor. The reference covers one selected synthetic series and supplied evidence views.
Automated source revision, several simultaneous clauses and general rights-changing financial
effects remain additional work, rather than consequences of this freshness check.
7.15
Closing a year without erasing a missed payment
A reporting boundary creates another opportunity to confuse present balances with financial
history. Return to the late-funded borrower. In the paid case, the final settlement leaves no
principal outstanding. In the unpaid case, the company has received the new equity but retains
cash 40, outstanding principal 40 and arrears 40 at year-end. Both cases record new shares of


PAGE 71
CHAPTER 7.
EVENTS AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
49
40 and a historical missed payment of 40. The second case must be able to enter the next year
with the unpaid obligation intact; requiring every claim to be settled before carrying the book
would exclude the scenario being studied.
Closing transfers interest expense of 20 into retained earnings under the selected report mapping.
Current-period flows are archived before the new period begins. Carry then initializes the next
reporting period without turning that accounting boundary into a payment, waiver or new issue.
The selected policy preserves outstanding principal, arrears, share quantities and the old missed
amount. It also preserves the prior clause category, observation date, observed economic-state
identifier, supplied evidence revision and acceleration record.
This policy is explicit because a generic typed carry operation may permit different treatment
of different memory fields. A type-correct reset of the missed amount to zero would destroy
financially relevant history while leaving the opening balances coherent. The selected financing
wrapper therefore requires preservation for these fields before admitting the next opening. Other
memory, including period-specific counters, can require a different policy; preservation of this
selected contract record is not a universal fiscal-closing rule.
Preserving the observation does not make it current. The new period and its flow state change
the economic dependency in equation (7.1), and the old observation date remains old. A new
proposed acceleration consequently requires a new assessment. Even after that assessment, a
changed supplied evidence revision requires reassessment again. Carry must not refresh these
fields merely to make a later command pass.
The unpaid example continues into 2028 with an explicit fresh observation and a payment of 40.
Payment reduces cash and the outstanding claim together; the historical miss remains 40. The
separately recorded acceleration also survives unless an applicable rescission or other supported
rights-changing event changes it. A principal-payment observation still cannot authorize the
automatic bankruptcy route. Those are different contractual premises even when a reporting
boundary lies between their observations.
The exact continuation compares the complete closing entries, archived flows, next opening,
subsequent payment and next-period statements on two account charts. The unchanged carry
wrapper has separate compatibility evidence for the stronger remedy profile that includes
supplied evidence revision and clause category. This is explicit compatibility for the selected
records, not an implicit migration between earlier and later schemas. The examples demonstrate
why financial histories and observation dependencies must travel together across reporting
periods. Actual terms, several simultaneous clauses, guarantees and the economic consequences
of acceleration still require their own sources and methods.
7.16
Conversion changes a claim and the people who control it
Preserving rights across a reporting boundary is necessary until an event changes them. A
conversion provides a contrasting case: cash may stay fixed while debt disappears, new shares
are issued and another holder gains control. A simulator that checks cash and balanced journals
alone can miss the decision consequence.
Consider a separately supplied fiscal opening on 1 January 2028. Project assets are 100 and
cash is zero. Senior debt has face and carrying amount 40. A convertible claim has face 80 and
a supplied carrying amount C. There are 40 old shares and no shares held by the convertible
creditor. Opening capital is 60 −C, so the signed balances reconcile. The examples use C = 80
and C = 17000/441 as two distinct accounting premises. The latter is a diagnostic amount;
selecting it does not establish a measurement rule.


PAGE 72
CHAPTER 7.
EVENTS AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
50
The supplied contract says that the conversion signal extinguishes the claim and issues 120
shares to its holder on that date. Under the selected carrying-value transfer, the journal debits
the convertible liability by C and credits issued equity by C. The face becomes zero, new shares
become 120 and the observed signal and conversion are recorded. There is no cash flow or profit
from this assumed transfer. The financial statements then show assets 100, liabilities 40 and
equity 60 in either carrying case. Under the other signal, the debt remains outstanding and no
shares issue.
Cash-flow and voting fractions follow from the resulting shares under the assumed equal rights
per share. After conversion the new holder owns 120/(40 + 120) = 3/4 and the old holder owns
1/4. Under this supplied economic governance rule, the new holder therefore has strict majority
control. Before conversion the old holder controls all shares. The separate accounting-control
assessment, including potential rights before legal conversion, is explained in Section 26.16.
These rights follow from the completed transition, rather than from a controller label retained
from the opening. Different voting classes would require a different supplied rights rule.
The complete comparisons retain every account, the claim and share record, unrelated memory,
event history, journal and report on two account charts. They also compare the resulting rights
with a separately authored payoff table. Omitting claim extinction or share issue fails even when
a proposed journal balances. Repeating conversion with a fresh event identifier fails because the
event has already occurred. Wrong dates and signals fail before effects are accepted. Reporting
the equity increase without explaining its noncash movement also fails, while preserving the
already accepted conversion.
This example starts from an explicit opening and assumes the accounting transfer. It does
not derive that opening from issuance, select a reporting standard, or establish that a real
contract has these terms. Its contribution is narrower and useful: the accounting balances and
economic rights change together, and the rights can supply the next decision without counting
both converted debt and equity as surviving claims.
7.17
Reporting frequency and event frequency
A company may report quarterly while obligations settle daily. A quarterly engine that nets
all flows can miss a temporary liquidity failure. A daily engine can still report quarterly by
aggregation. The appropriate event resolution depends on the question and the contracts.
Adaptive resolution is possible.
A long period with no state-dependent decisions can be
aggregated analytically. A period containing maturity, reset, dividend and covenant dates may
require explicit boundaries. The aggregation must preserve ordering and any intermediate
constraint that matters.
For example, cash 10, a receipt of 20 and payment of 25 produce closing cash 5 regardless of
order. If payment comes first and no overdraft is permitted, the path is infeasible despite the
positive closing balance. A minimum-cash query requires the event sequence. A closing-balance
query alone would not detect the problem.
7.18
Stock-flow consistency across counterparties
When several entities are modeled, a payment by one is a receipt by another, subject to fees,
taxes and timing. Keeping counterpart links helps detect money creation or disappearance. In
an external-counterparty boundary, the system can record the flow leaving the modeled domain
rather than pretending the counterparty does not exist.


PAGE 73
CHAPTER 7.
EVENTS AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS
51
The stock-flow consistent approach in Caiani et al. (2016) gives a useful example of explicit
sectors and transaction sequence. Its named cash-flow measures must be interpreted in its own
definitions: a quantity called operating cash flow in that model subtracts principal repayments
and should not automatically be mapped to an accounting statement category. Reusing labels
without inspecting definitions is a semantic error.
AccDSL should therefore give each reported or derived quantity a definition and units. A label
match is a candidate mapping, not proof of equivalence. This is especially important when
connecting economic simulators, accounting standards and regulatory templates.
7.19
A coherent event-to-report method
The proposed method first admits an economic event under its contractual and behavioral
premises. It updates quantities, rights and obligations. It then invokes the accounting rule for
the declared basis and context, validates the posting bundle, and updates report accumulators.
Source and scenario provenance accompany each stage.
At reporting boundaries, the system reconciles opening balances, movements and closing balances,
checks units and perimeter, and preserves unresolved amounts or conditional interpretations. It
does not force an unsupported movement into a generic other line merely to close the statement.
A residual can be displayed as a diagnostic requiring explanation.
This sequence supports both concrete witnesses and symbolic families. If every member of a
region has the same event rule, amounts can remain symbolic. If a guard changes the rule, the
region must split or retain a piecewise expression. Accounting coherence then applies within
every admissible branch, not just to an average statement that no individual history produces.


PAGE 74
Chapter 8
Keeping uncertainty without
enumerating every world
Twenty binary unknowns have more than a million assignments. A company can have thousands
of contracts, continuous unknown amounts and decades of possible events. Explicit enumeration
is therefore an unsuitable default. The alternative is to represent the family and compute the
answer demanded by a question. This can save enormous work, but cannot guarantee that
every problem has a small exact solution.
8.1
Representation size and answer size
A formula containing twenty Boolean variables can describe all 220 assignments without listing
them. A solver may answer whether any assignment violates a cash constraint without printing
every world. If the user asks for all distinct journals, the output itself can be enormous. No
representation can print an exponentially large answer in fixed small space.
Continuous uncertainty sharpens the distinction. The family 0 ≤α ≤1 has infinitely many
members, but interest 5 + 2α is a finite exact answer. Useful computations include symbolic
propagation, optimization over a region and classification of regions by behavior. Enumeration
is one method, not the definition of correctness.
Darwiche and Marquis (2002) compares knowledge-compilation languages by succinctness and
supported queries and transformations. Its definitions of directed acyclic representations, decom-
posability and determinism explain why sharing and structural restrictions help. Its tradeoffs
also warn against assuming that a compact formula makes every query cheap. Compilation can
itself be expensive.
8.2
Three different graphs
An evidence graph links assertions to sources and interpretations. A constraint graph links
variables through relations. An execution graph links states through dated events. Their edges
have different meanings. Source support is not causation; a constraint edge is not a probabilistic
independence statement.
An expression DAG can share a repeated discount factor or subtotal. An immutable history
graph can share common event prefixes.
A transition graph can merge states only when
relevant future behavior is equivalent. Confusing these forms of sharing can turn an engineering
52


PAGE 75
CHAPTER 8.
COMPACT UNCERTAINTY
53
optimization into an unjustified economic assumption.
Economic feedback may remain cyclic while expression storage is acyclic. Debt price and
investment policy can be simultaneous unknowns. An acyclic residual evaluator does not
eliminate the fixed-point problem. The representation and solution method answer different
questions.
8.3
Share common structure without merging worlds
If a thousand histories differ only in one loan’s reset clause, their source reports, asset register
and most events can be shared. Each alternative stores its clause condition and local differences.
This reduces storage without identifying the alternatives economically.
A common event prefix can be executed once when every history has the same relevant state
and rule up to a boundary. Execution separates at the first differing reset or decision. Persistent
data structures retain ancestors and changed fields for explanation and replay.
Sharing must not allow mutation of one branch to alter another. Refinancing in one history
cannot rewrite the common original loan.
Immutable contract versions and branch-local
successor records prevent that failure. Cache identities must include all relevant inputs and rule
versions, not merely a displayed company name.
8.4
Elimination must preserve consequences
If a query uses variables x and the full family is C(x, z), exact elimination of z means
CQ(x) ⇐⇒∃zC(x, z).
(8.1)
Dropping every constraint containing z is generally not this projection. Constraints x = z + 1
and 0 ≤z ≤2 imply 1 ≤x ≤3. Removing both constraints leaves x unrestricted. Correct
elimination retains the implied interval.
Projection can create dense relationships among remaining variables. A small original repre-
sentation does not guarantee a small projected one. A dependency slice must include outputs,
event guards, feasible actions and observation rules over the horizon. A variable absent from
today’s report can affect tomorrow’s signal or covenant.
An asset identifier irrelevant to interest expense may matter to collateral eligibility. A customer
identifier irrelevant to total receivables may matter to concentration. Query-driven elimination
therefore follows economic dependencies rather than simply selecting columns present in the
requested output.
8.5
Split where behavior changes
If every member of a region follows the same affine transition, propagate it symbolically. When
a guard changes the event rule, partition the region or retain a piecewise expression. For cash
90 −2α and requirement 89.4, the split is α ≤0.3 versus α > 0.3. The equality belongs to
compliance under the inclusive rule.
If breach has no effect on the requested output, its consequences may be deferred while the
condition is retained.
If it changes funding eligibility, it matters immediately.
Repeated
guards can still create exponentially many distinct behaviors. Guard-based execution avoids
unnecessary expansion; it does not prove universal tractability.


PAGE 76
CHAPTER 8.
COMPACT UNCERTAINTY
54
A partition should carry its logical coverage: regions are disjoint where intended and their union
covers the admitted parent. A missing equality boundary can silently lose a possible company.
An overlapping boundary can double probability mass or duplicate an event. Exact rational
threshold fixtures are especially useful here.
8.6
Equal totals are insufficient
Two states with cash 50 and debt 100 can differ in tomorrow’s maturity. Two states with equal
tomorrow payments can differ in a disclosure that changes the next decision. Neither pair can
automatically be merged for the corresponding query.
Givan et al. (2003, Section 3.1) shows why equality under fixed action sequences is too weak
for general policy equivalence in an MDP: policies react to intermediate states. Section 3.3
develops stochastic bisimulation through equal rewards and transition probabilities to equivalent
blocks for each action. The accounting application below adds observation and requested-output
preservation; this extension is a local sufficiency argument.
8.7
A sufficient probabilistic condition
Consider a finite horizon. Let x be full state, a an action and s a scenario control. Let
Kt(dx′, do, dy | x, a, s) be the joint law of next state, observation and requested output. Let
φt(x) be the compressed state. States sharing a compressed state must have equal feasible
actions and current outputs. For every allowed a, s and measurable set B, also require
Kt({(x′, o, y) : (φt+1(x′), o, y) ∈B} | x1, a, s)
= Kt({(x′, o, y) : (φt+1(x′), o, y) ∈B} | x2, a, s).
(8.2)
Initial distribution and information must be mapped consistently. Policies use only the retained
information history and permitted memory. Under these conditions, the compressed model
preserves the requested joint output and observation law. Equal means, or equal marginal
laws separately, are weaker. Dependence between observation and successor state matters for
learning.
8.8
Finite-horizon proof
At the final date, equality of current outputs establishes the result. Suppose the compressed
model reproduces continuation laws from date t + 1. At date t, an admissible policy selects
the same action distribution for identical retained information histories. Equal feasible actions
make this choice meaningful in either state.
Equation (8.2) gives the same joint law of next compressed state, observation and immediate
output.
At each successor, the induction hypothesis supplies the same continuation law.
Integrating equal continuation laws against the equal successor law gives equality of the
remaining path law. Backward induction reaches the initial date.
This condition is sufficient, not necessary. The finite helper described next checks explicitly
supplied finite models; it is not a general checker for arbitrary company-state representations. If
managers privately observe a hidden variable removed by φ, their policy may not be expressible
in the compressed model. The information condition must be strengthened or the state refined.
Incorrect kernels cannot be repaired by an otherwise correct aggregation proof.


PAGE 77
CHAPTER 8.
COMPACT UNCERTAINTY
55
8.9
Checking a supplied finite aggregation
A lending decision makes the joint-law requirement concrete. Suppose a company starts with
cash 10 and observes a green or red signal before deciding whether to lend. A good outcome
adds 2 to cash if it lends; a bad outcome subtracts 4. Waiting leaves cash unchanged. In this
hypothetical model, good and bad states each have probability one half, and the signal identifies
the state correctly with probability 9/10. The policy lends after green and waits after red. The
resulting cash movements +2, −4 and 0 have probabilities 9/20, 1/20 and 1/2. For example,
the profitable lending path needs both a good state and its green signal, so its probability is
(1/2)(9/10) = 9/20.
Two copies of the same initial state can be merged without changing those paths. But suppose
a second state reverses the signal’s association with the good outcome. Green and red still each
occur half the time, as do good and bad outcomes. The separate distributions therefore agree,
while the policy lends in different circumstances. Comparing the joint successor state and signal
detects the difference. A separate checked example keeps the successor state fixed and changes
dependence between observation and immediate cash movement; separate marginal checks miss
that change too.
The finite conformance helper checks this distinction for an explicitly supplied model and
proposed partition. At every date it compares permitted actions and current requested outputs
within each block. For every action and scenario it groups successor records by the complete
triple of successor block, observation and immediate output. In the probability case it sums
explicit rational masses and compares the exact totals; each supplied kernel must have total
mass one. In the unweighted case it compares sets of triples. Repeated representations of a
possibility never supply a probability.
Initial information needs the same care. The input specifies state and initial observation jointly.
Aggregation maps the state while retaining that observation and the current requested output.
Each later decision uses the retained history of observations, actions and outputs. A checked
first-decision example lends after one initial observation and waits after another, even though
their two states belong to the same block. Removing the observation would change the permitted
policy. A policy asking directly for the hidden state is outside this information interface.
The finite argument follows the preceding induction. At each successor block, the requested
current output is fixed by the block. Equality of the joint triples thus gives equal probabilities
for each extended observable history. Applying the same admissible policy and continuing
the induction preserves the whole path law. For an unweighted relation, compatible path
concatenation and union replace probability products and sums. Every quotient transition must
be possible from each member of its source block; otherwise an aggregated path might not lift
to a path in the original model.
An independently coded path enumerator checks twelve accepted finite cases, including every de-
terministic lend-or-wait choice after the two signals, duplicate successor states, initial-information
decisions and an unweighted model. Eight proposed merges fail with a specific unequal action,
output or joint-kernel component. Two deliberately incorrect representative merges also produce
different complete path laws. These checks support the supplied finite examples; they do not
establish that a company’s chosen state contains every economically relevant variable.
The helper refuses missing transitions, invalid probability mass, unrecognized information and
incomplete partitions. A comparison budget exhausted before all obligations are checked returns
an unknown result with the unfinished comparisons and no approved quotient. Saved results
bind the full model, partition, information, outputs and horizon; reuse rechecks the condition.
This is a sufficient condition over all declared states, including unreachable ones. Failure may
therefore require a finer partition without proving that every aggregation for a particular initial


PAGE 78
CHAPTER 8.
COMPACT UNCERTAINTY
56
policy is impossible. The next example shows why persistent contract type is one dependence
worth retaining.
8.10
A larger state can preserve the information structure
The distinction between a process and its information becomes especially clear in the work of
Beiglböck, Pflügl and Schrott. Their information process recursively augments today’s value
by the conditional law of tomorrow’s information process. Equal laws of suitable Markov lifts
characterize their notion of equivalence of filtered processes (Beiglböck et al., 2024, Defini-
tions 2.6–2.8, Proposition 3.6 and Theorem 3.9). A lift may be large or measure-valued. Its
existence does not imply that a small sufficient state can be recovered from financial statements.
Moreover, transferring strategies requires attention to independent randomization and the avail-
able stochastic basis (Beiglböck et al., 2024, Theorem 5.4 and Remark 5.7). An implementer
must decide whether an actor may randomize and which observations are available, rather than
deducing these permissions from equal outcome laws.
8.11
Unweighted families require relational memory
For an unweighted family, replace probabilities with a relation of feasible successors, observations
and outputs. Exact aggregation must preserve joint possibilities and persistent cross-time
constraints. One-step marginal sets alone are insufficient.
A fixed-or-floating loan with two-year interest paths (5, 5) and (7, 7) has one-step marginal set
{5, 7} at each date. Recombining those sets creates (5, 7) and (7, 5), which require unmodeled
contract changes. Retaining the latent type or equivalent memory prevents these paths.
An enlarged family can sometimes provide an outer bound for a worst-case query. It is not an
exact family of possible companies. A harmful witness from the enlarged set must be validated
against original constraints before being described as economically possible. Conservatism does
not excuse mislabeling the target.
8.12
Approximation needs a query-specific error
A cash error of 0.1 may be harmless for a chart and decisive at a covenant. For a smooth output,
state error can imply an output bound under regularity. For a default indicator, an arbitrarily
small change near a threshold can reverse the classification.
If computed cash lies in [19.95, 20.05] and payment required is 20, a midpoint cannot establish
feasibility for the whole region. The correct classification is unresolved unless a stronger method
determines the sign. Refinement should focus on this boundary rather than indiscriminately
increasing precision.
If f is Lipschitz with known constant L in a stated norm and ∥x−ˆx∥≤ϵ, then |f(x)−f(ˆx)| ≤Lϵ.
This follows from the definition. It requires a justified constant and domain. An unknown
or very large L can make the bound unhelpful. No such smoothness claim is made for a
discontinuous breach indicator.


PAGE 79
CHAPTER 8.
COMPACT UNCERTAINTY
57
8.13
Inner and outer approximations
Let H contain the true modeled family and let H be contained in it. Then
inf
h∈H
C(h) ≤inf
h∈H C(h) ≤inf
h∈H C(h).
(8.3)
The outer minimum is a lower bound on the true minimum; the inner minimum is an upper
bound. A low cash value from relaxation can be spurious. A high minimum over sampled
witnesses does not prove safety.
If the outer lower bound exceeds the payment requirement, the original family is safe for this
query under its assumptions. If an inner feasible witness falls below it, failure is possible.
Otherwise the status remains unresolved. This three-way answer is more informative than
presenting every approximate extremum as exact.
8.14
Counterexample-guided refinement
Begin with a coarse outer model. If it proves the desired bound, the query may finish. If
it returns a harmful candidate, validate that candidate against full constraints. A feasible
candidate is a genuine witness; an infeasible one identifies a lost dependence and motivates
refinement.
The marginal interest-cash rectangle admits (7, 90), but C + I = 95 rejects it. Adding that
relation removes the spurious combination without enumeration. Other refinements can restore
currency-maturity dependence, collateral ownership or persistent contract identity.
This is a proposed reasoning method, not a universal termination result. Refinement may
exhaust resources. The returned bounds and unresolved region should remain available, together
with the dependencies still preventing a definitive answer. A resource limit is not an economic
default or a proof that the region is empty.
8.15
Caching has an economic scope
A cached answer depends on evidence, assumptions, contracts, policy, observation model, horizon,
requested outputs and numerical method. A change in any relevant component invalidates it.
Company name and scenario label are insufficient cache keys.
An aggregation checked for one-year interest may fail for three-year refinancing. Adding a new
observed covenant can invalidate an information summary. The system should record the query
class covered by an abstraction and require revalidation before broader reuse.
Provenance remains separate even when computations are shared. Two sources supporting one
fact need not duplicate arithmetic, but their independent support matters if one is withdrawn.
Execution identity must not replace source identity.
8.16
Trees as selected explanations
A small tree is useful for showing information arrival, choices and counterexamples. It can be
generated from the symbolic family for a question. Nodes should identify whether they are
exact worlds, samples, regions or hypothetical observations.


PAGE 80
CHAPTER 8.
COMPACT UNCERTAINTY
58
A representative tree is not exhaustive unless the finite model and coverage establish that claim.
Branch probabilities require a measure. Pruning a display branch must not silently delete it
from the calculation or renormalize remaining probabilities. A region can instead remain visible
as an unresolved aggregate.
A hybrid interface can show important branches, formulas and bounds together. Users expand
regions affecting their decisions. This preserves explanation while avoiding compulsory exhaustive
materialization. The underlying family remains authoritative when a new question requires a
different partition.
8.17
Budgeted computation
A query should state resources and acceptable result types. Exact values, certified bounds,
feasible witnesses and statistical estimates are distinct deliverables. If exact work exceeds
budget, return partial bounds and unresolved regions. Do not substitute an unsupported point
merely because the interface expects a number.
Priority can go to liquidity, covenant, default and decision boundaries. This is a query-specific
strategy and must not remove rare regions from a tail-risk calculation. The next chapter
develops sampling and other approximations while preserving the distinction between retained
possibilities and sampled evidence.
The layered proposal is symbolic constraints, shared immutable records, dependency-aware
elimination, guard-based splitting, justified aggregation and controlled approximation. Each
layer has a correctness condition. Choosing a graph instead of a tree is useful engineering, but
the mathematical and economic obligations remain.


PAGE 81
Chapter 9
Approximate inference and
simulation without false certainty
Exact symbolic calculation will not solve every useful company model within available resources.
Approximation is therefore necessary. Its legitimacy depends on naming the quantity being
approximated and preserving the distinction between numerical evidence and economic possibility.
A finite sample can estimate an expectation under a declared distribution. It cannot, by itself,
characterize every history compatible with disclosures. A fitted surrogate can interpolate a
function well while giving the wrong answer at a default boundary.
9.1
Choose the target first
Different questions require different methods. An expected cash balance, a worst-case cash
balance, a breach probability, a feasible breach witness and a policy value are different targets.
A method appropriate for one can be misleading for another. Sample averaging targets an
expectation; minimizing over samples supplies a witness-based bound on a minimum, not an
exact worst case.
Let Y = F(H, S, π) under a declared joint measure for history H and scenario S. Monte Carlo
draws Y1, . . . , Yn give the sample mean
bmn = 1
n
n
X
i=1
Yi.
(9.1)
Under independent identical sampling and finite variance, its variance is Var(Y )/n. Those
conditions are part of the claim. Correlated paths, importance weights or adaptive sampling
require a different variance treatment. The formula does not apply merely because the output
file has n rows.
Without a probability model, a finite collection of histories can still be useful for explanation
and testing. It should be called a set of witnesses or selected cases. Equal frequencies in that
collection do not establish probabilities in the underlying company family.
9.2
Sampling a persistent parameter
If a contract type is fixed but unknown, sample it once for a simulated world and retain it
through time. Draw future innovations according to their conditional process. This produces the
59


PAGE 82
CHAPTER 9.
APPROXIMATE SIMULATION
60
intended joint law of parameter and trajectory. Redrawing the type each period approximates a
different model.
The distinction remains important after filtering. Resampling particles may duplicate a pa-
rameter value as a computational operation; it does not mean the company changed contract.
Particle identity and world identity must not be confused. A lineage record can preserve which
parameter and past events each particle represents.
If the uncertainty is set-valued rather than probabilistic, selecting parameter values for numerical
exploration should retain the original feasible region. Sampled values can reveal failure cases
or guide refinement. They should not replace the family with a smaller set and thereby turn
unexplored histories into impossible ones.
9.3
Particle filtering under known model assumptions
A particle filter approximates sequential distributions in a specified state-space model. With
transition density f(xt | xt−1) and observation likelihood g(yt | xt), an importance proposal q
generates particles and weights. A generic incremental weight is proportional to
w(i)
