The proposal must cover the relevant target support. A proposal that never visits a feasible
high-risk region cannot recover it by weighting.
Doucet and Johansen (2008, Sections 2–3) explains sequential importance sampling, resampling
and degeneracy. Its filtering setup conditions on a specified model. Static unknown parameters
are not automatically handled well by adding them to the state and repeatedly resampling.
That extension can suffer severe impoverishment.
For AccDSL, particle filtering is appropriate only after prior, transition and observation laws
are admitted. It can be an optional numerical view of a history family. The family and source
constraints remain available to detect support loss and distinguish probabilistic approximation
from exact admissibility.
9.4
Weight degeneracy and genealogical collapse
If nearly all weight concentrates on one particle, a nominal population of ten thousand does not
provide ten thousand effective alternatives. A common weight diagnostic is
Neff =
1
P
i( ¯wi)2 ,
(9.3)
where normalized weights sum to one. It summarizes weight concentration, not every form
of approximation error. Uniform weights after resampling can coexist with many duplicate
ancestors.
Genealogical collapse matters for historical reconstruction and smoothing. Particles can share
the same early history even while their current states differ. If an old unknown contract type is
lost, later data cannot revive it without a rejuvenation method or retained symbolic support. A
high current effective sample size does not establish historical diversity.
Diagnostics should therefore include unique persistent parameter values or clusters, ancestor
diversity, support checks against constraints and sensitivity to proposal design. These are


PAGE 83
CHAPTER 9.
APPROXIMATE SIMULATION
61
explanatory or veto diagnostics under a stated protocol. No single scalar certifies that the
posterior or scenario tail is accurate.
9.5
Rejuvenation and conditional calculation
Move steps can restore diversity if they preserve the intended target distribution.
Their
acceptance probabilities, conditional variables and constraints must be correct. Arbitrarily
perturbing a loan rate or maturity can create a history inconsistent with evidence. Projection
back into a feasible set can also alter the target distribution unless its effect is accounted for.
Analytic integration of conditionally tractable components can reduce sampling variance. Doucet
and Johansen (2008, Section 4.6) discusses Rao–Blackwellization. In a company model, some
accounting accumulations may be deterministic conditional on contract type and shock path, so
they need not be sampled separately.
This benefit depends on a valid conditional decomposition. Integrating a variable out while
forgetting the dependence it induces on remaining quantities gives the wrong target. The
same projection discipline used for symbolic families applies probabilistically: marginalization
preserves dependence, whereas independent replacement usually does not.
9.6
Rare events and empty samples
If a breach has probability p and paths are independent, the probability of observing no breach
in n draws is (1 −p)n. For p = 0.001 and n = 100, this is about 0.905. Zero observed breaches
are therefore unsurprising and do not establish safety.
For an ideal Bernoulli experiment with zero events, a one-sided 95 per cent upper bound
solves (1 −p)n = 0.05, giving p = 1 −0.051/n. This is a statistical bound under the sampling
assumptions, not a statement about model misspecification. If the simulator omits a default
mechanism, more draws cannot detect it.
Tail estimates need enough events and a suitable uncertainty analysis. Extreme quantiles and
maxima have high sampling variability. A lower observed maximum in one run is descriptive
evidence, not a defensible ranking of methods. The target, sampling design and uncertainty
calculation should be specified before interpreting a comparison.
9.7
Importance sampling
Importance sampling deliberately visits rare regions more often. If target density is p(x) and
proposal is q(x), an expectation can be estimated using weights p(x)/q(x) where the proposal
covers target support. Oversampling distress is useful only if weights and support are handled
correctly.
For event indicator B(x), the ordinary importance estimator is n−1 P
i B(Xi)p(Xi)/q(Xi) with
Xi ∼q. Self-normalized weights define a different finite-sample estimator. The method should
record which is used and how uncertainty is assessed.
A proposal chosen after observing a preliminary sample can be effective, but adaptive procedures
require care in interpretation. Selecting only paths that breach and averaging them estimates
a conditional severity, not the unconditional breach probability. A report should separate
frequency from loss given breach.


PAGE 84
CHAPTER 9.
APPROXIMATE SIMULATION
62
9.8
Stress testing without probabilities
A deterministic stress can be useful even when its likelihood is unknown. It asks what happens
under the specified path. Reverse stress can search for conditions that produce a shortfall.
Neither exercise needs invented probabilities.
For example, solve for the smallest rate increase causing cash below a threshold under each
compatible debt history. The answer is a family of thresholds. A minimum across histories
identifies a vulnerable compatible case; a maximum identifies how much stress is needed to
affect every admitted history, subject to monotonicity and feasibility.
The search should include joint constraints on shocks. Allowing sales, exchange rates and funding
spreads to vary independently over arbitrary ranges can produce an economically incoherent
reverse stress. A broad stress set may be intentionally conservative, but its interpretation is
then a designed adversarial exercise rather than an estimated likelihood.
9.9
Scenario reduction
A large finite scenario set can be reduced by selecting representative paths or aggregating similar
ones. Similarity in Euclidean shock distance does not automatically imply similarity in financial
consequences. Two rate paths close in norm can lie on opposite sides of a covenant or option
boundary.
The proposed AccDSL method should define reduction error in terms of requested outputs,
feasible policies and information timing. If a reduction is intended to preserve an optimization
value, it needs evidence for that target. Preserving a few unconditional moments is insufficient
for a path-dependent refinancing decision.
Probability-metric reduction provides a precise method when a measure and suitable regularity
are available. Its usefulness depends on the financial quantity and decision being approximated.
The following derivations distinguish fixed-policy expectations, threshold probabilities and
policy optimization.
9.10
What probability-metric reduction preserves
A finite probability model permits a more precise reduction than deleting paths and renormalizing.
Let scenarios si have admitted probabilities pi, with P

of representative j is

(9.4)
All probability mass remains accounted for, although the distribution has changed. The method
approximates a specified measure; it supplies no probabilities for an unweighted family of
unknown company histories.
Dupačová et al. (2000, Section 3, Theorem 3.1 and its proof) derives optimal redistribution for
a fixed retained subset under the paper’s probability-metric assumptions: deleted mass moves
to a nearest retained scenario under the admitted cost. The choice of which scenarios to retain
remains a separate combinatorial problem. Algorithms 3.6 and 3.7 are backward-reduction
and forward-selection heuristics. Their sequential choices do not generally establish a globally
optimal subset.

63
A short local derivation shows what a distance can buy for a financial query. Suppose a
fixed-policy cash outcome f(s) satisfies |f(s) −f(u)| ≤Ld(s, u) for all admitted pairs, where d
is a stated scenario distance and L is a justified constant. Then

The first equality substitutes the redistributed masses; the inequality applies the triangle
inequality and the assumed regularity. Thus the weighted movement of scenarios bounds this
expected-output error. A distance small in interest rates is useful only when the required output
responds regularly in that distance.
For example, let the original states be 0, 1 and 2 with probabilities 1/4, 1/2, 1/4. Keep 0 and 2,
and resolve the middle state’s distance tie by assigning it to 0. Reduced masses are 3/4, 1/4.
Under absolute distance, weighted movement is 1/2. For f(s) = s, the original mean is 1 and
the reduced mean is 1/2, exactly attaining the bound with L = 1. Mass conservation alone
does not preserve the mean.
A default indicator is discontinuous. Put equal mass at 1 −η and 1 + η, where η > 0, and
retain only the first state. Weighted movement is η, tending to zero, but the probability of
s > 1 changes from one half to zero. Therefore a small distance does not by itself give a useful
default-probability guarantee. One needs additional control of probability near the boundary, a
distance tailored to the event, or explicit preservation of the relevant region.
9.11
What sequential transport theorems require
Nested and adapted transport distances address the temporal issue by restricting couplings so
that matching paths cannot depend on information that is unavailable at the relevant date.
Pflug and Pichler’s stability argument uses convex decision sets, convex loss in decisions and
uniform regularity in uncertainty (Pflug and Pichler, 2012, Definition 5.1, Theorem 6.1 and
proof). A projected action is formed by conditional averaging. If the only permitted issuance
sizes are 0 and 1, an average of 1/2 is infeasible. The theorem therefore does not automatically
justify merging discrete issuance choices.
Backhoff-Veraguas and coauthors obtain related stability results for financial optimization with
bounded predictable strategies and regular payoffs (Backhoff-Veraguas et al., 2020, Theorem 1.5;
Lemma 3.7; Theorem 3.10). Their weak transfer permits a different strategy in the other model.
Reusing the same strategy needs additional regularity. Their semimartingale distance also has
a different cost functional from a simple sum of discrete path deviations; the paper explains
why time refinement matters. Its examples of discontinuous strategies and exact superhedging
delimit the guarantee. These are useful reasons to formulate the company decision problem
precisely, rather than reasons to attach one distance to every accounting query.
9.12
Reduction before choosing a policy
An approximation adequate for one fixed policy may fail after optimization selects a different
one. Suppose, more strongly, that every policy in the same feasible, nonanticipative class
satisfies
|VP (π) −VQ(π)| ≤δ.
(9.6)


PAGE 86
CHAPTER 9.
APPROXIMATE SIMULATION
64
If maximizers πP and πQ exist, adding and subtracting their reduced-model values gives
VP (πP ) −VP (πQ) = [VP (πP ) −VQ(πP )]
+ [VQ(πP ) −VQ(πQ)] + [VQ(πQ) −VP (πQ)]
≤2δ.
(9.7)
The middle term is nonpositive because πQ optimizes the reduced problem. This elementary
bound needs uniform control over the policy class, identical feasibility and consistent information.
A bound established only at the final selected policy cannot justify the argument.
The source’s Section 2 gives stability results for convex stochastic programs under explicit
solution-set, integrability and regularity assumptions. Remark 2.2 adds dependence conditions
for its multistage setting. Those conditions do not automatically cover discrete default, share
conversion, nonconvex financing or private information in a company model. Nor does a
close distribution over complete paths establish that intermediate observations and admissible
decisions are preserved.
An AccDSL reduction should therefore state the admitted measure, retained scenarios, redis-
tribution rule, distance and query guarantee. It should separately check liquidity and default
boundaries and the actor’s observation structure. The original joint history family remains
available for refinement. Reducing a numerical probability approximation must not erase a
structurally possible contract or turn an unobserved future path into information available
today.
9.13
Surrogates and boundary errors
A surrogate can approximate expensive valuations or transitions. Its input domain, training
distribution, output definition and constraints must be explicit. A small average prediction
error can coexist with large errors in rare distressed states. If those states determine issuance
feasibility, the surrogate may be a poor component despite good average accuracy.
Suppose true funding capacity is c(x) and issuance is allowed only when c(x) ≥K.
An
approximation error bound |ˆc −c| ≤ϵ permits safe classification only outside [K −ϵ, K + ϵ].
Inside that band, refine or return uncertainty. A point prediction alone gives no such protection.
A surrogate should also respect units and economic constraints. Negative principal, duplicated
shares or cash creation may be detectable regardless of predictive error. Enforcing constraints
can improve mechanical validity, but does not prove that the underlying economic mapping is
correct. Both checks remain necessary.
9.14
A quantitative boundary argument
Default, covenant breach and a margin call are threshold events. Let X be exact cash at a
decision date, Y its approximation under a specified joint coupling, and c the contractual
threshold. All three are in the same currency and date basis. A cash approximation of −ϵ by
+ϵ has error 2ϵ, yet changes 1{X < 0} from one to zero. Small cash error alone does not imply
small default-probability error.
Proposition 9.1. If E|X −Y | < ∞, then for every δ > 0,
|P(X < c) −P(Y < c)| ≤P(|X −c| ≤δ) + E|X −Y |
δ

(9.8)

Proof. The absolute difference in event probabilities is at most the probability that their
indicators disagree. If |X −c| > δ and |X −Y | < δ, X and Y lie on the same side of c.
Consequently disagreement is contained in {|X −c| ≤δ} ∪{|X −Y | ≥δ}. The union bound
and Markov’s inequality give (9.8).
The first term is probability mass close to the covenant; the second is the probability allowed
for a sufficiently large approximation error. This local derivation is independent of any claim
that a particular algorithm supplies the required coupling error. Write e = E|X −Y |. If e = 0,
then X = Y almost surely and the threshold probabilities agree. For e > 0, if X has a density
bounded by M > 0 near c, the first term is at most 2Mδ. Choosing δ =

For several dates, a union bound can bound the probability that any breach indicator differs by
the sum of the per-date bounds, provided the relevant coupling and cash errors are supplied. If
a different breach decision changes subsequent actions and cash flows, those cash errors must
already account for that feedback; an open-loop cash comparison does not establish them. When
there are no weights over histories, the appropriate output is instead an uncertain boundary
region or certified feasible-set containment. A probability bound cannot be manufactured from
the proportion of enumerated witnesses.
9.15
A common-policy regret argument
Let J(π) and eJ(π) be expected costs in two models, with the same admissible policy class Π,
finite expected costs and finite optimal values. This comparison presupposes that a policy can
be implemented with the information and feasible actions in both models. Suppose a uniform
bound supπ∈Π |J(π) −eJ(π)| ≤ε holds. If bπ is η-optimal for the approximate problem, then
J(bπ) ≤eJ(bπ) + ε
≤inf
π∈Π
eJ(π) + η + ε
≤inf
π∈Π J(π) + η + 2ε.
(9.9)
The last step follows by applying the uniform bound to an arbitrarily near-optimal policy of
the exact problem, so attainment is unnecessary. The result is a regret bound for a common
feasible policy. If one only knows that the two infima are close, the first inequality is missing. If
a policy lift changes the policy class, a bound for that lift and the relevant comparison class is
needed. This is why reporting a small optimized residual or close portfolio value alone does not
establish that a compressed financing policy will work in the original company.
9.16
When sampled paths disclose their own futures
With continuous first-period observations, independent sampled paths typically have distinct
first values. If each stored first value has only its single sampled continuation, that value becomes
an identifier for the sampled future. An optimizer can exploit this unintended information.
More paths do not by themselves repair the information structure of the empirical measure.
Backhoff and coauthors give an explicit optimal-stopping counterexample (Backhoff et al., 2022,
Example 7.1). Their adapted empirical measure quantizes observations so that paths share
histories. Strong consistency applies to iid complete paths on a fixed finite horizon and bounded


PAGE 88
CHAPTER 9.
APPROXIMATE SIMULATION
66
state space; quantitative rates additionally require Lipschitz conditional kernels (Backhoff et al.,
2022, Definitions 1.1–1.2, Theorems 1.3, 1.5, 1.7).
A Markov modification improves the horizon exponent only when the process is in fact Markov
(Backhoff et al., 2022, Theorem 6.1); constants still depend on the horizon. A company’s current
balance sheet generally is not a sufficient Markov state when hidden debt terms or asset vintages
matter. Nor are reports from one issuer an iid sample of complete company paths. The method
suggests how a deliberately generated simulation sample can retain information. Its rates cannot
simply be assigned to an observed issuer panel.
9.17
A finite coupling method for discrete decisions
A stronger but directly checkable condition can avoid averaging actions. Consider a finite
horizon of T transitions. Keep a persistent contract type θ fixed and let a history record the
actor’s observations and past actions. In both the full model and a reduced model, require
the same feasible actions at paired histories. Couple their initial states so that the required
observations agree. At each paired history and common action, suppose a joint coupling of the
next state, observation and reported output leaves the states paired and the observation/output
records equal with conditional probability at least 1 −δt. This condition includes every history
and action reachable before a mismatch; fitting one historical policy is insufficient.
If Et is the event that no mismatch has occurred through transition t, then P(Et | Et−1) ≥1−δt.
Repeated conditioning gives

For finite kernels with common outcome labels, the smallest one-step mismatch probability
is their total-variation distance 1

z |K(z) −eK(z)|. Pairing full states with reduced states
requires a coupling whose successful pairs remain in the declared relation; marginal observation
agreement is insufficient. An implementable finite procedure first partitions states by persistent
type, available information, feasible actions and required outputs. It then compares joint
successor kernels into the current blocks for every action, splitting a block whenever these differ.
Repeat until no block changes. Each split reduces the number of states per block, so a finite
partition stabilizes after finitely many splits. This yields an exact quotient for the declared
finite model, not a claim that the model contains every economically possible company.
Approximate sharing uses the same comparison with an explicit error allowance and a checked
pairing. Threshold regimes and different feasible actions are kept separate unless their mismatch
has been bounded. An unresolved comparison is retained as unresolved, not assigned zero error.
