67
For a fixed common policy class, the uniform cost bound in (9.11) also gives the common-policy
2ε + η regret bound derived in this chapter. If the persistent types are unweighted, apply the
bound within each type. A uniform type-wise bound also bounds the worst-type value, since
| supθ Jθ −supθ eJθ| ≤supθ |Jθ −eJθ|. The adversary does not redraw the contract type at each
date.
The exact reference uses three periods, cash observed by management, a hidden fixed type,
binary borrowing, and a default penalty. It compares complete paths for all 64 policies in the
explicitly restricted class of date-and- zero-cash rules. Removing an irrelevant tag preserves
every path. Merging types is rejected. A synthetic change of 1/20 in each period’s bad-expense
probability gives the conservative cost bound 12{1 −(19/20)3} = 3423/2000. The largest
observed value discrepancies are 4433/8000 and 1/2 in the two types, within the bound. These
finite checks support the stated method and expose its conservatism. They do not supply a
capacity estimate or a bound for an unenumerated continuous company model.
9.18
Error propagation through time
Small one-step errors can accumulate or amplify through nonlinear feedback. For an illustrative
recursion with Lipschitz transition constant L, one-step approximation error at most ϵ and
initial error e0, a bound satisfies
et+1 ≤Let + ϵ.
(9.12)
Iterating gives eT ≤LT e0 + ϵ PT−1
j=0 Lj. When L < 1, the sum is bounded by 1/(1 −L); when
L > 1, it can grow rapidly. This is a local mathematical bound under its assumptions, not a
claim that a company model is globally Lipschitz.
Default and option transitions can be discontinuous. Near them, a small state error can select a
different regime and invalidate a smooth bound. Validation should therefore include boundary
perturbations and multi-period rollouts, not only one-step residuals on typical states.
9.19
Combining exact and sampled components
A hybrid method can keep discrete contract alternatives exact, solve linear accounting relations
symbolically and sample future demand conditional on each alternative. It can also bound
unresolved histories while estimating conditional means for selected regions. This is often more
informative than forcing every uncertainty into one Monte Carlo distribution.
The result must retain which component is exact, bounded or estimated. An exact accounting
identity inside a sampled simulation does not make a sample quantile exact. A certified bound
on one contract region does not cover omitted regions. Mixed results should be combined using
rules appropriate to their evidence type.
A useful report can state that breach is impossible in one region, possible with a verified witness
in another, and probabilistically estimated in a third under an admitted measure. These are
compatible forms of information. Reducing them to one unlabeled scalar would lose their
meaning.
9.20
Stopping rules
A numerical budget should specify what makes an approximation adequate for the decision. A
tolerance on expected cash may be appropriate for planning; a confidence interval for breach


PAGE 90
CHAPTER 9.
APPROXIMATE SIMULATION
68
probability may be needed for a probabilistic statement; a certified lower bound may be needed
for a robust safety claim.
Stopping because the sample mean appears stable can miss a rare region. Repeatedly checking
a confidence interval without accounting for the stopping design can alter its coverage. A
deterministic residual tolerance does not establish statistical accuracy. The stopping criterion
should match the target and method.
If the required evidence cannot be obtained within budget, the answer remains provisional or
unresolved. This is not a reason to discard the model’s useful conditional results. It is a reason
to state what is known, what remains uncertain and which computation would discriminate the
remaining cases.
9.21
Proposed approximation records
Each approximation should record its target, retained family, measure if any, sampling or
discretization rule, random seeds, support assumptions, error estimates, failed regions and
method version. A run manifest supports reproduction; the reader-facing result should explain
the consequences rather than expose every internal field.
Tests should include a small exact reference, a rare-event fixture, a persistent-parameter fixture,
a boundary-sensitive fixture and an intentionally unsupported region. Comparison should use
the same model and query. A fast method solving a simplified target is not a faster solution to
the original target unless the difference is quantified.
The objective is controlled approximation, not universal exactness. The system can remain useful
while refusing an unjustified probability or exact bound. Retaining the original family ensures
that future evidence and better methods can revisit uncertainty that today’s computation could
not resolve.


PAGE 91
Chapter 10
Decisions when probabilities and
histories remain ambiguous
A simulator can describe possible consequences without selecting an action. A decision method
needs an objective, a feasible policy class and a treatment of uncertainty. These are substantive
choices. Worst-case liquidity, expected shareholder value and minimax regret answer different
questions. No accounting identity selects among them. When uncertainty persists over time,
the choice also determines how plans respond to new information.
10.1
Evaluation before optimization
For a fixed policy π, let V (π, h, s) be a defined payoff or cost. It may be terminal cash, discounted
owner distributions, service continuity or another quantity. Its units and horizon should be
stated. Combining several objectives requires weights or constraints that should remain visible.
Evaluating a supplied policy is often the safest first task. It separates economic mechanics from
policy selection. Once those mechanics are checked, optimization can search over an admissible
class. A numerical optimizer should not be allowed to choose actions outside contractual rights
or use information unavailable to the actor.
The distinction also helps interpret historical behavior. A policy that would maximize a modeled
objective need not describe what management actually did. Simulation under that policy is a
normative or counterfactual exercise unless behavioral evidence supports its use as a forecast.
10.2
Expected value under a declared measure
If a joint probability measure P is admitted, a policy can be evaluated by EP [V (π, H, S)]. The
distribution must preserve dependence between history, shocks and signals. A posterior over
report-compatible histories is one possible input, but its prior and likelihood assumptions remain
part of the result.
If two histories have equal likelihood under all retained evidence, their posterior odds depend on
prior odds. More observations of the same uninformative kind do not remove that dependence.
The robust Bayesian analysis of Giacomini and Kitagawa (2021) makes this issue explicit for
set-identified models. Its application here motivates sensitivity to unresolved prior choices, not
an automatic probability for each journal.
A report can show policy rankings under several admitted priors. If every prior gives the same
69


PAGE 92
CHAPTER 10.
DECISIONS UNDER AMBIGUITY
70
ranking, that conclusion is more robust to the specified ambiguity. If rankings differ, a single
average conceals a material decision sensitivity. The next useful evidence may be the fact that
distinguishes those priors’ consequences.
10.3
Worst-case evaluation
For an unweighted feasible family U, a robust payoff is
W(π) = inf
u∈U V (π, u).
(10.1)
A maximin policy maximizes W. This criterion protects against the worst admitted case.
Its conservatism depends on the family and objective.
If the family includes implausible
combinations introduced by a loose relaxation, the policy may respond to a company that
cannot exist.
Robust feasibility is often more interpretable than robust optimization of a single scalar. For
example, require every admitted history to maintain cash above zero, then compare expected
or conditional costs among feasible policies. If no policy satisfies the requirement, the result
should identify the conflicting cases or needed assumptions. It should not silently shrink the
family until feasibility appears.
A worst-case result does not need probabilities, but it does need a defensible scope. An unlimited
shock set can make every policy fail. That may reveal a badly posed decision problem rather
than an economic conclusion. Bounds on shocks and actions require provenance and sensitivity
analysis.
10.4
Regret compares actions within the same world
Regret measures the loss relative to the best feasible action for the same realized world. For a
one-time action a, define
R(a, u) = sup
b∈A
V (b, u) −V (a, u).
(10.2)
Minimax regret chooses the action with the smallest worst regret. The comparator uses the
same u, so it should not combine an optimistic world for one action with a pessimistic world for
another.
Consider two actions and two states with payoffs A = (10, 0) and B = (6, 6). Maximin selects
B because its minimum is 6 rather than zero. Statewise best payoffs are (10, 6), so regrets are
A = (0, 6) and B = (4, 0); minimax regret also selects B here. Other payoff tables can separate
the criteria. Agreement in one example does not establish equivalence.
In sequential settings, the hindsight comparator may use information the actual actor did not
have. That can be intentional in regret analysis, but it must be stated. A claim about imple-
mentable policy improvement should compare policies under the same information restrictions.
10.5
Multiple priors
A set P of probability measures can represent ambiguity about a stochastic model. A lower
expected payoff is
V (π) = inf
P∈P EP [V (π)].
(10.3)


PAGE 93
CHAPTER 10.
DECISIONS UNDER AMBIGUITY
71
This differs from an unweighted family of deterministic worlds. A prior set can retain restrictions
on event probabilities, dependence and learning. Replacing it by all distributions over its support
generally enlarges ambiguity and can change the decision.
A singleton prior set reduces to ordinary expected value. A set containing every point mass over
a finite family reduces lower expectation to the minimum payoff over that family. Intermediate
sets express intermediate restrictions. The representation should preserve which restrictions
came from evidence and which express preferences or modeling caution.
Updating each prior by Bayes’ rule requires positive conditioning probabilities or a declared
extension for null events. Removing priors solely because they yield inconvenient decisions
changes the ambiguity model. A likelihood-based restriction can be justified under a specified
method, but should not be confused with an arbitrary pruning heuristic.
10.6
Why dynamic consistency matters
A policy selected today may cease to be preferred tomorrow after information arrives. Some
changes are rational responses to information. Others arise because the decision criterion
evaluates future uncertainty differently from the original plan. To model behavior, one must
specify whether the actor commits to the original policy or reoptimizes.
Epstein and Schneider (2002, Definition 3.1 and Theorem 3.2) connects recursive multiple-
priors preferences, dynamic consistency and rectangular prior sets under its stated axioms and
information filtration. Rectangularity permits specified combinations of one-step marginals
and conditional distributions. It is a substantive restriction on the prior family relative to
information, not a harmless implementation trick.
The source’s theorem is not a statement that every economically natural uncertainty set is
rectangular. Nor does it authorize changing a fixed unknown contract at each date. Applying a
recursive worst-case operator without examining the allowed cross-time combinations can solve
a different decision problem.
10.7
A concrete pasting counterexample
Consider two dates of losses and a hidden type θ that remains fixed. If θ = A, losses are (0, 10).
If θ = B, they are (10, 0). The total loss is 10 in either type. Thus the worst total loss over the
original two-world family is 10.
If a stagewise procedure independently selects the larger loss at each date, it constructs (10, 10)
and reports total loss 20. That path is absent from the original family. The procedure has
allowed the adversary to switch type across dates. It computes a worst case over an enlarged
family.
Calling the enlarged result conservative does not make it equal to the original target. It may be
useful as an outer bound, but the target must be labeled. Retaining θ in the state prevents the
unintended switch. This simple example is the deterministic analogue of the dependence issues
that rectangularity addresses for prior sets.
10.8
Rectangular enlargement changes the model
A rectangular hull can facilitate recursive evaluation by admitting combinations of conditionals
that were not jointly present in the original prior set. The source explicitly discusses enlargement


PAGE 94
CHAPTER 10.
DECISIONS UNDER AMBIGUITY
72
and its relation to the information structure. In an economic application, added combinations
may represent an adversary whose choices adapt over time.
If the original ambiguity concerns one fixed but unknown loan contract, allowing adaptive
adversarial changes to that contract is usually a different physical model. One can retain the
contract type as part of the state and formulate conditional uncertainty appropriately. If a
robust policy intentionally protects against changing terms, those changes should be modeled
as amendments, refinancing or legal events.
AccDSL should therefore record whether a robust method uses the original joint ambiguity
set, a rectangular enlargement or a commitment formulation. The result should compare the
enlargement’s effect on important outputs where feasible. A convenient Bellman recursion does
not justify hiding that change.
10.9
Commitment and reoptimization
A commitment policy is selected at the initial date as a full rule for future information histories.
A reoptimizing actor selects a new continuation policy at each date. These can differ under
nonrectangular ambiguity or changing preferences. The simulator should not alternate between
them silently.
For a treasury policy approved by a board, some actions may be committed while others
remain discretionary. A hedge may be binding after execution; a future dividend target may
be revisable. The feasible policy class should represent these commitments. Reoptimizing an
already executed hedge as though it never occurred gives the actor an unavailable option.
A useful analysis can compare commitment and reoptimization explicitly. Differences then
reveal the value or cost of flexibility under the chosen uncertainty model. They should not be
attributed solely to forecasting skill.
10.10
Distributional ambiguity and historical ambiguity
Uncertainty about which debt contract exists differs from uncertainty about next year’s demand
distribution. Both can enter a decision, but they should retain separate origins. A contract
family may be constrained by notes; a demand ambiguity set may be estimated from data or
chosen for stress robustness.
Combining them independently can be unjustified if financing structure and demand exposure
are related. The joint ambiguity set should preserve known dependence. Conversely, imposing
a relation merely because it makes optimization easier can exclude valid histories. The default
should be explicit uncertainty, not an invented joint law.
A layered result can report policy performance conditional on contract region and across
demand models. This makes it possible to ask whether better contract evidence or better
demand estimation would change the decision. A single blended uncertainty score obscures that
distinction.
10.11
Risk constraints and probability thresholds
A chance constraint such as P(Ct < 0) ≤ϵ requires a probability model and a specified time
event. A bound on failure at each date differs from a bound on failure at any date. Summing
marginal probabilities gives a union bound, not generally the exact path probability.


PAGE 95
CHAPTER 10.
DECISIONS UNDER AMBIGUITY
73
If failure events are strongly dependent, treating them as independent can distort the probability
of ever breaching. Persistent contract uncertainty is one source of this dependence. A company
with a floating loan may remain vulnerable in every high-rate period; redrawing the type can
alter duration and clustering.
A robust chance constraint requires the probability bound under every measure in an admitted
set. It is stronger than a constraint under an averaged measure. Numerical estimation must
account for sampling error near the threshold. A point estimate just below the limit is not
sufficient evidence of compliance.
10.12
Value of information under ambiguity
A proposed disclosure partitions the history family. Under a probability model, expected
information value compares optimized expected payoffs before and after the signal, accounting
for its cost and timing. Under maximin or regret, the criterion changes. Information can matter
because it enables different feasible actions in different regions.
A signal that arrives after a financing commitment may have little operational value even if
it greatly narrows a posterior. A signal about an irrelevant parameter may reduce statistical
uncertainty without changing decisions. The useful target is the decision consequence, not
entropy reduction alone.
Without defensible probabilities, one can report the range of improvements across possible signal
outcomes and criteria. A guaranteed improvement may be zero even when some outcomes are
valuable. This is a meaningful conclusion and avoids assigning probabilities merely to produce
an expected number.
10.13
Selecting a method for a user question
For disclosure explanation, preserve possible and certain answers. For a supplied stress, evaluate
conditional mechanics. For planning under an admitted distribution, estimate expected values
and relevant risks. For a safety requirement across compatible histories, use robust feasibility
and bounds. For competing actions without probabilities, consider dominance and explicitly
chosen robust or regret criteria.
Dominance is particularly useful: if one feasible policy performs at least as well in every admitted
world and strictly better in some, it can be preferred under many monotone criteria. If policies
cross, the choice depends on preferences or probability assumptions. The simulator should show
that dependence rather than invent a universal ranking.
A method-selection interface should ask for a criterion only when the user has requested a
decision that the evidence alone cannot determine. It can still complete mechanical comparisons
and identify dominant or infeasible options.
This preserves progress while keeping value
judgments explicit.
10.14
Implementation and validation
A decision record should specify actor, objective, information, feasible actions, uncertainty set
or measure, commitment convention, horizon and terminal treatment. Terminal assumptions
matter: liquidating assets at book value at the horizon can create an artificial reward and
change earlier decisions.


PAGE 96
CHAPTER 10.
DECISIONS UNDER AMBIGUITY
74
Small exact finite examples should compare direct policy enumeration with recursive methods.
Include the fixed-type pasting counterexample, an information-revelation example, a capacity-
constrained financing example and a case where expected value and robust choice disagree.
These fixtures discriminate semantic errors before expensive numerical optimization.
Passing them establishes the specified criterion and timing mechanics. It does not establish that
the criterion describes management or that the uncertainty set is empirically adequate. Those
remain separate assumptions or research questions. The system can support several methods
without declaring one a universal default.


PAGE 97
Chapter 11
Solving the economic equations that
the model actually states
A correct financial representation can still be evaluated by the wrong numerical method.
Conversely, a numerical method can converge accurately to equations that describe the wrong
financing problem. The first obligation is to state the target equations, their timing and their
feasible domain. Only then does it make sense to choose a solver or train a neural approximation.
The risky-debt convention discussed earlier illustrates why a small residual is insufficient: an
inconsistent cash convention can be solved very accurately.
11.1
Separate identities, conditions and approximations
Accounting identities reconcile stocks and flows. Contract equations determine obligations from
terms. Behavioral conditions describe choices. Equilibrium conditions connect counterparties
and prices. Numerical approximations evaluate these relations with finite resources. They
should not be treated as interchangeable equations in an undifferentiated loss.
An accounting identity can often be enforced exactly by construction. A lender pricing equation
may involve an expectation and an endogenous default rule. A management optimum may
require inequalities and boundary conditions. A reporting standard may require a categorical
treatment selected by facts. Each type needs a suitable validation method.
A residual vector can combine them for computation, but the result should still report each
component in meaningful units. A small average residual can hide one economically decisive
violation, especially when large accounting amounts dominate the scale. A pricing residual of
one currency unit and a share-count residual of one share are not directly comparable without
a declared normalization.
11.2
The Bellman problem
For a finite-horizon shareholder problem, let x be state, a a feasible action, dt(x, a) the current
net distribution to the modeled owners, and β a discount factor under the chosen valuation
formulation. A Bellman equation is
Vt(x) =
sup
a∈At(x)
{dt(x, a) + βE[Vt+1(Xt+1) | x, a]} .
(11.1)
75


PAGE 98
CHAPTER 11.
SOLVING THE ECONOMIC EQUATIONS
76
The expectation uses the declared transition law and information. If an action depends on
private information or a belief state, the state must be defined accordingly. The terminal value
VT is an assumption with economic consequences, not merely a numerical boundary condition.
A liquidation-at-book-value terminal rule can make asset accumulation artificially attractive. A
zero terminal value can induce premature payouts or abandonment. A continuation approx-
imation can be useful, but its accuracy and domain should be checked. Changing terminal
treatment while comparing solvers changes the target.
In a stylized limited-liability problem, continuation value may be compared with default or exit
value. The choice and its consequences must be specified. A maximum operator alone does not
provide a legal restructuring model or creditor recovery schedule.
11.3
Pricing and value must agree on policy
Suppose debt price P(x, F) depends on repayment under future policy π. The policy depends
on funding proceeds and therefore on P. A joint solution must satisfy both the policy-value
problem and lender pricing for that same policy.
One can write residuals RV (V, π, P) = 0 and RP (V, π, P) = 0, supplemented by feasibility and
optimality conditions. Solving RV with a fixed old price and RP with a different policy leaves
a mismatch. Alternating iteration can be a candidate algorithm, but convergence and target
consistency need checking.
The retained Duarte and Fonseca (2026) working paper uses a lagged value network in part
of its pricing training and a subsequent local grid refinement. These are specific numerical
choices in its model. They do not supply a general convergence or global-optimality guarantee
for AccDSL. Transferring them requires a target-specific protocol and matched timing, recovery,
taxes and state definitions.
11.4
A residual can target the wrong expectation
Let r(x, ε; θ) be a random equation contribution with parameterized functions θ. Suppose the
desired residual is the conditional expectation m(x; θ) = E[r | x]. Squared expected residual is
m(x; θ)2. Squaring a single random contribution and averaging gives
E[r2 | x] = m(x; θ)2 + Var(r | x).
(11.2)
The second objective adds a variance term. If that variance depends on θ, minimizing the two
objectives can select different functions. This is a mathematical target mismatch, not merely
noisy estimation of the same loss.
With conditionally independent draws ε1, ε2 under the same x,
E[r(x, ε1; θ)r(x, ε2; θ) | x] = m(x; θ)2.
(11.3)
Independence is conditional on the common state. Reusing the same draw restores the unwanted
variance term. Maliar et al. (2021, Definitions 2.5–2.9 and Algorithm 1) discusses objective
formulations for dynamic economic equations, including this distinction. The local derivation
explains why the sampling convention must match the intended residual.


PAGE 99
CHAPTER 11.
SOLVING THE ECONOMIC EQUATIONS
77
11.5
A small objective counterexample
Let r(θ, ε) = θ −1 + θε, with E[ε] = 0 and variance one. Then m(θ) = θ −1, so the desired
squared residual is (θ −1)2, minimized at one. The single-draw squared objective is
(θ −1)2 + θ2 = 2θ2 −2θ + 1,
(11.4)
minimized at one half. At that point the expected equation residual is negative one half. The
optimizer has correctly minimized the wrong objective.
This exact example is a useful unit test for residual construction. It also shows why training
loss alone cannot certify economic equations. Validation should directly evaluate the target
conditional residual using independent quadrature or sufficiently controlled sampling.
11.6
Inequalities and complementary slackness
A borrowing or cash constraint can bind. Interior first-order conditions do not apply unchanged
at a boundary. Consider minimizing a differentiable cost f(a) subject to a ≥0. With multiplier
λ ≥0, the Karush–Kuhn–Tucker conditions under appropriate regularity include
f′(a) −λ = 0,
a ≥0,
λ ≥0,
aλ = 0.
(11.5)
If a > 0, the multiplier is zero and the interior derivative vanishes. If a = 0, the derivative need
not vanish. A residual enforcing only f′(a) = 0 can demand an infeasible negative action.
For f(a) = (a + 1)2, the constrained minimum is a = 0, with derivative 2 and multiplier 2. An
interior solver seeks a = −1, which violates the constraint. Clipping its output to zero may
recover the action in this example, but does not establish a general constrained solution or
correct derivatives through the clipping operation.
Complementarity formulations, active-set methods and direct constrained optimization are
possible approaches.
Their conditions are necessary or sufficient only under their stated
assumptions. Nonconvex financing problems may have local optima and discrete choices; a
small KKT residual does not prove global optimality.
11.7
Discrete choices and nondifferentiability
Default, conversion, refinancing and investment shutdown can be discrete. Their value functions
may have kinks or discontinuities. Differentiating a smooth surrogate through these decisions
can optimize a smoothed problem rather than the original one.
A smooth approximation may be useful as a warm start. Its smoothing parameter changes
the target and should be varied, with final policies evaluated under the original event rules.
A soft default indicator that pays a fractional blend of full repayment and recovery does not
describe an actual realized default event unless explicitly interpreted as an expectation under a
probabilistic model.
Near a threshold, classification error can dominate value error. A network can fit values closely
while moving the default boundary enough to affect debt pricing. Validation should include
state pairs on both sides of the boundary and pricing consequences under the resulting policy.


PAGE 100
CHAPTER 11.
SOLVING THE ECONOMIC EQUATIONS
78
11.8
Partial and total derivatives
A financing choice can affect a price directly and through future policy. If P(a) = P(a, π(a)),
the total derivative includes both paths:
dP
da = ∂P
∂a + ∂P
∂π
dπ
da.
(11.6)
Holding a learned policy fixed computes a partial derivative. It may be the intended derivative
in an alternating algorithm, but it is not automatically the total derivative of the equilibrium
price.
Automatic differentiation differentiates the implemented computation. It does not decide
which economic dependencies should be present. Stop-gradient operations, target networks and
detached iterations can deliberately omit paths. Their use should be justified relative to the
algorithm and validated against the final target.
An implicit derivative can be derived when a smooth equilibrium equation has a nonsingular
Jacobian, but default boundaries and multiple equilibria can violate those conditions. Numerical
finite differences can diagnose implementation mistakes away from such boundaries; they do
not resolve the economic choice among equilibria.
11.9
Scaling and units
Residuals measured in millions of currency, fractions, shares and utility can differ by orders
of magnitude. A loss that sums their squares without scaling effectively assigns arbitrary
importance based on units. Changing currency units from dollars to millions can change the
trained solution if the formulation is not invariant.
Normalization should be documented and checked. One can scale a cash residual by a meaningful
transaction or balance scale, a share residual by outstanding units, and a probability residual
by its natural range. Zero or near-zero denominators need explicit treatment. An adaptive scale
can itself alter the objective over training.
A unit-rescaling test is useful: transform all monetary inputs consistently and confirm that the
implied economic policy and normalized diagnostics transform appropriately. Failure reveals a
numerical or modeling dependence on representation rather than economics.
11.10
Training distribution and the queried domain
A learned solver is trained on selected states. If training emphasizes typical solvent states, it
may be unreliable near liquidity crises, maturity walls or default. Uniform sampling in raw
coordinates is not automatically balanced in economic importance. A high-dimensional box
may contain mostly infeasible states.
The training protocol should identify feasible-state generation, boundary coverage, tails, heldout
regions and distribution shift. Data from simulated policies can omit states reached by a different
policy or scenario. Updating the policy can therefore change the relevant state distribution.
No architecture, learning rate or loss weighting should become a default merely because it
worked on another model. Transfer can provide a baseline or warm start. A substantially
different target needs its own capacity, optimization, budget and validation protocol. This
manuscript proposes no promoted neural default without that evidence.


PAGE 101
CHAPTER 11.
SOLVING THE ECONOMIC EQUATIONS
79
11.11
Causal generators and feasible policy transfer
Acciaio, Eckstein and Hou construct a time-causal variational autoencoder whose encoder and
decoder at date t use only input coordinates available by t. Their retained preprint bounds a
directional causal transport discrepancy by reconstruction error and a term involving latent
relative entropy (Acciaio et al., 2024, Lemma 2.4, Theorem 2.5 and Corollary 2.6). The relative-
entropy step requires the reconstructed and generated distributions to have the stated bounded
support. Normalizing the observed sample does not establish that a learned decoder satisfies
that support restriction for every generated input. A finite training estimate is also different
from a population bound.
There is a further issue in transferring the control result. Theorem 3.1 of the retained version
states its comparison for a closed convex subset H of adapted strategies. Its proof takes a
policy G on the second process and constructs eG(x) = E[G(Y ) | X = x]. Causality establishes
adaptedness, but adaptedness alone does not establish eG ∈H. Here is a direct counterexample
to that stated generality. There is one date, µ = δ1, ν = δ0, and
H = {h},
h(x) = min(1, max(0, x)),
Q(x, a) = a.
(11.7)
The singleton class is closed and convex, and Q is convex in a and uniformly zero-Lipschitz in
x. Nevertheless, V (µ) −V (ν) = 1, whereas the proposed right-hand side with L = 0 is zero.
The transferred action at x = 1 is zero, outside the singleton policy class. This disproves the
unrestricted policy-class statement in that retained version. It does not refute the argument
when the feasible class is explicitly closed under the conditional transfer. The published edition
has not been matched equation by equation, so this is not asserted to be a journal erratum.
For AccDSL, state-dependent borrowing limits, integer issuance sizes and contractual exercise
restrictions make closure substantive. A repaired transfer argument must verify feasibility of
the mapped policy, or use a method that retains the original feasible actions. The one-sided
value comparison is not itself a bound on the performance of the same learned policy after
deployment. The inspected paper-linked code has forward LSTM encoder/decoder paths, but
also external conditioning variables; those variables require their own availability dates. Its
selected conditional decoder uses ReLU and a final linear layer, so the architecture does not
enforce the theorem’s unit-ball support. These observations identify additional obligations
before adoption; no training result is inferred from source-code inspection.
11.12
Controlled transition laws and off-policy support
Saldı and Yüksel distinguish weak and strong kernel-embedding topologies and give a square-
wave counterexample to their equivalence (Saldı and Yüksel, 2025, Theorem 4.1 and proof). Let
Y be uniform on [0, 1]. On alternating half-cells of a fine partition, make a binary successor U
deterministically 1 or 0. As the partition becomes finer, smooth integrated tests can approach
those of a fair binary kernel. At every particular input, however, the conditional law remains a
point mass. For the Kronecker kernel on {0, 1}, its squared embedding discrepancy from the
fair law is 1/2. The local exact calculation checks this discrepancy and the vanishing error for a
linear test, rather than claiming that a few tests prove weak convergence.
Their discounted-control application uses bounded continuous costs, finite actions, continuous
transition kernels and a common dominating measure, including for the initial distribution. One
route requires a strong essential-supremum condition; the weaker route adds bounded equicon-
tinuous densities (Saldı and Yüksel, 2025, Assumptions 2–4, equation (11), Theorems 5.1–5.2).
A default discontinuity, hidden creditor information or an action-dependent financing constraint
therefore needs a separate argument. An equally important problem is support: models that


PAGE 102
CHAPTER 11.
SOLVING THE ECONOMIC EQUATIONS
80
agree under the historically chosen action may disagree under a new action. With action losses
(1, 100) in the real model and (1, 0) in an approximate model, historical use of the first action
gives identical observations. Optimizing the approximation chooses the second action and
incurs regret 99. Agreement under the historical policy alone cannot establish a policy-uniform
approximation claim.
The original robustness result used in that application makes another condition explicit. Kara
and Yüksel distinguish continuity of optimal values from robustness of the policies attaining them.
Their discounted-control argument uses continuous kernel convergence, bounded continuous
costs and compact actions. Policy transfer additionally requires each approximate policy to be
optimal from every initial state, as when it satisfies the discounted Bellman equation throughout
the state space (Kara and Yüksel, 2020, Assumption 1.1, Theorems 4.2–4.4 and Appendix A.5).
An optimizer trained only from a historical starting state can leave its actions elsewhere arbitrary;
the true transition can reach those neglected states. The paper’s counterexample makes precisely
this distinction. Its finite-state learning result also assumes continuing coverage of every state–
action pair (Kara and Yüksel, 2020, Corollary 5.1). Financial statements generated by one
management policy do not by themselves provide that coverage.
11.13
Classical reference methods
Small discretized problems can be solved by direct enumeration, grid search, backward induction
or carefully controlled quadrature. These methods can be slow, but they provide transparent
references for testing learned or accelerated methods. The reference should be strong enough to
answer the same target question.
A weak coarse grid can make a surrogate appear superior merely because the comparator is
underresolved. A fair comparison includes convergence or refinement evidence for the reference
and matched terminal conditions, constraints and state domains. Comparing different models
is not a solver benchmark.
Exact rational examples are especially useful for cash identities and simple pricing. They are
not substitutes for numerical validation of high-dimensional expectations, but they can expose
sign, timing and unit errors before expensive training. The positive-recovery debt example
belongs in this first layer.
11.14
Multiple solutions and selection
A nonlinear system can have no solution, one solution or several. A solver initialized differently
may converge to different roots. Reporting the first root as the economic equilibrium requires a
selection argument or an explicit conditional interpretation.
A financing market can have feedback in which perceived distress raises spreads and higher
spreads increase distress. A stylized fixed point may admit low- and high-spread solutions. Sta-
bility under a chosen iteration is a numerical property, not necessarily an economic equilibrium-
selection criterion.
The method should search or bound multiplicity where material, report dependence on initial-
ization and preserve unresolved alternatives. If only one local root is found, describe it as such.
A failed solver does not establish that no financing equilibrium exists.


PAGE 103
CHAPTER 11.
SOLVING THE ECONOMIC EQUATIONS
81
11.15
Downstream validation
A learned value function is useful only if its errors are acceptable for the decisions and reports it
drives. Validation should evaluate feasible actions, debt price consistency, default classification,
cash paths, ownership changes and accounting outputs under heldout scenarios. Low prediction
error alone is insufficient.
A pricing error can alter proceeds, which changes investment, which changes default and feeds
back into price. A one-step residual screen may miss that loop. Multi-period policy evaluation
under an independent method is needed for a claim about the coupled computation.
Stochastic comparisons require uncertainty evidence. One seed or a few short runs can nominate
a candidate but cannot establish superiority from descriptive averages alone. Hard failures,
descriptive differences and statistically supported rankings should remain separate. This is
particularly important when rare defaults dominate economic conclusions.
11.16
A proposed solver interface
The solver receives a typed economic problem: state domain, timing, objectives, constraints,
transition and observation laws, price equations and requested accuracy.
It returns func-
tions or policies together with target residuals, feasibility diagnostics, unresolved regions and
reproducibility information.
A solver should not mutate the economic model to obtain convergence without recording the
change. Smoothing, truncation, penalty relaxation and altered terminal values define modified
problems. Their relation to the original target must be evaluated. A successful run on a
modified problem is not an implicit solution of the original.
AccDSL can support several solvers behind the same problem definition. The interface should
preserve method-specific limitations rather than force every solver to claim identical guarantees.
A certified finite result, a local numerical solution and a learned approximation can all be useful
when labeled accurately.
11.17
The smallest justified numerical program
Begin with a one-period debt model whose face, proceeds and recovery are distinct. Verify
cash-leg pricing and limited-liability conventions exactly. Add a finite-state dynamic model
with explicit feasible policies and compare enumeration with backward induction. Then add
borrowing limits, equity issuance and default transitions one at a time.
Only after these targets are stable should a learned solver be compared on the same equations.
The comparison needs a predeclared primary criterion, veto diagnostics, budget ladder and
heldout downstream checks. Training settings remain hypotheses until supported on this target.
This staged approach does not postpone the difficult economic questions. It exposes them early
in forms that can be checked. The purpose of deep learning is to accelerate a well-defined
computation, not to make an ambiguous financing model appear resolved through a low loss
value.


PAGE 104
Chapter 12
Validation, adversarial cases, and the
limits of a simulation
A credible simulator should fail intelligibly when its premises are insufficient. It should also
be tested against cases where a plausible implementation gives a confident but wrong answer.
The purpose of validation is not to accumulate a large count of passing assertions. It is to
distinguish errors in evidence, representation, economics, accounting, information, numerical
calculation and interpretation. Each class requires a different test and a different repair.
12.1
What is being validated
There are at least three separate questions. Does the implementation compute the specified
model? Is the specified model mathematically and economically coherent? Does the model
usefully describe the company or decision under study? A passing unit test can answer part of
the first question. It cannot answer the third by itself.
For example, a simulator can correctly implement unlimited borrowing at a fixed rate. That
implementation may pass every accounting reconciliation. It remains unsuitable for a claim
about refinancing risk unless unlimited borrowing is an appropriate premise. Conversely, a
realistic financing model can be implemented with the wrong proceeds convention. The two
failures should not be blurred into a generic low accuracy score.
A validation record should state the claimed target, quantity actually computed, comparison
method and interpretation. If the target is a worst-case cash bound but the method reports a
sampled minimum, the mismatch is explicit. The sampled result may still be useful as a witness;
it should not inherit the stronger claim.
12.2
An extensible failure taxonomy
No finite catalogue can prove that every possible simulation failure has been anticipated. New
contracts, standards, behaviors and software interactions can create new cases. A systematic
taxonomy is nevertheless valuable because it exposes the dimensions along which failures arise
and guides new tests.
The taxonomy used here follows the calculation: evidence admission, historical reconstruction,
economic state, actor information, scenario definition, feasible behavior, financing, accounting,
aggregation, approximation and reporting. Cross-cutting dimensions include time, units, entity,
82


PAGE 105
CHAPTER 12.
VALIDATION AND FAILURE ANALYSIS
83
dependence, precision and provenance. A failure can occupy several categories. A maturity
error, for example, affects evidence, timing, financing and liquidity.
Coverage should be expressed by mechanisms and boundaries, not only by scenario count. One
thousand random rate paths do not test a missing conversion clause. A single carefully designed
positive-recovery case can expose a proceeds-versus-face error that many no-default paths miss.
12.3
Evidence admission failures
A value can be extracted from the wrong row, assigned the wrong sign, scaled by the wrong
unit or associated with the wrong period. A note can be omitted because it is a scanned image.
A cross-reference can point to an incorporated document outside the downloaded filing. An
amended filing can be mistaken for the original.
Tests should include duplicate labels with different contexts, negative values in parentheses,
mixed currencies, overlapping periods and rounded totals. The expected result should preserve
uncertainty where the source is ambiguous. A parser that always returns a number may look
complete while inventing precision.
A source coverage report should distinguish retrieved, parsed, interpreted and modeled content.
These are different achievements. Retrieving every page does not establish semantic completeness.
A passage that cannot be formalized should remain linked to the model as unresolved material
rather than disappearing from the scope claim.
12.4
Reconstruction failures
Historical balances can reconcile while hidden gross flows are wrong. A net borrowing change can
conceal large issuance and repayment. A net share change can conceal issuance and repurchase
at different prices. An aggregate receivable can conceal concentration. Tests should use pairs of
histories with the same observed totals and different future responses.
The family should include both histories when evidence permits them. A selected witness
should be labeled as selected. If a regularizer removes one, the result should identify the
selection assumption and its consequences. A duplicate encoding of one history must not alter
probabilities or set membership.
Infeasibility tests should distinguish source contradiction from solver failure. Deliberately
conflicting exact observations should produce an interpretable conflict. Deliberately difficult but
feasible constraints should not be reported impossible merely because the budget expires. Un-
supported instruments should remain outside the solved profile with their economic significance
visible.
12.5
State sufficiency failures
A state can omit a quantity that matters later: accrued interest, remaining loss carryforward,
asset age, covenant cure clock, collateral ownership, unpaid dividends or option exercise status.
Current statements may still match. A multi-period replay reveals the omission.
A useful test starts from two histories with equal proposed compressed state but different omitted
quantities. Apply the same future policy and shocks. If outcomes differ, the compression is
insufficient for that query. If observations differ and later decisions change, the information
component is insufficient even when immediate cash agrees.


PAGE 106
CHAPTER 12.
VALIDATION AND FAILURE ANALYSIS
84
The repair is to retain the missing state or restrict the compression’s claimed domain. Adding a
balancing adjustment after the divergence hides the cause. A state variable should be justified
by future dependence, not by its convenience in a data schema.
12.6
Information failures
Look-ahead can enter through restated data, future prices, a sampled shock passed to a policy,
or a compressed state containing private information the actor lacks. It can also enter through
training labels if a purported real-time decision uses features calculated from the full future
path.
A nonanticipativity test changes future unobserved data while holding current information fixed
and checks current actions. A publication test shifts a disclosure after the decision cutoff and
checks that it no longer influences that decision. A private-information test permits an informed
manager to respond to a known contract while preventing a public analyst from treating it as
observed.
These tests must inspect policy inputs and execution, not merely documentation. An interface
can claim to hide future information while a cached feature leaks it. Provenance should connect
each decision input to an information rule and availability time.
12.7
Scenario-definition failures
A shock can be applied twice, applied to the wrong rate, or applied for the wrong fraction of
a year. A currency depreciation can be inverted. A rate shock can affect a fixed coupon that
should not reset. A scenario can change both demand and price while being described as a pure
volume shock.
Metamorphic tests are useful when exact full outcomes are hard to specify. A zero shock
should reproduce the baseline under unchanged policies and information. Splitting one identical
contract into two proportional pieces should preserve aggregate cash when no nonlinear clause
is introduced. Changing currency units consistently should scale monetary outputs without
changing dimensionless decisions.
These invariances have premises. Contract splitting can change fees, concentration limits or
legal rights. A test should enforce invariance only where the model says it holds. Otherwise the
test itself becomes an incorrect economic requirement.
12.8
Financing failures
Face can be mistaken for proceeds, net cash for gross cash, coupon for effective interest, or book
debt for market value. Refinancing can be assumed available without an offer. A fee can be
charged twice. Recovery can be distributed beyond available assets. A conversion can coincide
with an unlinked redemption.
The positive-recovery diagnostic in Chapter 5 is a primary fixture because it distinguishes two
conventions that agree in simpler cases. Additional tests should use discounted issuance, delayed
settlement, binding capacity, failed subscription, priority and recovery delay.
A no-funding result is economically meaningful only after the method has correctly evaluated
the admitted offers and actions. A numerical pricing failure should be reported separately. If


PAGE 107
CHAPTER 12.
VALIDATION AND FAILURE ANALYSIS
85
the model omits a financing channel, the conclusion is conditional on that omission; it is not
proof that the real company has no financing option.
12.9
Accounting failures
A journal can balance while mapping borrowing to revenue, treating a valuation change as cash,
or recognizing a transaction twice. A reporting aggregation can combine different perimeters or
bases. A cash-flow label from an economic model can be mapped incorrectly to a standard’s
category.
Tests need event-specific expected postings under explicitly supplied rules. They should reconcile
physical quantities, cash, obligations and accounting balances. Where authority or facts are
unresolved, the expected result may be a refusal or conditional treatment, not a forced entry.
A standard-specific test establishes behavior under the retained authority version and admitted
facts. It does not establish universal compliance across jurisdictions and dates. The rule package
should preserve applicability conditions, source passages and unresolved interpretations.
12.10
Aggregation and dependence failures
Independent marginal ranges can create impossible combinations. Stagewise worst cases can
switch persistent types. A mean state can cross a nonlinear threshold differently from every
original state. A scenario reduction can remove the path causing a maturity shortfall.
Tests should retain a full small reference family and compare exact joint paths with the
compressed representation. Check outputs, observations, feasible actions and policy values, not
just means. Include a case where fixed-action sequences agree but adaptive decisions differ.
A failed exact aggregation test can still motivate an approximate method with bounds. It
should not be repaired by weakening the test until the original claim passes. State whether the
target changed and quantify the consequence for the decision.
12.11
Approximation failures
Particles can lose a persistent contract type. Importance sampling can omit support. A surrogate
can extrapolate beyond training. A numerical tolerance can classify a covenant incorrectly. A
Monte Carlo sample can contain no rare defaults while the true modeled probability is positive.
A small exact reference provides a baseline. Coverage checks verify that rare or boundary
regions are visited or bounded. Replications and uncertainty intervals assess stochastic estimates.
Distinct seeds are not independent evidence if every run shares the same misspecified transition
or omitted mechanism.
A computational diagnostic should be assigned a role before interpretation. It may veto validity,
explain behavior, trigger repair or support promotion under a declared criterion. A low loss can
nominate a candidate; it does not automatically promote it to a default solver.
12.12
Terminal and horizon failures
A model can postpone default beyond the horizon, refinance indefinitely without repayment,
or liquidate assets at an unrealistic terminal value. Extending the horizon can reveal that an


PAGE 108
CHAPTER 12.
VALIDATION AND FAILURE ANALYSIS
86
apparently safe policy merely shifts the problem.
Tests should compare adjacent horizons under consistent continuation assumptions and inspect
obligations remaining at the end. A terminal balance sheet should retain outstanding debt,
contingent rights and future commitments. A clean closing cash number is not a complete
account of solvency.
The terminal rule should be part of every policy comparison. Using a favorable terminal value
for one method and zero for another is an unfair comparison. A finite-horizon model can still
be useful, but its conclusion should state what remains outside the horizon.
12.13
Interaction failures
Individually correct modules can compose incorrectly. A debt amendment can change future
interest while a cached tax calculation uses the old schedule. A share conversion can update
ownership but leave diluted earnings per share on the old count. A group transfer can be
recorded in both entities but omitted from elimination.
Composition tests should follow complete event sequences across modules. They should check
shared identities, dates, units and versions. A module’s standalone test suite cannot certify
every interaction. The most valuable sequences are those crossing boundaries: refinancing plus
fees plus tax, conversion plus ownership plus reporting, or default plus collateral plus recovery.
A failed interaction should be localized to its violated relation. Re-running a broad test suite
without understanding the failure can obscure whether the model, adapter or shared state is
wrong. The repair should preserve unaffected evidence and explicitly update dependent results.
12.14
A hierarchy of reference calculations
Start with hand-reconstructible exact cases using rational arithmetic. Then use finite-state
enumeration for learning and policy tests. Add independent numerical quadrature or refined
grids for continuous small models. Only then compare scalable approximate methods on the
same target.
A reference calculation must itself be audited. Coarse grids, truncation and arbitrary terminal
values can bias it. Agreement between two implementations sharing the same mistake is not
strong validation. Independent derivations and different computational routes are valuable
where the economic conclusion is important.
The hierarchy is not a requirement to solve every real company exactly. It establishes local
semantics and numerical behavior before applying methods to larger unresolved settings. The
large application retains its own evidence and domain limitations.
12.15
Empirical validation
Forecast validation requires a defined prediction target, information cutoff, evaluation period
and benchmark. A statement reconstruction evaluated against the same statements it was
constrained to match is not an out-of-sample forecast test. A restated target can differ from
the information that was observable at prediction time.
Probabilistic forecasts need scoring methods appropriate to their target. The proper-scoring
literature represented in the inherited bibliography by Gneiting and Raftery (2007) motivates


PAGE 109
CHAPTER 12.
VALIDATION AND FAILURE ANALYSIS
87
evaluating distributions rather than only point means. Applicability still requires a correctly
defined observed outcome and a forecast distribution produced without leakage.
Set-valued predictions need measures of coverage and informativeness under a declared evaluation
design. A very broad set can cover every outcome while offering little decision value. Narrowness
without coverage is also inadequate. The tradeoff should be stated before comparing methods.
12.16
No empirical result is claimed here
The examples in this part are exact or analytic diagnostics of proposed semantics. They are
not fitted company forecasts, evidence of profitability or rankings of simulation methods. No
neural training campaign or stochastic benchmark is performed as part of this writing task.
This distinction keeps the next research step clear. A future empirical study must choose
companies, evidence cutoffs, scenarios, baselines, outcomes and uncertainty analysis. It should
preserve the question separately from the implementation checklist and state what result would
invalidate the harness rather than merely reject a candidate method.
A negative result can reveal a bad implementation, insufficient tuning, an inadequate observation
model or a weak economic mechanism. These explanations require different follow-up. Treating
every failure as rejection of the entire direction would waste the diagnostic information.
12.17
Failure behavior is part of the specification
The system should return different statuses for unsupported instrument, contradictory evi-
dence, undecided feasibility, infeasible action, economic default, numerical nonconvergence and
completed approximation. A result can contain several statuses across regions.
Unsuccessful regions must not disappear from aggregate results. Renormalizing probabilities
after dropping numerical failures changes the distribution, often favorably if difficult cases
are distressed. A report should state unresolved probability mass when a measure exists, or
unresolved feasible regions otherwise.
A completed run also needs a trace to its assumptions and evidence. Reproducibility requires
exact versions of source views, rules, methods and numerical settings. A content hash identifies
a file; it does not prove the file’s correctness. Both provenance and substantive validation are
needed.
12.18
From catalogue to implementation work
The proposed failure catalogue should be machine-readable enough to link each mechanism to a
fixture, expected outcome and relevant chapter, while the manuscript explains why the failure
matters. New cases can be added without claiming that the catalogue is exhaustive.
Implementation can proceed by vertical slices. A slice reconstructs a bounded history family,
applies a scenario and policy, generates events and journals, produces statements and reports
unresolved regions. This is more discriminating than implementing isolated formula modules
without a source-to-result case.
The first slice should preserve ambiguous debt type, issue proceeds distinct from face, a
binding financing limit and actor information. Later slices add ownership rights, group transfer
constraints, persistent tax state and approximate inference. Each slice should retain the wider


PAGE 110
CHAPTER 12.
VALIDATION AND FAILURE ANALYSIS
88
open scope rather than redefine the mission to match what is easiest to execute.
12.19
What successful completion would mean
For this document, success means an explicit and traceable argument, checked illustrative
calculations, preserved prior work, inspected source support and a clear account of proposed
methods and their failure cases. It does not mean that all methods are implemented or that
the product is ready for unrestricted use.
For the simulator, success is query-specific. It means the implementation computes the declared
target within its stated evidence and numerical guarantees, preserves unsupported regions, and
explains the economic dependence of the result. Whole-company applicability requires coverage
of the actual company’s material contracts, events and reporting basis.
The following worked chapters apply these principles to complete small problems.
Their
purpose is to make the design reconstructible: the reader can follow observations through
competing histories, decisions, financing, journals and outcomes, and see exactly where a
different assumption changes the answer.


PAGE 111
Chapter 13
A complete small company: from
reports to divergent stress outcomes
The earlier examples isolate individual mechanisms. This chapter joins them in one synthetic
company so that every projected balance can be traced to an event. The company is deliberately
simple: one currency, one legal entity, no tax, no dividends, no acquisitions and no other
comprehensive income. These exclusions make the arithmetic inspectable. They do not describe
a complete real-company model. The point that carries over is the need to preserve compatible
economic histories and their joint statement consequences.
13.1
The opening statement
At the beginning of the historical year, the company has cash 40, receivables 30, inventory 50
and property carrying amount 80. Its liabilities are debt 100 and trade payables 40. Equity is
60. All amounts use one currency unit.

The equality is an accounting starting point. It does not identify customers, inventory units,

The company operates during the year and reports sales 120, cost of sales 70, cash operating
expenses 25, interest expense 5 and depreciation 10. With no tax in this teaching model,
profit is 10. The cash-flow information reports customer receipts 130, supplier payments 50,
operating-expense payments 25, interest payments 5 and capital expenditure 20. No debt is
issued or repaid.

PAGE 112
CHAPTER 13.
A COMPANY RECONSTRUCTED AND STRESSED
90
13.2
One compatible event history
One history divides sales into cash sales 80 and credit sales 40. Collections on receivables
total 50: 20 from opening receivables and 30 from current-year credit sales. Customer receipts
therefore total 80 + 50 = 130. Purchases on credit total 60. Supplier payments are 50.
The receivables and inventory bridges are
A1 = 30 + 40 −50 = 20,
(13.1)
I1 = 50 + 60 −70 = 40.
(13.2)
Trade payables become 40 + 60 −50 = 50. Property becomes 80 + 20 −10 = 90. Cash becomes
C1 = 40 + 130 −50 −25 −5 −20 = 70.
(13.3)
Profit of 10 increases equity from 60 to 70. Thus closing assets are 70 + 20 + 40 + 90 = 220,
equal to debt 100, payables 50 and equity 70. Every amount follows from a stated movement.
This is one witness. The reports may not disclose the split of collections between opening
and new receivables, and they do not disclose which customers remain unpaid. Other event
histories can generate the same statements. The model should not turn the chosen witness into
an assertion about what actually happened.
13.3
The journal bundle
Under the illustrative recognition rules, cash sales debit cash and credit revenue by 80. Credit
sales debit receivables and credit revenue by 40. Collections debit cash and credit receivables
by 50. Purchases debit inventory and credit payables by 60. Consumption of inventory debits
cost of sales and credits inventory by 70.
Supplier payments debit payables and credit cash by 50. Operating expenses debit expense
and credit cash by 25. Interest payment debits interest expense and credits cash by 5. Capital
expenditure debits property and credits cash by 20. Depreciation debits expense and credits
accumulated depreciation or the summarized carrying-value account by 10.
Each posting bundle balances. More importantly, the bundles reconcile the economic movements.
The inventory purchase is not expensed a second time when cash is paid. Credit sales are not
recognized again on collection. Capital expenditure is not simultaneously charged as the full
current expense and depreciated. These familiar errors are easy to introduce when separate
forecasting formulas are joined without event identities.
The summarized property account hides gross cost and accumulated depreciation. That is
acceptable for this bounded calculation, but a later disposal or impairment query may require
those components. The representation should retain the option to refine rather than declare
the summary universally sufficient.
13.4
What the reports leave unknown
At the forecast origin, receivables of 20 can be concentrated in one customer or spread among
ten customers owing 2 each. The debt of 100 can be fixed-rate at five per cent or floating-rate
with a historical rate of five per cent. Both alternatives reproduce the historical interest payment
and closing debt.


PAGE 113
CHAPTER 13.
A COMPANY RECONSTRUCTED AND STRESSED
91
Let c indicate concentration: c = 1 for one customer and c = 0 for ten equal customers. Let f
indicate floating debt: f = 1 for floating and f = 0 for fixed. For this exercise we admit all four
combinations because the specified evidence provides no linking restriction. This is an explicitly
declared feasible family, not a claim that real financing structure and customer concentration
are statistically independent. No probabilities are assigned.
Other hidden quantities remain fixed in this first stress calculation. Inventory and property
do not change during the short stress horizon, and there are no new sales or purchases. This
isolates collection and financing exposure. An operating forecast would add production, demand
and replenishment policies rather than assume these balances remain static indefinitely.
13.5
Define the shock precisely
At the start of the stress horizon, payables of 50 must be paid. Next, one largest customer (any
one of the tied customers in the diversified case) defaults and pays nothing on its outstanding
receivable; every other customer pays in full. At the end of the horizon, one full year’s interest is
paid after market rates rise by two percentage points. There is no hedge, cap, floor or principal
maturity during the horizon.
The customer loss is 20 in the concentrated history and 2 in the diversified history. Write
L(c) = 2 + 18c.
(13.4)
Collections are 20 −L(c). Interest is 5 + 2f. After paying suppliers, collecting surviving
receivables and paying interest, cash is
C2(c, f) = 70 −50 + [20 −L(c)] −(5 + 2f)
(13.5)
= 33 −18c −2f.
(13.6)
The formula preserves the joint dependence on concentration and rate type. It is an exact result
for the declared event sequence.
