Receivables and payables both become zero. Inventory remains 40 and property 90. Each
history satisfies
C2 + 40 + 90 = 100 + E2.
(13.7)
The accounting relation is shared, but the economic outcomes differ by 20 across the family. A
single historical witness could conceal that range.
The worst cash outcome is concentrated floating debt.
The best is diversified fixed debt.
These are feasible joint witnesses, not independent marginal extrema assembled from different
worlds. If later evidence rules out one combination, the family and its extrema should update
accordingly.


PAGE 114
CHAPTER 13.
A COMPANY RECONSTRUCTED AND STRESSED
92
13.7
A liquidity threshold changes the question
Suppose a policy requires closing cash at least 14. Three histories meet it; the concentrated
floating history ends at 13. Without probabilities, the correct statement is that the policy is
not robustly feasible across the admitted family. It is wrong to say the probability of failure is
one quarter merely because there are four rows.
The row count depends on representation. The diversified case could be split into ten different
customer-name assignments without changing its economic exposure. Equal weighting would
then change the apparent failure probability. A measure must be attached to economic states
or events with justified weights, not to a convenient enumeration.
If the threshold is a contractual covenant, its consequences require the actual covenant definition
and remedy process. If it is an internal treasury target, a shortfall is a policy deviation rather
than legal default. The same numerical comparison can support different meanings depending
on the source of the threshold.
13.8
A funding response
Suppose a committed facility can supply up to 2 at the final payment date, with no fee or
interest within this short example, and management draws only enough to reach cash 14. The
concentrated floating case draws 1. Its closing cash becomes 14 and debt 101; equity remains
43. The balance sheet still reconciles: 14 + 40 + 90 = 101 + 43.
The draw improves current liquidity but creates a future obligation. It does not reverse the
credit loss or interest expense. A model that raises equity by the draw amount would misclassify
financing. A model that treats the draw as free permanent cash would overstate future capacity.
If the facility is unavailable after the customer default, the response is infeasible. If it settles
after interest is due, it cannot fund that payment without a bridge. If its covenant depends on
equity falling below 44, the worst history may lose eligibility precisely when it needs funding.
These are distinct contract variants that should be modeled as conditional cases.
13.9
A dependence hidden by aggregate data
Suppose a private lender report establishes that the floating loan was granted only when the
receivables were diversified. Then the concentrated floating combination is excluded. The
remaining minimum cash is 15, so the threshold 14 is satisfied by every retained history.
This new conclusion comes from a joint restriction, not from narrower marginal ranges. Con-
centration still can be either zero or one, and debt still can be fixed or floating. Marginal sets
are unchanged. A representation keeping only those marginals cannot express why the worst
combination disappeared.
The example demonstrates the value of constraints over named variables. The restriction can
be written f = 1 ⇒c = 0. A new source should be attached to it, with availability and scope.
If the source is later rejected, the excluded combination returns in the revised family.
13.10
Learning before the payment
Suppose management knows the loan type but the public analyst does not. Management
can plan a precautionary draw in the floating history without using future information. The


PAGE 115
CHAPTER 13.
A COMPANY RECONSTRUCTED AND STRESSED
93
analyst’s forecast must integrate or bound those conditional management actions. Forcing
management to use the analyst’s ignorance would understate its information.
Now suppose a customer deterioration signal arrives before supplier payment. A policy may
delay discretionary capital spending or seek financing in response. If the signal arrives only
after payment, those actions may no longer be available. The date and actor access matter as
much as the signal’s accuracy.
The current stress example has no discretionary spending. Adding one should identify its
benefit, cancellation cost and commitment status. An apparently simple cash-saving policy
can be infeasible if the expenditure is already contractually committed. The event sequence
prevents that distinction from being lost in an annual forecast.
13.11
Historical journal ambiguity beyond contracts
The historical reports also permit different sales and collection timing within the year. One
company may collect most cash early and pay suppliers later; another may pay first and collect
near year-end. Annual cash flow and closing cash can agree while minimum cash differs.
For example, take opening cash 40 and aggregate receipts 130 with payments 100 in total. If
payments of 100 occur before receipts, a company without credit cannot execute the sequence.
If receipts arrive first, it can. The annual closing cash of 70 does not distinguish them. A
feasible historical reconstruction must respect intermediate cash and financing events where
those are within scope.
This does not mean every annual disclosure allows precise daily reconstruction. It means the
model should retain timing uncertainty or state that it cannot answer an intra-year liquidity
question. Inventing a uniform monthly schedule gives a point path whose apparent smoothness
comes from the interpolation assumption.
13.12
Inventory alternatives
Closing inventory value 40 can represent eight units costing 5 or four units costing 10. If a future
order requests six physically interchangeable units, the first history has sufficient stock and the
second does not. If products are not interchangeable, even total unit count is inadequate.
A monetary demand shock should therefore not be converted automatically into units using an
average historical cost. Selling price, cost, product mix and capacity are different quantities. A
future price decline can reduce revenue per unit without changing available physical stock. A
supply shock can reduce units while increasing unit cost.
The present company case holds inventory fixed to isolate credit and rates. Extending it requires
product or cohort state, procurement lead times and sales rules. The aggregate carrying amount
remains a reporting consequence of those states. A richer query justifies refinement of the family
rather than invalidating the earlier bounded calculation.
13.13
Property and replacement cash
Property of 90 after depreciation can comprise young assets with long remaining lives or old
assets near replacement. Equal historical depreciation does not identify next year’s capital
needs. A policy that sets capital expenditure equal to depreciation assumes a replacement
relation.


PAGE 116
CHAPTER 13.
A COMPANY RECONSTRUCTED AND STRESSED
94
Suppose one history has a machine requiring replacement next year for 30 and another can
continue for five years with maintenance 2. Their current carrying amounts may agree under
different acquisition costs and accumulated depreciation. A shock to financing availability has
different operating consequences.
A complete extension should retain capacity and maintenance as well as book values. Replace-
ment can change productivity and costs, so it is not only a cash outflow. The model needs a
production or service relation and a management policy. The accounting bridge alone cannot
supply them.
13.14
Rounding widens the family
The exact amounts used above make arithmetic transparent. If reported receivables 20 are
rounded to the nearest unit, the underlying amount may lie in a reporting interval. Customer
loss and collections then become functions over that interval. A cash threshold at 14 can be
crossed by values that a point reconstruction misses.
Suppose the concentrated floating case’s opening cash is itself rounded from an amount between
69.5 and 70.5 under the declared convention. Its stressed cash lies correspondingly between
12.5 and 13.5 if other terms are exact. A facility of exactly 1 may no longer guarantee reaching
14. The required draw can range from 0.5 to 1.5.
Rounding intervals can also be jointly constrained by a total. Treating each line’s rounding
error independently may admit totals outside the reported balance-sheet interval. The feasible
family should retain the total relation rather than merely widen every number separately.
13.15
An explanation the system should produce
For the worst witness, an explanation should identify the concentrated receivable history, floating
debt, customer default, rate increase, supplier payment and lack of an eligible funding response.
It should show cash 13 and equity 43 with their bridges. It should identify which facts are
observed and which are alternative assumptions.
The explanation should not say that the real company will fail. It should say that a compatible
history produces the stated shortfall under the specified shock and policy. If the threshold is
internal, it should call it a target shortfall. If a covenant consequence is modeled, it should
name that consequence and its premises.
A second explanation can identify the source that would exclude the witness, such as a verified
customer concentration note or fixed-rate contract. This turns the simulation into a guide
for focused investigation. It also makes disagreement productive: a reviewer can challenge a
premise rather than merely distrust the final number.
13.16
What this complete case establishes
The case connects reports, compatible histories, events, journals, cash and equity. It shows
why identical statements do not identify shock response, why joint restrictions matter, why
financing needs a transaction and why timing matters. All numerical relations are small enough
for exact checking.
It does not establish a calibrated corporate model, a universal customer-default process or a
reporting-standard treatment for every event. Those would require additional facts and authority.


PAGE 117
CHAPTER 13.
A COMPANY RECONSTRUCTED AND STRESSED
95
The bounded example is valuable because it exposes the mechanisms without pretending those
wider questions are resolved.
An implementation fixture should store the input observations, the four initial history cases, the
optional dependence restriction, the event sequence, expected journals and final statements. It
should also test the rejected variants: equal branch probabilities without a measure, independent
marginal output selection, a late facility draw and a cash-balancing equity plug. These are
concrete failures of the target semantics.


PAGE 118
Chapter 14
A refinancing crisis: dates, offers,
covenants, and recovery
A year-end debt ratio can look manageable while a particular morning’s payment is impossible.
This chapter follows a synthetic treasury problem through dated cash events. The company
has several potential responses, but each has conditions. The purpose is to show how financing
feasibility differs from choosing a debt amount in an optimization formula. All offers and legal
consequences below are assumptions of the example; they are not estimates or statements of
general law.
14.1
The payment calendar
At the opening of day 1, unrestricted cash is 12. A customer receipt of 18 is due on day 5, wages
of 10 on day 3, an old loan principal of 30 on day 4 and its coupon of 2 on day 4. Inventory
worth 20 in the accounting records can be sold, but only through a specified offer. There is no
automatic overdraft.
If all receipts and payments are netted over five days, closing cash is
12 + 18 −10 −30 −2 = −12.
(14.1)
The total shortfall is 12. But the timing problem is larger: after wages, cash is 2 before the
day-4 debt payment of 32. The company needs 30 by day 4, even though 18 is expected the
next day. A facility that supplies only the final net shortfall cannot bridge the maturity.
The receipt is also uncertain. In history H1 it is contractually due and collected on day 5. In
H2 it is subject to customer acceptance and may be delayed to day 9. Both histories fit an
opening receivable of 18. The financing decision must distinguish the customer’s contractual
obligation, actual collection policy and the analyst’s uncertainty.
14.2
Three offers
Offer A is a bridge facility settling on day 4 before the old payment, with maximum principal 30
and a fee of 1 withheld at draw. Its repayment of principal plus 1 is due on day 6. Offer B is a
term debt issue settling on day 5, priced at 0.9 per unit face, with a fixed fee of 2 and maximum
face 40. Offer C is an inventory sale settling on day 4, paying 15 for inventory carried at 20.
These offers have different cash, accounting and future consequences. A supplies short-term
cash but creates another near maturity. B can supply longer funding but arrives too late to
96


PAGE 119
CHAPTER 14.
A REFINANCING CRISIS IN DATED EVENTS
97
pay the old loan without a bridge or deferral. C supplies timely cash and incurs a disposal loss
under the illustrative carrying-value treatment, while removing inventory needed for operations.
The offers are not interchangeable columns in a financing plug. Their settlement dates and
quantity constraints determine feasible sequences. The company can choose combinations only
if the contracts permit them and management accepts their economic costs.
14.3
Why the bridge alone fails
Drawing maximum bridge principal 30 supplies net cash 29 after the withheld fee. Cash before
old debt payment becomes 2 + 29 = 31, one short of 32. A model that treats principal as
proceeds would declare the payment feasible and miss the fee-induced shortfall.
Required bridge principal to supply net cash 30 would be 31 under this fixed fee, but the cap
is 30. The transaction is infeasible at the needed amount. This conclusion does not require a
default-probability model; it follows from offer terms and dates.
If the company can defer wages by 1 under a separately permitted agreement, the bridge
becomes sufficient for day 4. But that deferral creates another obligation and may have costs. It
must be represented as a negotiated event, not as a spreadsheet reduction of operating expenses.
14.4
The inventory sale plus bridge
Suppose management accepts the inventory sale. After wages, cash 2 plus sale proceeds 15
gives 17. The remaining need for old debt payment is 15. A bridge principal of 16 supplies 15
net after its fee, so day-4 cash reaches 32 and the old obligation is paid exactly.
At day 5, customer receipt 18 arrives in H1. The bridge repayment due day 6 is 17. Paying it
leaves cash 1. The old debt is extinguished, bridge debt is extinguished, inventory is lower by
20, and the company has recognized the illustrative sale loss 5 and bridge cost 2 in total under
the assumed expense convention.
This path solves the payment problem at an economic cost. The inventory sale can reduce future
production or require replacement. A short-horizon model ending on day 6 would miss that
operational consequence unless the terminal state retains the missing inventory and relevant
commitments.
14.5
Delayed collection changes the result
In H2, the customer does not pay until day 9. After the old debt payment, cash is zero. Bridge
repayment 17 is due day 6. The inventory-plus-bridge sequence therefore creates a later shortfall
unless another offer or deferral is available.
The term debt issue B can settle on day 5. To raise net cash 17 after a fixed fee 2 at price 0.9,
required face is
FB = 17 + 2
0.9
= 190
9
≈21.1111.
(14.2)
This is within the face cap 40. If B remains eligible, its proceeds repay the bridge and leave a
new longer-term promise. Customer collection on day 9 then raises cash 18. The company has
transformed a timing mismatch into longer funding at a cost.
The financing path depends on the continued availability of B. A policy that assumes B simply


PAGE 120
CHAPTER 14.
A REFINANCING CRISIS IN DATED EVENTS
98
because it is numerically sufficient confuses an offer with an executed transaction. Eligibility,
acceptance and settlement must be checked at the relevant dates.
14.6
A covenant can remove the rescue
Assume offer B requires inventory carrying amount at least 10 at settlement. The company
initially has exactly 20 and the sale C disposes of all of it. After C, the condition fails. B is
unavailable in the very path that needed it to refinance the bridge.
A modular simulator can miss this interaction if the asset-sale module reports cash but does not
update the collateral or eligibility state used by financing. The cash schedule appears feasible
until day 6, then fails. The root error is a missing cross-module dependency, not arithmetic.
If C can sell only half the inventory for 7.5, inventory remains 10 and B stays eligible. After
wages and partial sale, cash is 9.5. To reach 32 requires net bridge 22.5, so principal 23.5 is
within cap. Bridge repayment is 24.5. In H1, receipt 18 leaves a day-6 need of 6.5; in H2 it
leaves a need of 24.5. B can supply either need subject to its own cap.
14.7
Face caps and net funding
Under B, maximum face 40 supplies gross proceeds 36 and net cash 34 after the fee. In H2 the
need 24.5 is feasible. Required face is (24.5 + 2)/0.9 = 265/9, about 29.4444. In H1 the need
6.5 requires face 85/9, about 9.4444.
If B’s price falls to 0.6 after a market shock, maximum net proceeds become 40(0.6) −2 = 22.
The H2 need 24.5 is no longer feasible. H1 remains feasible. Thus identical opening statements
and the same price shock produce different financing outcomes because collection timing differs.
A sensitivity table should vary offer price and collection history jointly. Reporting only the
average required face can hide the infeasible H2 path. A midpoint collection date may produce
a third path that neither contract history permits.
14.8
A symbolic feasibility boundary
Let q be B’s price per unit face and K the net funding need on day 6. With fixed fee 2 and cap
40, feasibility requires
40q −2 ≥K
⇐⇒
q ≥K + 2
40
.
(14.3)
For H1 after partial inventory sale, K = 6.5, so the threshold is 8.5/40 = 0.2125. For H2,
K = 24.5, so it is 26.5/40 = 0.6625. These thresholds are exact for the offer structure.
The price may itself depend on face or the asset sale. If so, the linear threshold no longer applies
without modification. The symbolic result should carry its premises. A new price model is a
new financing query, not merely a different number substituted into an unchanged guarantee.
14.9
Who knows the collection history
Suppose management knows whether customer acceptance has occurred, while the public analyst
sees only the receivable total. Management can condition its financing plan on H1 versus H2.
The analyst should evaluate that informed policy across the two histories.


PAGE 121
CHAPTER 14.
A REFINANCING CRISIS IN DATED EVENTS
99
If management does not know the eventual delay, it must choose a plan before observing
collection. A robust plan must remain feasible in both histories, or specify a contingent action
after the day-5 observation. A term issue requested only after nonreceipt may settle too late if
the offer has a notice period. The observation date and action lead time determine the policy’s
feasibility.
A simulator that waits to see H1 or H2 and then retroactively chooses the best day-1 plan gives
management foresight. A correct tree branches at the observation time and shares decisions
before that branch. A symbolic policy representation enforces the same restriction through its
allowed inputs.
14.10
Default after a failed refinancing
Suppose H2 occurs, B is unavailable and bridge repayment 17 cannot be made on day 6. Under
a stipulated contract, a two-day cure period begins. The day-9 customer receipt arrives after the
cure deadline. Whether the lender can accelerate, waive or restructure is specified by additional
terms.
The economic state on day 6 contains unpaid principal and fee or interest according to the
contract. It does not set debt to zero. The company may continue operations during cure. If
a waiver is granted on day 7 for fee 0.5 and repayment on day 9, the receipt can resolve the
arrears. If not, enforcement may proceed.
These branches are not numerical solver failures. They are economic transitions. Conversely,
inability to solve a pricing equation for B is not itself the bridge default event. The simulator
should preserve the distinction even if both ultimately leave the funding need unresolved.
14.11
A recovery waterfall
Assume enforcement yields distributable assets of 25 after costs. The bridge has senior claim
17 and another unsecured creditor has claim 20. Under the example’s strict priority rule, the
bridge receives 17 and the unsecured creditor 8. Total recovery equals available assets 25.
If the bridge is secured only on an asset that realizes 12 and has no further recourse under
the assumed contract, its recovery may instead be 12. The remaining distribution depends
on ownership and priority of the other assets. A single aggregate recovery percentage cannot
represent both cases without additional assumptions.
If recovery occurs one year later, creditor valuation discounts it from that date. The accounting
treatment of the debtor’s unresolved obligation and eventual extinguishment requires its own
rules. Cash recovery to creditors and book debt removal at the debtor need not occur at the
same instant.
14.12
Why aggregate net debt fails here
At opening, net debt may be calculated as debt less cash. That statistic does not contain the
day-3 wage date, day-4 maturity, day-5 receipt or day-6 bridge repayment. It cannot decide
feasibility of the sequence.
After the partial sale and term refinancing, net debt may improve when the customer finally
pays. Yet the company may have lost productive inventory and paid substantial financing costs.


PAGE 122
CHAPTER 14.
A REFINANCING CRISIS IN DATED EVENTS
100
A net-debt chart alone omits these consequences. A useful result reports cash timing, remaining
obligations, inventory capacity and owner value separately.
A compressed state can still be possible. For this bounded problem it must retain the ordered
obligation calendar, collection state, inventory eligibility amount and offer terms. The full
historical journal need not be replayed at every step, but its economically relevant consequences
must survive.
14.13
A finite policy comparison
The admissible choices are no sale, full sale or half sale, combined with feasible bridge draws
and term issuance. Before optimizing, enumerate this small policy set and apply the same
timing and eligibility rules to each history. Reject actions exceeding caps or settling too late.
For each feasible policy, record minimum cash, final cash, fees, remaining debt and inventory.
A policy minimizing fees may differ from one preserving inventory or maximizing worst-case
liquidity. There is no single best policy until the objective is stated.
This finite enumeration is a reference calculation for later optimization. A continuous optimizer
should reproduce its results when restricted to the same choices. If it finds a better result,
inspect whether it used fractional sale rights, ignored notice periods or assumed a different offer.
Apparent improvement can be a target mismatch.
14.14
Accounting consequences of the sequence
The old loan repayment reduces cash and the old obligation. Bridge issuance increases cash by
net proceeds and creates the specified liability and fee treatment. Inventory disposal removes
the asset and records proceeds and loss under the illustrative basis. Term issuance creates a
new obligation with proceeds distinct from face.
The exact treatment of debt discounts and transaction costs requires the accounting basis. A
cash schedule can be checked without prematurely choosing that treatment. The accounting
layer should then reconcile carrying amounts separately from contractual face.
A journal test should ensure that the old debt is repaid once, the bridge is extinguished only
when settled or legally discharged, and the term debt remains after bridge repayment. Netting
all financing flows into one closing debt amount would make these checks impossible.
14.15
Sensitivity that preserves the same company
Vary B’s price while holding the collection history, asset sale and contractual caps fixed. This
produces a meaningful price sensitivity for each policy. Vary the collection date while retaining
the same receivable identity and amount. This produces a timing sensitivity.
Changing both at once can be a joint stress, but it should be named as such. Comparing a
high-price H1 case with a low-price H2 case and attributing the entire difference to price would
confound two mechanisms. Paired scenario comparisons should preserve the dimensions not
intended to change.
The same discipline applies to recovery. A lower recovery value can affect price at issuance if
anticipated, but a surprise enforcement loss after issuance is a different information path. The
model should specify whether creditors knew the recovery change when making the offer.


PAGE 123
CHAPTER 14.
A REFINANCING CRISIS IN DATED EVENTS
101
14.16
A practical implementation slice
This case suggests a bounded event scheduler with exact rational amounts and ordered times-
tamps.
It needs offer admission, net-proceeds calculation, inventory eligibility, collection
observation, policy action and arrears transitions. It does not need a neural network to expose
the important errors.
The fixture should include both successful and refused paths, with reasons. The refused bridge-
only path fails by one due to the fee. The full-sale H2 path loses term eligibility. The half-sale
H2 path fails when price falls below 0.6625. The cure-period variant distinguishes late collection
from timely cure.
These cases provide a concrete basis for extending the repository’s financing methods. They also
show what remains outside a small implementation: actual market offers, legal enforceability,
operational cost of inventory sale and empirically supported management preferences. The
model can explain those dependencies without pretending to have resolved them.
14.17
The price must anticipate who chooses after conversion
The refinancing calendar tells us when an offer can settle. A second issue arises when the
security changes the person who chooses later company policy. A price based on the old owner’s
future choice can be inconsistent with a conversion that transfers control. The following finite
example isolates that dependency.
There are three dates: financing on 1 January 2027, a signal and policy choice on 1 January
2028, and terminal distributions on 1 January 2029. Investment costs 60 at the first date.
Existing senior face is 40 and new convertible face is 80. The gross discount factor is 21/20
in each of the two intervals. All cash recoveries occur at the terminal date. The equal signal
probabilities and the conditional probabilities below are a supplied hypothetical pricing law,
also used to evaluate expected cash utility.
Under the debt signal, the new claim remains debt and the old owner chooses policy. Safe
policy produces terminal estate 100. Risky policy produces 20 or 220 with equal probabilities.
Senior debt is paid first, then the new claim up to its face, and old equity receives the residual.
The old owner consequently prefers risky expected cash 50 to safe cash zero. The new creditor’s
expected cash under that choice is 40.
Under the conversion signal, the new claim is extinguished and its holder receives 120 shares
alongside 40 old shares, as in Section 7.16. The new holder now controls policy. Safe estate is
again 100; risky estate is 20 or 140 with equal probabilities. After senior priority, safe policy
gives the new holder 3(100 −40)/4 = 45. Risky policy gives 75/2, so the controlling holder
chooses safe.
To make the control distinction consequential, assume the old owner receives a noncash private
benefit worth 4 terminal utility units when risky policy is chosen in this conversion branch.
Old-owner risky cash has expectation 25/2, giving utility 25/2 + 4 = 33/2, compared with safe
utility 15. Thus a stale old-controller assumption would select risky policy. The benefit is
deliberately hypothetical and never enters estate, creditor recovery or accounting equity. At
zero private benefit the old owner also prefers safe policy, which checks the mechanism’s limiting
case.
The consistent continuation is therefore risky under the debt signal and safe after conversion.
Let P denote date-zero proceeds paid by the new holder. In this selected competitive-quote


PAGE 124
CHAPTER 14.
A REFINANCING CRISIS IN DATED EVENTS
102
comparison, proceeds equal the discounted expected cash the holder will receive:
P =

(14.4)
The two discount intervals matter: the signal date is not a payment date. The second term
is the cash the old owner must contribute at financing. Available cash 20 is insufficient, while
cash 22 is sufficient.
Affordability and willingness to invest are separate conditions. Under this continuation, the old
owner’s expected terminal cash is (50 + 15)/2. Its date-zero value is 13000/441. Subtracting
the contribution gives 3540/441 > 0, so participation exceeds the assumed outside utility zero.
That positive value does not supply the missing liquid funds in the cash-20 case.
If old control were incorrectly retained after conversion, the new holder’s conversion-branch
cash would instead be 75/2. The corresponding quote would be 15500/441, requiring owner
cash 10960/441, more than 22. Charging the correct-control quote while executing that stale-
control policy leaves the lender short by 1500/441. The discrepancy is a failure to keep rights,
continuation and price consistent. It does not establish that conversion is economically superior.
Backward induction agrees with a separate enumeration of all four pure strategies mapping the
observed signal to a policy, including every local deviation. A tie variant retains both optimal
policies. Equal votes without a supplied governance rule remain unresolved. Policies cannot
depend on the terminal realization, which is not known when the choice is made.
The owner’s financing contribution is sunk by the policy date, so the continuation ranking
in this example is independent of the posted price. This is a finite consistency calculation,
not a general fixed-point or market-clearing result. The typed conversion example starts at a
separately supplied fiscal opening. The following sections connect the dated funding exchange
and identify the source, measurement and control-history conditions needed before issuance,
conversion and final liquidation can form one accounting history.
14.18
Funding the project does not yet determine its accounts
The price calculation leaves two different questions. Can the parties deliver the promised
cash in the required order? If they can, which amounts should their financial statements
recognise between issue and conversion? The first question has a complete answer under the
supplied synthetic cash balances. The second still depends on the contract, reporting basis and
measurement evidence. Keeping the questions separate prevents a feasible transaction from
silently becoming an unsupported accounting history.
At the financing date the new holder pays 17000/441 to the company and the old owner
contributes 9460/441. Their receipts sum exactly to the project price 60. The company then
pays 60 to the seller. The transfer records identify both parties, the date and the same amount
in the two legal books. In this example the company starts with no available cash for the
purchase, so both receipts must precede payment to the seller. There are six possible orders of
these three transfers. The two orders with the seller paid last succeed; the other four fail at the
first payment that exceeds available company cash. Positive final net cash is not a substitute
for an affordable cash prefix.
The four-party reference retains the company, old owner, new holder and seller books separately.
It checks two account charts and both owner cash 22 and cash exactly equal to the required
contribution. A refused later transfer preserves the accepted prefix. Counterparty cost-basis
entries are explicit synthetic premises. They demonstrate reciprocal transfers and funding


PAGE 125
CHAPTER 14.
A REFINANCING CRISIS IN DATED EVENTS
103
feasibility, not the appropriate measurement of a real convertible instrument or the seller’s tax
and profit consequences.
The limitation becomes visible if the 5% pricing discount is reused as an accounting yield.
Suppose one incorrectly treats the face 80 claim as a plain two-year zero-coupon liability and
discounts its entire face at that rate. The resulting liability and residual allocation would be
