require their own evidence.
For the conventional issuer compound-instrument case, the inspected AASB 132 text measures
the comparable liability first and assigns the residual to equity. Its application guidance refers
to instruments of comparable credit status, substantially the same cash flows and terms, without
the conversion option (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2022d, paragraphs 31–32 and
AG31). Those conditions are not established by the economic quote. The typed conversion
demonstration instead starts with a supplied carrying amount C. Its balanced transfer from
liability to equity remains conditional until classification, intervening measurement and the
conversion treatment are supported.
14.19
Issuer and holder classify the same contract differently
The same contract is a claim issued by the company and an investment held by the creditor.
Those perspectives need not have matching classification components. The source research
therefore begins with the complete settlement terms: who can affect the conversion signal,
whether either party can elect cash settlement, the currency and share terms, any redemption
provisions, and agreements involving other members of a group. The example supplies none of
these facts merely by stating that 120 shares will be issued.
Under the inspected AASB 132 provisions, fixed-for-fixed analysis is subject to the definitions
and exceptions for qualifying own-equity instruments. A genuine contingent liability settlement
beyond both parties’ control raises the separate conditions in paragraph 25. A derivative with
settlement choices has its own rule in paragraph 26. The non-derivative compound-instrument
assessment in paragraphs 28–32 cannot be inferred from a fixed share count alone (Australian
Accounting Standards Board, 2022d, paragraphs 15–16, 22–26 and 28–32). These passages
identify the questions to answer; they do not classify the underspecified fixture.
The holder’s analysis is different. For an in-scope financial-asset host, AASB 9 assesses the
hybrid as a whole. Its fixed-number convertible-bond example links the return to issuer equity
and fails the solely-principal-and- interest condition (Australian Accounting Standards Board,
2026i, paragraphs 4.3.2 and B4.1.14, Instrument F). Issuer liability/equity components therefore
cannot simply be copied into the holder’s asset book. Initial fair value, transaction costs,
subsequent measurement and relevant exceptions remain separate dependencies. Likewise,
AASB 132 AG32 expressly addresses conversion at maturity; it alone does not justify the
fixture’s 2028 conversion before its 2029 terminal date.
The resulting holder interest also raises a reporting-perimeter question. Section 26.16 follows the
separate-statement, consolidation and business-acquisition alternatives, including the possibility
of control before legal conversion. Its repaired dependency analysis preserves uncertain control


PAGE 126
CHAPTER 14.
A REFINANCING CRISIS IN DATED EVENTS
104
histories rather than choosing whichever journal balances. The retained source compilations
do not yet establish the complete accounting authority for the fixture’s 2027 issue and 2028
conversion. Classification, measurement, control history and applicable edition therefore remain
linked questions with distinct evidence requirements.
14.20
Evidence over an interval is stronger than two snapshots
To support uninterrupted control, an analyst needs evidence covering the intervening period. An
empty event list supplies no such evidence. The selected interval reference requires an explicit
assertion that a control status holds throughout a specified half-open interval, together with its
subject, reporting basis, source identity and availability to each actor. This is a representation
of an asserted coverage premise. It does not certify that the assertion is true or that the source
collection is complete.
For example, let one source assert no control from 30 December to 31 December, and another
assert control from 31 December to 1 January. A half-open interval includes its starting instant
and excludes its ending instant, so the shared boundary is assigned once. If both assertions are
eligible for the selected actor view, they conditionally support a gain at that boundary. If the
second source is missing, the second interval remains unresolved. If it conflicts with another
eligible assertion, the conflict remains visible rather than being resolved by whichever record
was loaded last.
Effective time and information time do different jobs. A correction can describe control during
December yet become available to an analyst in January and to a manager only in February.
The analyst’s January view can use it; the manager’s January view cannot. A later available
revision replaces the corresponding earlier assertion only within its declared subject and interval.
Unknown actor availability remains unresolved. The selected profile refuses partial-interval
replacement, whose meaning would require an additional rule.
The implementation reuses the exact fixed-offset timestamp parser and partitions the query at
every eligible interval boundary. It retains every covered, uncovered, unknown or conflicting
piece. Equivalent timestamp offsets denote the same instant, and adding a future correction
leaves an earlier view’s control conclusions unchanged. The checked cases include all two-cell
combinations of control, no control and unknown control, as well as revision conflicts, coverage
gaps, missing ancestry and corrupted source identities.
This gives the simulator a precise way to carry unresolved control history alongside otherwise
balanced company books. It does not yet connect an admitted real history to acquisition
measurement, changing group membership or complete financial statements. The next financial
composition must supply those methods and their dated authority while preserving the source
and actor-view distinctions. Neither a successful funding transfer nor a complete conditional
interval partition can supply the missing accounting evidence.


PAGE 127
Chapter 15
A recapitalization: cash raised, rights
transferred, and incentives changed
A financing plan can save a company while reducing the value held by its existing owners. It
can also dilute their percentage without reducing their value. Distinguishing these outcomes
requires a security-level calculation. This chapter follows a synthetic recapitalization with
ordinary shares, a preference offer and a convertible claim. The terms are stipulated so the
arithmetic can be reconstructed. They illustrate mechanisms rather than legal or valuation
conclusions for a real issuer.
15.1
The company before financing
There are 100 ordinary shares. Existing operating assets have a stipulated economic value of
600, cash is 20 and a senior debt payment of 80 is immediately due. For the first comparison,
assume asset value is unchanged by financing and there are no other claims. Before paying debt,
residual economic equity value is 600 + 20 −80 = 540, or 5.4 per ordinary share.
The company nevertheless cannot pay 80 from cash 20. It needs 60 of additional cash. Positive
economic equity value does not eliminate this liquidity problem. Selling assets, borrowing, issuing
equity or negotiating deferral are different possible responses. We first study an ordinary-share
offer.
Book assets may have a different carrying amount. Nothing in the calculation requires book
equity to equal 540. If the model uses book equity to set a subscription price of 5.4, it would be
making a separate valuation assumption. Here 540 is a supplied economic valuation used only
for the illustrative allocation.
15.2
A fairly priced ordinary issue
Suppose investors subscribe at 5.4 with no fees, and the company raises exactly 60. New
shares are 60/5.4 = 100/9, about 11.1111. Cash becomes 80 and is paid to the senior creditor.
Remaining assets are 600, there is no debt, and shares total 1000/9.
Post-transaction value per share is
600
1000/9 = 5.4.
(15.1)
Existing holders retain value 540, and new holders own value 60. Their percentages change, but
105


PAGE 128
CHAPTER 15.
A RECAPITALIZATION AND ITS OWNERS
106
no value is transferred under these assumptions. The creditor receives its contractual 80. The
cash injection solves liquidity without creating extra value by itself.
This result relies on the supplied fair price, homogeneous rights, no costs and unchanged
operating value. It is a useful benchmark because deviations can be attributed to specific
mechanisms. It is not a prediction that a distressed company can always issue at fair value.
15.3
A discounted issue
Now suppose the offer price is 3, with the same 60 cash target and no fees. Twenty shares are
issued. After paying debt, value remains 600 but shares total 120. Each share is worth 5 under
the stipulated valuation. Existing holders own 500, a loss of 40 relative to the benchmark; new
investors paid 60 for claims worth 100.
The transfer is not an additional operating loss. It is an allocation effect of selling claims below
the assumed post-transaction value. A journal recording cash and equity does not reveal it.
The ownership register and valuation assumptions are needed.
If investors’ required price of 3 reflects information that operating assets are worth less than 600,
the benchmark valuation is wrong. The calculation should not accuse the offer of unfairness
without validating the valuation premise. Scenario analysis can show transfers conditional on
alternative asset values and prices.
15.4
Fees change both quantity and value
Add a fixed cash issuance fee of 6. To net 60, the company must raise 66. At price 3, it issues
22 shares. After fees and debt payment, operating assets remain 600, cash is zero and shares
total 122. Existing owners’ value is 600(100/122), about 491.8033.
The fee destroys 6 of total value relative to an otherwise identical financing if it buys no
additional benefit in this model. The discounted price transfers value between old and new
owners. These effects should be separated. Calling both dilution obscures which mechanism
could be changed by renegotiation or a different financing channel.
If the fee is paid later, current funding need differs and a payable remains. If it is financed
by issuing additional shares to an intermediary, share count changes without the same cash
payment. The offer must state the form and timing of compensation.
15.5
An authorization constraint
Suppose only fifteen new ordinary shares may be issued under the admitted authorization. At
price 3 and fee 6, maximum net cash is 39. Existing cash 20 then supplies only 59 toward debt
80, leaving a shortfall of 21. The ordinary issue cannot solve the maturity under these terms.
At price p, feasibility requires 15p −6 ≥60, so p ≥4.4. This is an exact offer threshold. It does
not establish that investors will pay 4.4. A market model or a new offer is needed to answer
that question.
An optimizer that increases shares beyond fifteen has changed the feasible set. An accounting
routine that credits capital for the missing 21 without issuing a security has invented financing.
A correct result records the shortfall and considers other admitted actions.


PAGE 129
CHAPTER 15.
A RECAPITALIZATION AND ITS OWNERS
107
15.6
A rights offer
Suppose existing holders have a right to subscribe proportionally at the discounted price. If
every holder subscribes in proportion, their relative ownership remains unchanged, but they
contribute new cash. The issue can still have fees and execution risk.
A holder who does not subscribe may sell the right if trading is permitted and a buyer exists.
The value of the right depends on issue terms and market value. A model that treats all existing
holders as equally able and willing to contribute assumes away liquidity and participation
differences.
For AccDSL, a rights offer needs entitlement, subscription, transfer and expiry events. Unexer-
cised rights may lapse or be allocated under a backstop arrangement. The company receives
cash only from settled subscriptions or the backstop, not from the mere existence of rights.
Historical statements may not reveal the eventual participation pattern.
15.7
A preference alternative
Consider instead an investor providing 60 for a nonparticipating preference claim. At a future
exit, the investor may take 90 before ordinary shareholders or convert into 20 per cent of
ordinary equity. Assume no senior debt remains, no interim dividends, no other rights and
distributable value V . The investor receives
P(V ) = max{min(90, V ), 0.2V }.
(15.2)
Ordinary holders receive V −P(V ). The first term respects limited available value: a preference
of 90 cannot pay 90 if only 50 exists. Conversion dominates the full preference when 0.2V > 90,
or V > 450.
For V = 50, the investor takes 50 and ordinary holders receive zero. For V = 300, the investor
takes 90 and ordinary holders receive 210. For V = 600, conversion pays 120 and ordinary
holders receive 480. The same 60 of initial funding creates very different allocation across
outcomes from a simple ordinary issue.
15.8
Participation changes the waterfall
A participating preference can take a preference amount and then share in remaining value.
Suppose the investor receives up to 90 first and then 20 per cent of the residual, with no cap.
Its payoff is
Ppart(V ) = min(90, V ) + 0.2 max(V −90, 0).
(15.3)
At V = 300, this gives 90 + 42 = 132, compared with 90 for the nonparticipating offer. At
V = 600, it gives 192, compared with 120 under conversion in the earlier contract.
These are different securities, not two calculation methods for one security. A parser that
extracts only the word preference and the amount 90 will miss the distinction. The contract
admission model must preserve participation, conversion and caps where they affect the query.
If a participation cap applies, another region boundary appears. The engine can represent the
payoff as a piecewise function and split only where the active right changes. A sample of a few
exit values is useful for illustration but not a complete characterization of the payoff.


PAGE 130
CHAPTER 15.
A RECAPITALIZATION AND ITS OWNERS
108
15.9
Value conservation in each state
For every exit value, distributions to all claimants must not exceed distributable assets under
the assumed waterfall. In the nonparticipating example, P(V ) + [V −P(V )] = V exactly. This
is a conservation check independent of whether the offer is fairly priced.
A common implementation error pays the preference and also allocates 20 per cent of total
value as though conversion were simultaneous. That double counts mutually exclusive rights.
Another pays 90 when V < 90, creating cash. Both can be detected with low-value boundary
cases.
Fair pricing requires discounting and a distribution or valuation operator over future payoffs.
The waterfall itself supplies state-contingent claims, not their current price. An investor’s
willingness to pay 60 can be taken as a declared offer without claiming that a model has proved
its fairness.
15.10
A convertible debt alternative
Suppose an existing note has face 80 due at exit and can convert into sixteen ordinary shares,
with no other debt. If conversion is optional for the holder and the company has 100 ordinary
shares before conversion, conversion gives a fraction 16/116 of total equity after conversion.
The holder compares that claim with the debt payoff under the specified default and priority
rules.
If distributable asset value is high enough that debt is paid in full, conversion value is 16V /116.
It exceeds 80 when V > 580. Below that threshold, the holder may prefer debt. At low asset
values, debt recovery is limited by available assets and seniority. The conversion threshold can
change if other securities exist.
A mistaken calculation using 16V /100 ignores the new shares in the denominator and gives
a threshold of 500. That is a dilution error. A second mistake pays debt 80 and also issues
sixteen shares. The conversion event must extinguish the specified debt claim if that is what
the contract says.
15.11
Cash settlement and issuer options
Some instruments permit cash settlement of conversion value or allow the issuer to choose
a settlement form. That changes liquidity and ownership. A model that always assumes
physical shares may understate cash need; one that always assumes cash may overstate dilution
avoidance.
The choice belongs to the contract and the authorized actor. If the issuer can choose, its policy
must consider cash capacity and economic cost. If the holder chooses, management cannot
select the favorable form merely to balance the forecast.
An unknown settlement clause should produce conditional outcomes. The system can show a
physical-settlement branch and a cash-settlement branch with their premises. It should seek the
clause when the distinction changes a liquidity conclusion. This is a high-value source-research
target.


PAGE 131
CHAPTER 15.
A RECAPITALIZATION AND ITS OWNERS
109
15.12
Control and approvals
A recapitalization can shift voting control even when economic ownership changes modestly. A
preference investor may receive veto rights or board appointment rights. Those rights can affect
future financing, dividends and asset sales. Treating them as irrelevant because they have no
immediate journal amount can distort later policies.
The model need not simulate every governance detail for every query. It should retain material
rights and declare when behavior assumes their exercise or nonexercise. If a future equity issue
requires investor consent, the feasible action set must include that condition.
A public-data analyst may not know whether consent will be given. This is future behavioral
uncertainty, distinct from uncertainty about whether the consent right exists. Combining both
into one unspecified branch makes it harder to identify useful evidence or policy assumptions.
15.13
Incentives after financing
Suppose ordinary holders choose between a safe project with exit value 300 and a risky project
yielding 100 or 600 with equal probability. Under the nonparticipating preference, ordinary
payoff from the safe project is 210. Under the risky project, ordinary payoffs are 10 and 480,
with mean 245.
Total expected exit value of the risky project is 350, above 300, so both total value and ordinary
value favor it in this particular example. Change the low outcome to zero and high outcome to
500. Total expected value is 250, below 300, while ordinary expected payoff is [0 + 400]/2 = 200,
also below 210. The rankings still agree. These examples should not be used to claim a general
conflict.
To exhibit a conflict, consider a senior fixed claim of 250 and ordinary residual value. A safe
project with value 300 gives ordinary holders 50. A risky project with values zero and 500
equally likely has total expected value 250 but ordinary expected payoff 125. Ordinary holders
prefer the risky project while total expected value is lower. The contract allocation creates the
divergence under the stated objective and probabilities.
15.14
Why the actor objective matters
A policy maximizing total enterprise value selects the safe project in the final example. A policy
maximizing ordinary equity selects the risky one. A lender pricing debt must anticipate the
policy actually modeled. Pricing under the safe policy and then letting shareholders choose the
risky one is inconsistent.
Covenants or control rights may constrain the choice. Their presence changes the feasible set
and possibly the price offered initially. This joins the security register to behavioral modeling.
The register is not merely a reporting table; it determines who receives gains and losses and
who can authorize actions.
The example is a local derivation of incentive conflict, not a calibrated prediction. It gives a
discriminating fixture for a structural solver: the actor objective, priority and feasible actions
should reproduce the stated ranking. A solver returning the same policy for both objectives
without a binding constraint is suspect.


PAGE 132
CHAPTER 15.
A RECAPITALIZATION AND ITS OWNERS
110
15.15
Book entries and economic allocation
The cash issue creates cash and accounting equity under the applicable treatment. The preference
or convertible may require classification and component allocation that cannot be inferred from
the simplified payoff alone. Those accounting decisions need the standard, jurisdiction and
complete terms.
Economic valuation can proceed conditionally on a specified payoff while accounting classification
remains unresolved. Conversely, a classified instrument can still have uncertain market price.
The system should allow these statuses to coexist. Forcing both layers into one known or
unknown flag loses useful work.
A book-equity bridge should reconcile issue proceeds, fees under the chosen treatment, retained
earnings, distributions and other movements. A separate ownership bridge reconciles units and
rights. A valuation bridge explains changes in economic claims. The three bridges communicate
but should not be substituted for one another.
15.16
Historical ambiguity in a capital movement
Suppose a filing reports net cash from equity transactions of 60 and an increase of twenty
ordinary shares. This could be a simple issue at 3 with no costs. It could also be an issue of
thirty shares at 4 and repurchase of ten at 6, again netting to 60 and twenty shares. The closing
share count and net cash agree.
The histories differ in gross funding, repurchase behavior and prices. If future policy extrapolates
issuance discounts or repurchase commitments, the distinction matters. A note disclosing gross
proceeds and repurchases can narrow the family. Without it, a single implied price of 3 is not
established as an actual transaction price.
This example parallels the receivables and debt bridges. Net movements constrain sums but do
not identify gross events. The security register should therefore permit unresolved transaction
families while preserving observed opening and closing counts.
15.17
An end-to-end fixture
A useful fixture starts with the 100-share company, the debt maturity and the cash need.
It evaluates ordinary issuance at fair and discounted prices, fixed fees, authorization limits,
preference rights and conversion. Each transaction updates cash, liabilities, shares and rights
before producing statements.
Expected failures include using book value as an unstated subscription price, ignoring the
authorization cap, counting conversion shares against the old denominator, paying mutually
exclusive preference and conversion rights together, and treating an unsigned offer as settled
cash. Each failure has a specific input that exposes it.
The fixture should also retain unresolved accounting classification as a legitimate result where
full terms are absent. A financing calculation can be mechanically complete while a statement
presentation remains conditional.
That distinction is preferable to inventing a universal
accounting rule to make the example look finished.


PAGE 133
CHAPTER 15.
A RECAPITALIZATION AND ITS OWNERS
111
15.18
What the case adds to simulation design
Equity funding is a transaction among actors with claims, rights and constraints. Its effect
cannot be reduced to an external-equity cost scalar when ownership and future decisions matter.
Nor can share count alone establish value transfer. Prices, fees, rights and valuation premises
jointly determine the result.
The proposed AccDSL design therefore keeps a dated security register, offer and settlement
states, class-specific payoff rules and actor objectives. It can use a simple homogeneous-share
profile when appropriate, but retains the boundary that tells a user when richer terms are
required. This makes dilution and financing consequences explicit without pretending that
every security must always be simulated at maximum detail.


PAGE 134
Chapter 16
A decision before and after
disclosure: an exact learning example
Learning matters only through a specified relation between hidden facts, observations and
decisions. This chapter constructs that relation explicitly. The example is finite, so every policy
and probability can be checked by enumeration. It demonstrates why two companies with
equal current statements can require different information states, and why preserving only
unconditional future cash distributions is insufficient for adaptive decisions.
16.1
Two hidden exposures
A company must choose a hedge before a future settlement. Its exposure type is either A or B.
Under type A, hedge A avoids a loss of 10 while hedge B avoids none. Under type B, hedge B
avoids a loss of 10 while hedge A avoids none. Each hedge costs 2. Doing nothing costs nothing
and leaves the loss 10.
For the first calculation, assume type A and B each have probability one half. This probability
is a supplied teaching assumption. The company has enough cash to execute either hedge and
can choose at most one. Current financial statements do not distinguish the types.
Net loss after the correct hedge is 2, after the wrong hedge is 12, and after no hedge is 10.
Without further information, either hedge has expected loss 7, so a risk-neutral expected-loss
policy chooses either hedge rather than doing nothing. Symmetry gives two equally optimal
actions. The model should not invent a preference between them.
16.2
A noisy signal
Before choosing, the actor can receive a signal labeled A or B. Let the signal be correct with
probability q, symmetrically across types, with q ≥1/2. Bayes’ rule with equal priors gives
posterior probability q for the signaled type. Following the signal yields expected loss
Lsignal(q) = 2 + 10(1 −q) = 12 −10q.
(16.1)
The first term is hedge cost. The second is the loss when the signal is wrong. At q = 0.9,
expected loss is 3; at q = 0.5, it is 7, matching no informative signal.
The gross value of the signal relative to the best uninformed hedge is 7−(12−10q) = 10q −5. A
signal costing k is worthwhile under this criterion when k < 10q −5. At q = 0.9, the maximum
112


PAGE 135
CHAPTER 16.
A DECISION BEFORE AND AFTER DISCLOSURE
113
willingness to pay is 4. This calculation requires the signal to arrive before the action and the
actor to be able to respond.
16.3
A complete joint probability table
For q = 0.9, the four type-signal combinations have probabilities
Type
Signal
Probability
Loss following signal
A
A
0.45
2
A
B
0.05
12
B
A
0.05
12
B
B
0.45
2
The expected loss is 0.9(2)+0.1(12) = 3. The signal is marginally A or B with equal probability.
Type is also marginally A or B with equal probability. The information lies in their dependence.
If a compression retains these two marginals but makes them independent, it changes each joint
cell to 0.25. Following the signal then has expected loss 7. The compressed model has destroyed
the signal’s information while preserving both marginal distributions. This is why the joint
successor-observation law appears in the exact aggregation condition.
16.4
Two observation technologies with equal current reports
Consider company state U, which will receive the informative signal with q = 0.9, and state V,
which will receive an uninformative signal with q = 0.5. Their current cash, debt and income
are identical. Their hidden type priors are identical. Before signal arrival, a fixed hedge has the
same expected loss 7 in both.
An adaptive policy following the signal has expected loss 3 in U and 7 in V. Merging U and V
because current reports and fixed-action values agree loses a decision-relevant difference. The
future observation technology is part of state for this question.
This finite example gives an accounting-oriented counterpart to the policy-versus-action-sequence
distinction in Givan et al. (2003, Section 3.1). The numerical construction is local. The source
supplies the general warning that policies react to intermediate information and therefore require
a stronger equivalence than fixed sequences.
16.5
What happens when the signal arrives late
Suppose the hedge must be committed at date 1 and the signal arrives at date 2. The informative
technology no longer improves that hedge decision. A policy that follows the date-2 signal but
executes the hedge at date 1 is infeasible.
The expected-loss value returns to 7 for the best uninformed hedge, even though the analyst
eventually learns much more about the type. Information has retrospective explanatory value
but no value for this already committed action. A simulation must represent action deadlines
and settlement lead times, not only observation dates.
If the contract permits cancellation or adjustment at date 2 for cost c, some signal value can
return. The calculation then compares adjustment benefits with c and available liquidity. The
ability to revise is a contract term or policy permission, not a default property of every action.


PAGE 136
CHAPTER 16.
A DECISION BEFORE AND AFTER DISCLOSURE
114
16.6
A cancellation calculation
Suppose hedge A is chosen initially. After receiving the signal, the actor may switch to B at
extra cost c. With signal B, posterior probability of type B is q. Keeping A has conditional
expected loss 2 + 10q. Switching has conditional expected loss 2 + c + 10(1 −q).
Switching is preferred when
2 + c + 10(1 −q) < 2 + 10q
⇐⇒
c < 20q −10.
(16.2)
At q = 0.9, the threshold is 8. The switching opportunity is exercised only after signal B under
the symmetric setup. Its unconditional value must also account for the probability of that
signal.
If the initial hedge cannot be unwound and the second hedge creates an offsetting or additional
exposure rather than replacing it, the formula changes. A model should not assume that a new
action erases an old contract. This is the same event-persistence issue seen in debt amendments.
16.7
Unknown signal quality
Now suppose evidence supports only q ∈[0.6, 0.9]. The expected loss from following the signal
lies in [3, 6] under the same prior and symmetry assumptions. The gross information value lies
in [1, 4]. A signal priced at 0.5 improves expected loss for every admitted q; one priced at 3 is
worthwhile only for some qualities.
These are bounds over a parameterized probabilistic family. They are not probabilities over
signal quality. Assigning a uniform distribution to q would add another assumption. A robust
decision can use the worst admitted quality, while a Bayesian decision can use a prior over it if
justified.
If the quality depends on exposure type, one scalar q is insufficient. Let sensitivity and specificity
differ. Posterior probabilities then depend on the prior and both error rates. A report that is
usually correct overall can still be misleading for a rare type. The observation model should
preserve the relevant asymmetry.
16.8
Unequal prior probabilities
Let prior probability of A be p and signal accuracy remain symmetric at q. After signal A, the
posterior is
p+
A =
pq
pq + (1 −p)(1 −q).
(16.3)
After signal B, it is
p+
B =
p(1 −q)
p(1 −q) + (1 −p)q.
(16.4)
The actor chooses hedge A when the posterior probability of A exceeds one half, hedge B when
below, and is indifferent at equality under the symmetric loss structure.
If p is very high, even signal B may not overturn the prior. Following the signal mechanically is
then suboptimal. For example, p = 0.95 and q = 0.9 gives posterior A after signal B equal to
0.095/(0.095 + 0.045), about 0.6786. Hedge A remains preferred.
This illustrates why a signal label is not itself a decision. The policy combines the observation
with prior information and payoffs. In an unweighted reconstruction, there may be no justified


PAGE 137
CHAPTER 16.
A DECISION BEFORE AND AFTER DISCLOSURE
115
prior at all, so a posterior calculation would be inappropriate until that modeling choice is
admitted.
16.9
An unweighted version
Suppose both types remain possible and no probabilities are assigned. An exact signal identifying
the type allows the correct hedge in every world, producing loss 2. Without a signal, either
hedge has worst-case loss 12, while doing nothing has worst-case loss 10. A minimax-loss policy
therefore chooses no hedge before information and the correct hedge after exact information.
The criterion differs from the earlier expected-loss decision, which chose a hedge without a
signal. This is not a contradiction. The uncertainty representation and decision rule changed.
A system should report the dependence rather than label one answer universally optimal.
If a noisy signal can be wrong in either type and only support is known, both types may remain
possible after either signal. The worst-case benefit can be zero. A probability of correctness
above one half is a probabilistic restriction; it cannot be inferred from support alone.
16.10
Management and public investors
Suppose management already knows the type from internal contracts, while public investors see
only aggregate statements. Management can choose the correct hedge with loss 2 even before
public disclosure. The analyst should simulate that informed behavior if it is the declared
policy.
A public announcement can then affect investor beliefs and financing offers without changing
management’s hedge choice. The same information event has different economic consequences
for different actors. A single global belief state cannot represent this distinction.
If management’s action itself is observable, investors may learn from it. A hedge choice can
signal the hidden type under an assumed policy. That inference depends on the policy model. If
management sometimes hedges for other reasons, the action is not a perfect signal. Endogenous
disclosure and signaling require a richer model than an exogenous likelihood.
16.11
Endogenous observations
A company can choose when and what to disclose within its obligations. A lender can request
information before making an offer. An auditor can discover discrepancies with probability
depending on effort. These observations depend on actions and hidden state.
The observation kernel should then be written conditional on action as well as state. Treating
disclosure as independent can distort the value of monitoring or the selection of companies that
reveal information. A missing report may itself be informative only under an explicit model of
why reports are missing.
AccDSL need not assume a strategic disclosure game in every application. It should allow
exogenous observation models as a bounded profile and state when they are inadequate. If the
query concerns financing responses to disclosure, the endogenous channel may be material and
should remain an open modeling requirement.


PAGE 138
CHAPTER 16.
A DECISION BEFORE AND AFTER DISCLOSURE
116
16.12
Persistent type and repeated signals
Suppose two conditionally independent signals of accuracy 0.9 arrive about the same fixed type
with equal prior. Two A signals give posterior A
0.92
0.92 + 0.12 = 81
82.
(16.5)
An A followed by B returns posterior one half under the symmetry and independence assumptions.
These updates concern one persistent type, not a newly drawn type for each signal.
If the signals are copies of the same underlying source, conditional independence is false. Count-
ing a duplicated report as independent evidence would overstate confidence. Two identical copies
should not square the likelihood unless the observation process actually supplies independent
information.
Source provenance therefore matters statistically. A news summary quoting a filing is not
necessarily another independent measurement of the loan type. The system should preserve
derivation links so that a likelihood model can avoid double counting.
16.13
Correlated errors
Suppose both signals share a common extraction error. With probability 0.1 the type label
is reversed in the source, and both downstream reports repeat it. Two matching reports then
have accuracy 0.9, not 81/82. The agreement supplies no additional independent confirmation.
A model with separate independent error terms would produce unjustified confidence. The
remedy is a shared latent error or a source-dependence relation. If the dependence is unknown,
sensitivity over plausible observation models is more defensible than assuming independence
because it is convenient.
The same issue arises when several financial databases reproduce one filing or when multiple
ratios use the same extracted amount. More records do not necessarily mean more evidence.
The model should count informational content through its observation relation, not file count.
16.14
A belief-state calculation
In the finite probabilistic example, the posterior probability pt of type A summarizes the
evidence relevant to the hedge decision, provided the signal model and loss structure remain
fixed. Expected losses are 2 + 10(1 −pt) for hedge A, 2 + 10pt for hedge B and 10 for no hedge.
The policy chooses the smallest of these expressions. Hedge A is preferred to B when pt ≥1/2.
It is preferred to no hedge when pt ≥0.2. Hedge B is preferred to no hedge when pt ≤0.8.
Thus at least one hedge is preferred throughout [0, 1] in this particular cost structure, with the
choice switching at one half.
This compact belief is sufficient because the future depends on the past only through the fixed
type posterior under the declared model. If signal quality is itself unknown or past actions
create commitments, pt alone may cease to be sufficient. The state must then include those
additional quantities or their joint belief.


PAGE 139
CHAPTER 16.
A DECISION BEFORE AND AFTER DISCLOSURE
117
16.15
Compression tests from the example
A proposed compression should preserve the joint type-signal distribution, action availability,
costs and timing.
It should reproduce expected loss 3 for informative signals and 7 for
uninformative signals. It should refuse to merge those technologies for an adaptive policy query.
A separate test can merge states differing only in an irrelevant customer label when neither
payoffs nor observations depend on that label. This establishes a valid reduction within the
small model. Positive and negative examples are both needed: a system that never merges is
safe from one error but does not solve the computational problem.
Changing the query to one fixed hedge can make a coarser compression sufficient. The approval
should be scoped to that query. Reusing it for information acquisition or adaptive hedging
without rechecking would repeat the original mistake.
16.16
What the case teaches about trees
The full tree has hidden types, signals and actions. Hidden type branches need not be visible to
the actor. Signal branches define information partitions, and actions before a signal must be
shared across indistinguishable worlds. A drawing that places separate decisions under each
hidden type can accidentally imply forbidden knowledge.
A belief-state or symbolic implementation can encode the same problem without storing every
path. It must still preserve the information partition. The tree is a useful explanatory projection
of that structure, not its only possible storage form.
The central requirement is therefore not one point per possibility. It is preservation of the joint
relation among persistent facts, observations and feasible decisions. This example gives exact
numbers against which a proposed representation can be tested before it is applied to a much
larger company.


PAGE 140
Chapter 17
Continuous history families:
projection, optimization, and
threshold refinement
A finite list of histories is often an inadequate description even of a small company. Unknown
rate exposure, customer allocation, asset age and settlement timing can vary continuously. The
right question is frequently whether a conclusion holds over a constrained region. This chapter
develops several exact small calculations and shows where they cease to be adequate. The
methods are proposals for bounded profiles, not a claim that every company reconstruction is a
linear program.
17.1
An affine exposure family
Let x be foreign-currency debt maturing soon and a the floating share of total debt. Suppose
disclosed margins imply 0 ≤x ≤50 and 0 ≤a ≤1. A simplified next-period cash requirement
under currency depreciation d and rate increase r is
Q(x, a) = Q0 + dx + 100ra.
(17.1)
If d and r are nonnegative and the two unknowns are jointly unconstrained beyond their bounds,
the maximum is Q0 + 50d + 100r and minimum is Q0. These extrema occur at feasible corners.
No grid is needed.
Now add a disclosure that the floating portion is entirely within the foreign-currency debt
maturing soon, so 100a ≤x. The feasible family is no longer a rectangle. The minimum remains
Q0, but the maximum floating share is one half, giving maximum Q0 + 50d + 50r. The relation
changes the worst case even though debt total remains 100.
The condition is a stipulated example, not an inference from the margins alone. It illustrates why
exact bounds must optimize over the joint constraints. Independently maximizing every marginal
amount can overstate or understate the relevant result depending on signs and relationships. A
constraint-aware bound carries the assumptions that make its corner feasible.
If the query includes a hedge whose receipt increases with x, the coefficient on x can change
sign. Monotonicity should be derived from the full expression, not assumed from a variable’s
label. A larger foreign-currency exposure can have a smaller net cash effect when a linked hedge
changes at the same time.
118


PAGE 141
CHAPTER 17.
CONTINUOUS FAMILIES AND EXACT BOUNDS
119
17.2
Linear programs as a bounded exact method
When observations, bridges and query are linear in continuous unknowns, a bound can be
formulated as a linear program. Let z collect amounts and let Az ≤b, Ez = e define the family.
The minimum cash query is
m = inf{cTz + c0 : Az ≤b, Ez = e}.
(17.2)
The formulation separates a mathematical target from a solver. A solver can return an optimum,
infeasibility, unboundedness or an unresolved numerical status. Each requires interpretation.
Unboundedness may reveal a missing economic constraint rather than a truly unlimited company
exposure.
A dual certificate can support a bound when its assumptions and numerical accuracy are
checked. For a simple example, constraints x ≥0, y ≥0 and x + y ≤10 imply 2x + y ≤20
because 2x + y ≤2(x + y) ≤20. Equality is attained at (10, 0), so the maximum is exactly 20.
The inequality proof and feasible witness establish both sides.
A reported optimum without a feasible witness or trustworthy bound is weaker. Floating-point
tolerances can matter near a covenant. Rational reconstruction or validated interval checks
may be appropriate for a small certificate. The economic model should not change its threshold
merely to accommodate solver tolerance.
Linear programming does not resolve categorical interpretations, nonlinear prices or integer
security units automatically. Those additions can require mixed-integer, nonlinear or hybrid
methods. The profile should identify which relations remain outside the exact solver and how
their unresolved regions are retained.
17.3
Projection can preserve a useful small relation
Suppose receivables satisfy A1 = A0 + S −K −W, with nonnegative write-offs W, collections
K and known credit sales S. If only A0, A1, S matter to a future query, eliminating K and W
leaves K + W = A0 + S −A1. That sum is determined while its allocation is not.
A credit-loss stress depends on W or surviving customer exposure; a pure cash-history query
depends on K. Eliminating both into an unnamed residual can make neither query answerable.
A better compressed record retains the identified sum and the feasible allocation interval.
If A0 = 30, S = 40 and A1 = 20, then K + W = 50. With a note limiting write-offs to at most
5, collections lie in [45, 50]. A cash-flow disclosure of collections 48 then identifies write-offs 2
within this simplified bridge. Each additional source narrows the joint relation.
If currency movements or acquisitions are later admitted, the relation changes. A cached
inference W = 2 must be invalidated unless those terms are established zero. This is an example
of model expansion changing an earlier identified quantity. The system should preserve the old
inference with its premises rather than silently rewriting its meaning.
17.4
Intervals and dependence
Interval arithmetic can bound expressions quickly, but repeated variables can cause overes-
timation. If x ∈[0, 1], the expression x −x is exactly zero. Treating its two occurrences
independently gives interval [−1, 1]. The dependency loss is entirely computational.


PAGE 142
CHAPTER 17.
CONTINUOUS FAMILIES AND EXACT BOUNDS
120
For the financial expression C = 95 −I with I ∈[5, 7], interval evaluation correctly gives
C ∈[88, 90]. But evaluating C + I from separate intervals gives [93, 97] instead of exact 95. A
relational representation or symbolic substitution preserves the identity.
Overestimation can be acceptable for an outer bound if it is labeled and used with the correct
direction. It can become useless near a threshold or create spurious defaults. Refinement
can retain shared symbols, affine forms or polyhedral constraints rather than independently
widening every intermediate amount.
Nonlinear functions add further issues. A naive interval for x(1 −x) on [0, 1] is [0, 1], while
the exact range is [0, 1/4]. Completing the square, x(1 −x) = 1/4 −(x −1/2)2, identifies the
maximum. A small analytic reformulation can be more valuable than a large random sample.
17.5
Piecewise contract payoffs
A floating coupon with floor l and cap u can be written Nδ min(u, max(l, r + s)) under a stated
schedule. Unknown reference rate r partitions into floor, interior and cap regions. The function
is continuous but has slope changes.
Suppose principal is 100, year fraction one, spread zero, floor 3 per cent and cap 6 per cent.
For r ∈[0.02, 0.08], interest ranges from 3 to 6. A linear approximation using the midpoint rate
five per cent and an unconstrained slope predicts 2 to 8, missing both contractual limits.
If the query is only the interest range, monotonicity gives exact endpoints. If the query includes
a refinancing choice triggered by interest above 5.5, a split at r = 0.055 is needed in the interior
region. If the cap changes after a covenant event, contract state must retain that dependence.
A piecewise formula can therefore represent many paths compactly. The implementation needs
complete and disjoint guards, correct boundary inclusion and a record of the clause version.
A missing cap is an economic-model error; an incorrect inequality at the cap boundary is an
implementation error. Their repairs differ.
17.6
Nonmonotone outcomes
Many financial outputs are not monotone in a primitive shock. A rate increase raises floating
interest but can increase income on cash, reduce a fixed-rate debt’s market value or trigger a
favorable option exercise. A customer demand increase can improve revenue while creating a
working-capital funding need.
Consider cash after a profitable sale with collection delay. Producing q units costs 4q immediately,
while revenue 6q is collected later. Current cash falls as q rises even though eventual profit rises.
A solver maximizing profit may select a quantity that violates interim liquidity.
If financing capacity is B and opening cash C0, feasible production satisfies 4q ≤C0 + B before
collection. The eventual profit objective 2q does not remove this constraint. Aggregating the
period so that revenue and cost net immediately changes the feasible set.
A query-driven method must therefore include intermediate constraints and not rely on a generic
sign assumption. Monotonicity can be proved locally within a regime and fail when a guard
changes. The method should partition or return a qualified bound rather than extrapolate the
local result globally.


PAGE 143
CHAPTER 17.
CONTINUOUS FAMILIES AND EXACT BOUNDS
121
17.7
Integer quantities and minimum lots
Shares, bond lots and physical units may be discrete. If a company needs net cash 10 and
shares sell for 3 with no fees, a continuous calculation requires 10/3 shares. If only whole shares
can be issued, four shares raise 12. The excess cash and dilution are real consequences of the
lot constraint.
A minimum issuance size can make a small funding need expensive or infeasible. A fixed fee
can make splitting one issue into two change net proceeds. These effects break the proportional
invariance that holds for a simple linear loan.
The model should specify whether quantities are continuous approximations or exact units. For
large public share counts, a continuous approximation may be immaterial for some queries. For
a closely held company or indivisible asset, it may dominate the decision. The approximation
needs a bound tied to the question.
17.8
Branch-and-bound interpretation
A mixed discrete-continuous family can be explored through bounds on regions. If a region’s
best possible result cannot improve the incumbent for an optimization query, it can be pruned
under the method’s correctness conditions. If a region is infeasible, it can be removed with
evidence.
Pruning for computational convenience is different. A low-probability region cannot be removed
from a worst-case query merely because it is unlikely. A region far from current data may still
be admissible if the observation model permits it. The pruning reason belongs in the result
record.
For a safety query, an outer lower cash bound above the requirement can close a region as safe.
A feasible witness below it establishes possible failure. Regions whose bounds straddle the
threshold remain unresolved and receive refinement priority. This approach can concentrate
work where it changes the answer.
The procedure may still face exponential growth. Resource exhaustion should preserve the
incumbent witnesses, certified bounds and unresolved regions. Reporting only the best found
point hides the remaining uncertainty and can reverse the meaning of a worst-case result.
17.9
Sensitivity of identified sets
A small source revision can change a feasible family discontinuously when constraints meet at a
boundary. Suppose exact observations require x = 1 and an interpretation requires x ≤1. The
family is nonempty. Revising the observation to x = 1.001 makes it empty if no tolerance or
other interpretation is admitted.
This does not mean the company changed abruptly. It may reveal that exact equality was too
strong for a rounded report. Observation precision and numerical tolerance should be audited
before interpreting a discontinuity economically.
Conversely, a small genuine change can cross a contractual threshold and have a large effect.
Smoothing it away would hide a real mechanism. The analyst must distinguish measurement
uncertainty from contractual discontinuity. Both can occur near the same number but require
different treatment.
A useful sensitivity report varies source precision, interpretation choices and economic shocks


PAGE 144
CHAPTER 17.
CONTINUOUS FAMILIES AND EXACT BOUNDS
122
separately. It identifies whether a conclusion is driven by evidence, modeling assumptions or a
threshold in the contract. This is more informative than a single generic tolerance parameter.
17.10
A bounded implementation profile
An initial exact profile can support rational linear constraints, finite interpretation choices
and piecewise affine event rules. It can return feasible witnesses, exact or certified bounds,
and guarded outcome formulas. Nonlinear valuation and unsupported clauses remain explicit
extensions.
The profile should reject incompatible units, unbound variables and missing event premises before
solving. It should preserve unknown amounts as variables with justified domains. Arbitrary
large bounds inserted to make a solver finite should be labeled as computational assumptions
and tested for sensitivity.
A later nonlinear profile can use interval methods, constrained optimization or sampling, but
must retain the original query target and distinguish local from global results. A local minimum
of cash is not a global worst case. A successful solver exit code is not a proof of completeness.
The practical advantage of a bounded profile is that its claims can be checked. It does not
redefine the whole mission to exclude difficult contracts. Instead, it provides a reliable core and
a precise account of which additional methods are needed for broader company simulation.
17.11
Sharing a rate family without sharing away a borrowing
right
Return to the capped floating coupon, but now ask whether the company can pay it. The
company starts with cash of 5.5, other assets of 94.5 and debt of 100. Its equity is initially zero.
One admissible history has a fixed coupon of 5; another has the floating coupon
I(r) = 100 min{0.06, max{0.03, r}},
r ∈[0.02, 0.08].
(17.3)
At the earlier observed rate of five per cent, both histories report interest of 5. A later rate of
six per cent separates them: fixed interest stays at 5 while floating interest reaches 6. A journal
history that retains only the earlier interest amount cannot answer the new payment question.
Each history also has two possible contractual interpretations. A committed facility may be
available, or no facility may be available. In this stipulated example the facility lends 1, charges
an immediate fee of 0.25 and can be drawn only when interest exceeds 5.5. These terms define
a small test case; they are not inferred from the earlier statements. The four combinations of
coupon type and facility availability remain separate possibilities. No probability is attached to
them.
There is one decision date. Interest accrues before the decision, and the manager submits either
a payment order or a combined draw-and-pay order. These are indivisible instructions. An
instruction that fails a prerequisite is refused. Contingent instructions, such as draw only if the
bank permits it, would enlarge the action set and require a fresh analysis.
17.11.1
The amount and the right determine different conditions
A payment order requires enough existing cash. A draw-and-pay order requires the contractual
right, the strict trigger and enough cash after the fee. Write b = 1 when the facility is committed

(17.5)
Because interest never exceeds 6, an eligible draw leaves at least 0.25 cash. At interest exactly
5.5, ordinary payment remains possible and the draw is forbidden. Replacing the strict trigger
with a weak inequality would change the contract at that point. At interest 6, the two facility
interpretations have identical interest amounts but different feasible actions: the committed
company can draw, while the company without the facility can execute neither order.
Accrual is independent of that execution result. The company first debits interest expense and
credits interest payable by I. If payment refuses, cash remains 5.5 and interest payable remains
I; equity has fallen to −I. A failed payment does not erase the expense or the creditor’s claim.
If payment succeeds, it removes the payable and cash becomes 5.5 −I. If the draw succeeds,
borrowing adds 1 of cash and debt, the fee reduces cash and equity by 0.25, and payment
removes the payable. The resulting balance sheet satisfies

17.11.2
A continuous comparison can be exhaustive
For floating debt, the coupon changes formula at rates 0.03 and 0.06, and the payment trigger
changes at 0.055. Suppose also that a coarse signal tells the manager whether the rate is at
most 0.04. The six points
0.02,
0.03,
0.04,
0.055,
0.06,
0.08
(17.7)
then delimit every relevant change. Keep each point as a singleton and each interval between
successive points as an open interval. The eleven resulting regions are disjoint and cover the
entire closed rate domain. The fixed-coupon alternative can use fewer regions because it has no
rate-dependent coupon or payment trigger.
Within each open region, every financial amount is affine and every signal and eligibility condition
is constant. Two implementations therefore agree throughout that region when their affine
coefficients and discrete results agree. At singleton regions, compare their values directly. If one
implementation uses a coarser partition, compare both on a common refinement and establish
that each refined region lies entirely inside one region of each implementation. Sampling a
midpoint can identify a known affine piece, but it cannot establish that the list of pieces is
complete.
The checked reference makes this comparison for the full journal, cash flows, balance sheet,
payable, facility right, observations and action outcomes. Its independent comparator explicitly
enumerates all coupon types, facility interpretations and the eleven rate regions. The smaller
representation retains 32 mode-specific regions and shares 14 identical calculations. Every
original mode and region still points to its calculation. These counts describe this example:
sharing reduces duplicated results here, but supplies no general bound on memory use or
runtime.


PAGE 146
CHAPTER 17.
CONTINUOUS FAMILIES AND EXACT BOUNDS
124
Even this small construction exposed a consequential implementation mistake. Solving the
draw-liquidity equation using the interior formula I = 100r gives a root at r = 0.0625. That
formula applies only below the cap at 0.06. The root is irrelevant to the capped region and
must not be inserted as though the interior formula still governed there. A root finder must
check the domain of the formula that generated its candidate boundary.
Open regions also require careful treatment of extrema. On 0.055 < r < 0.06, cash after drawing
and paying is 6.25 −100r, so
inf C = 0.25,
sup C = 0.75.
(17.8)
Neither endpoint is attained within that open region. The point r = 0.06 attains cash 0.25,
while r = 0.055 belongs to the ordinary-payment region. Reporting an interval without its
endpoint status loses a distinction needed for strict contractual conditions.
17.11.3
Information can prevent an otherwise affordable action
For the moment retain only histories with a committed facility. Every detailed history admits
one of the two orders. This does not imply that the manager can select a feasible order using
the information available at the decision date.
Under the coarse signal, high means only that r > 0.04. It includes floating rates with interest
below 5.5, where payment is possible but drawing is forbidden, and rates above 0.055, where
drawing is possible but ordinary payment fails. The manager must issue the same order at
indistinguishable histories. Neither order works throughout this signal class.
Now suppose a second, explicit pre-decision signal reveals whether interest exceeds 5.5. The
manager can pay when the trigger is false and draw and pay when it is true. This rule is feasible
throughout the committed-facility family. Its feasibility comes from the additional information,
not from a better numerical approximation. If unavailable facilities are restored to the family,
some high-rate floating histories admit neither order. Even complete information cannot create
borrowing capacity.
The exact condition is elementary in this finite action setting. Let H(o) contain all retained
histories with observation o, and let A(h) be the actions feasible at history h. A deterministic
policy feasible throughout the family exists exactly when
\
h∈H(o)
A(h) ̸= ∅
for every observed class H(o).
(17.9)
Necessity follows because one observation selects one action, which must work at every compatible
history. For sufficiency, choose an action from each nonempty intersection. The finite collection
of signals then defines a total policy. This argument depends on the supplied observation
map, two atomic orders and robust feasibility across the family. It is not a conclusion about
contingent instructions or a probability-weighted objective.
17.11.4
What remains safe when calculation is unfinished
A computation budget can stop evaluation of individual regions without removing them from
the family. In the reference, the partition is constructed first and transitions are evaluated when
requested. If only one region has been evaluated, the others remain explicitly unresolved. The
coupon still has the valid outer bound 3 ≤I ≤6, but that bound alone does not identify a
feasible payment policy. Eager construction of the partition itself can become expensive when
more continuous variables or contractual alternatives are added.


PAGE 147
CHAPTER 17.
CONTINUOUS FAMILIES AND EXACT BOUNDS
125
Approximation needs a separate justification. Suppose an independently established bound
gives |I −bI| ≤ε throughout a region. A strict trigger I > K is certainly true when bI −ε > K,
and certainly false when bI + ε ≤K. In the remaining band the bound permits both answers, so
the region must be refined or left unresolved. The conclusion follows by comparing the entire
allowed interval with K; sampled errors do not supply its uniform premise.
Observation thresholds need their own error bounds. Preserving the interest trigger while
changing which rate signal the manager sees can still change the feasible-policy set. A reused
calculation must therefore retain its rate domain, contract interpretation, clause version, horizon,
query, observation map, action class and precision. Changing any of these requires reconsidering
the grouping. The example provides an exact one-rate, one-date reference for that discipline.
Persistent hidden terms across several dates, higher-dimensional regions, nonlinear guards and
defensible approximation bounds remain further method problems.1
17.12
Repayment changes tomorrow’s feasible payments
The previous payment calculation ends as soon as interest has been settled. A facility used to
make that payment may have to be repaid before the next coupon. To see why the horizon
matters, carry the same company forward for one more date. The fixed or floating coupon
interpretation persists, as does the existence or absence of the facility. The two reference
rates may vary independently within the earlier interval. This rectangular rate family is an
assumption for the example, not a probability model.
The facility permits one draw of 1 during the two-date horizon and charges the same immediate
fee of 0.25. A first-date draw must be repaid on the second date, after an operating cash receipt
and before the second coupon. Repayment extinguishes the facility debt but does not restore
the right to draw. The original principal of 100 matures beyond the horizon. We omit tax
and interest on the facility to isolate the timing of cash and the persistence of the drawing
restriction.
Let I1 and I2 denote the two coupons and R the operating cash receipt on the second date. In
the fixed interpretation both coupons are 5. In the floating interpretation each is calculated
from its own reference rate using the earlier floor and cap. Receiving one coupon of 5 does not
by itself reveal the debt type: floating debt can produce the same amount. Nor does it reveal
whether the facility exists.
17.12.1
The first order leaves a different second opening
Ordinary payment on the first date leaves cash 5.5 −I1. Drawing and paying leaves 6.25 −I1
and facility debt of 1. On the second date the operating receipt increases cash and income by
R, after which any outstanding facility debt is repaid. Cash available before paying the second
coupon is therefore
C2 =

5.25 + R −I1,

(17.10)
The second line follows by adding R to 6.25 −I1 and subtracting the repayment of 1. Although
the facility improved first-date liquidity, its fee leaves less cash available over the complete
two-date sequence.

The receipt of 6 cannot fund the second coupon. Omitting repayment would incorrectly show

Before either payment order, the second coupon accrues as interest expense and interest payable.
A refused order leaves that payable and the earlier receipt, repayment and fee entries intact.
If a first-date order already refuses, this particular reference returns that incomplete prefix
without inventing second-date states. Modeling later default, recovery or renegotiation requires
additional events.
17.12.2
The relevant boundary now joins both rates
Following a first-date draw, ordinary second payment requires C2 −I2 ≥0. Substituting the
cash expression gives
I1 + I2 ≤5.25 + R.
(17.11)
For a receipt of 6, the boundary is I1 + I2 = 11.25. Within the interior floating-coupon pieces it
becomes a diagonal boundary in the two rates, 100r1 + 100r2 = 11.25. Separate bounds on the
two marginal rates do not describe which joint histories cross it. Equality permits payment;
larger coupon totals cause refusal.
For a receipt of 7, the corresponding bound is 12.25. Each coupon is at most 6, so their sum is
at most 12 and ordinary second payment is feasible throughout the first-draw region. After
ordinary first payment, I1 ≤5.5; even the receipt of 6 then supplies at least 6 before the next
coupon. The failure with the smaller receipt is thus concentrated in histories that needed an
earlier draw and then face a sufficiently large combined coupon.
The exact comparison first takes the products of the earlier point and open rate intervals. It
then splits those regions at the later linear cash conditions. Every resulting balance and journal
amount is affine in (r1, r2), while each action condition has a fixed truth value. The independent
financial calculation uses the closed-form cash expressions above. It checks that accepted regions
cover exactly the feasible domains, that refusals cover the remainder, and that both calculations
produce the same complete journal and financial state.
The geometric calculation also retains strict boundaries. On a bounded region described by
rational linear inequalities, its closed relaxation is generated by finitely many vertices. The
average of all those vertices satisfies a required strict inequality whenever at least one vertex
satisfies it strictly: every other vertex has nonpositive slack, so their average remains strictly
negative. If all vertices lie on its boundary, no point of the closed region can satisfy that inequality
strictly. Applying this reasoning to every strict constraint decides whether the original region
contains a point. Extremum attainment is checked by adding the optimizing equality and testing
the resulting face in the same way. These arguments apply to the supplied bounded linear
regions; a nonlinear or unbounded problem needs another method. The financial comparator
shares these geometric primitives, so its agreement does not constitute an independent solver
validation.


PAGE 149
CHAPTER 17.
CONTINUOUS FAMILIES AND EXACT BOUNDS
127
17.12.3
More cash does not reveal a hidden contract
With a committed facility and a receipt of 7, the refined first-date signal supports the same
initial rule as before: pay when the trigger is false and draw when it is true. On the second
date the manager observes whether the coupon exceeds 5.5 and whether available cash covers
it, together with the earlier signal and chosen order. Ordinary payment is feasible throughout
this family.
Some histories also permit a second-date draw: the first coupon was paid without drawing,
the facility remains unused and the second coupon exceeds 5.5. There are two such reachable
observation classes in this example, and either ordinary payment or drawing is feasible in each.
Consequently four policies satisfy the stipulated feasibility conditions. This count does not rank
their value. Optional borrowing incurs the fee and leaves debt beyond the current horizon.
Under the coarse first-date signal, the manager still cannot distinguish histories requiring
opposite first orders.
Raising the later receipt does not repair that information problem.
Retaining histories without a facility also leaves some first payments unfunded. A correct
continuation therefore preserves contractual memory, cash timing and the information available
to the actor together. Renewing the facility, redrawing the debt type or revealing an unobserved
trigger would change the problem being solved.2
17.13
Connecting the history to accounting execution
A financial path can be correct in a small mathematical model and still be translated incorrectly
into a company’s accounting system. The preceding example treats equity as a single amount
after income and expenses. An accounting book usually distinguishes capital, operating income,
interest expense and fees. Those distinctions matter when the simulator must produce an
income statement, cash-flow statement and retained claim history as well as a closing balance
sheet.
The checked connection uses the existing common accounting executor with explicit definitions
of the selected events. It does not put the earlier Python posting calculation inside that executor.
The comparison instead asks whether independently specified methods produce the same full
accounts, journals, cash-flow origins and contractual memory. Two different account charts test
whether a method depends on accounting roles rather than convenient account names.
17.13.1
Equity agrees only after the accounts are mapped
Let Bk be an account’s signed balance, positive for a debit and negative for a credit. Assets and
expenses then normally have positive balances, while liabilities, capital and income normally
have negative balances. In this example the reduced equity E used in the earlier calculation
corresponds to
E = −Bcapital −Bincome −Binterest expense −Bfee expense.
(17.12)
For example, an operating receipt of 7 gives the income account a credit balance of −7. Two
coupons of 6 give interest expense a debit balance of 12, and a facility fee adds expense of 0.25.
With no opening capital, the reported equity after profit is 7 −12 −0.25 = −5.25. This equals
the reduced model’s equity without hiding the income statement inside the capital account.

An interest accrual I adds I to expense and −I to interest payable. Under the mapping, equity

the payable, leaving profit unchanged. A combined draw-and-pay instruction has the following

∆Bcash = 0.75 −I,

(17.13)

Their sum is zero. The cash-flow record separately retains borrowing of 1, the fee payment of
0.25 and the interest payment of I. Netting those amounts in the journal’s cash entry must not
erase their origins in the cash-flow statement.
Repayment removes the facility liability and reduces cash by the same amount. Its accounting
balance becomes zero, but the separate memory that the facility has already been used remains
true. Each method also checks that interest payable agrees with accrued coupons less paid
coupons, that the event occurs at the declared phase and date, and that its prerequisites are
satisfied. A refused payment leaves the accepted prefix unchanged, including the coupon already
accrued.
For the concrete execution comparison, the two decisions are assigned to 30 June and 31
December 2027 within one supplied reporting period. The coupon amounts remain the one-
period charges of the earlier example. These dates test ordering; they do not supply an actual
instrument’s day-count convention. Each comparison checks the complete account balances,
income statement, balance sheet, gross cash flows, journals and memory. Applying the equity
mapping also recovers the earlier independent financial calculation.
17.13.2
A checked path is not a checked continuous region
The common executor accepts concrete rational amounts. The selected comparison therefore
uses exact points at the coupon cap, at the first trigger, and at and immediately to either
side of the later joint cash boundary. Those executions exercise important failure mechanisms.
They do not by themselves establish that every rate in a continuous region produces the correct
accounting result.
Within a region where the guards are fixed, the amount relations above are affine. If the opening
accounts map correctly, each event has the stated affine effect, and the same guard, timing
and memory conditions govern both descriptions, induction over the accepted events gives the
same mapped financial quantities. The conditions carry the argument: a wrong guard or lost
drawing right can change which event occurs even when its monetary formula is correct. A
general checker must verify those conditions and every supported method, rather than replace
that obligation with a larger sample of rates.
The concrete evidence at this stage has two parts. The exact continuous reference establishes
its financial formulas and feasible domains. The concrete common-engine comparison checks
complete accounting execution at declared points.
The explicit mapping and local event
derivation motivate the selected symbolic certificates in Section 17.14; extending them to general
methods remains a separate problem. Source-backed terms and actual accounting applicability
also remain distinct from agreement between two synthetic representations.
17.13.3
Stopping computation must preserve what remains unknown
Suppose the simulator has executed the first coupon and then reaches its computation budget.
A useful saved result contains the complete accepted book and journal, the next command, and


PAGE 151
CHAPTER 17.
CONTINUOUS FAMILIES AND EXACT BOUNDS
129
the commands still pending. It must also retain the alternative histories and regions that have
not been evaluated. A successful payment on one path supplies no reason to delete a different
path that may fail tomorrow.
The selected checkpoint reference validates a saved concrete prefix by replaying it from its
declared opening and method registry. It compares the complete transition records and current
state before continuing. The tested interruptions recover the same result as uninterrupted
execution, including the first-date and second-date refusal prefixes. Altering cash while keeping
the book balanced, changing paid-coupon memory or removing the used-facility flag fails that
comparison. Merely recomputing a checksum on the altered record would not establish that the
altered history followed from its opening.
A zero budget attempts no new financial event. It can still incur the cost of checking the saved
prefix. More generally, the budget in this reference counts new event attempts, while replay
validation costs additional work. A practical implementation would need a separately justified
way to reduce that cost. The present result establishes a small exact recovery comparison, not
an efficient or crash-durable storage system.
The queue also distinguishes a completed concrete program from a resolved continuous region.
For the two-date example, the declared roster contains four persistent contract combinations,
11 × 11 rate-atom pairs, four order pairs and two receipt hypotheses:
4 × 121 × 4 × 2 = 3,872
region and instruction obligations.
(17.15)
A finite witness cannot mark any of those whole regions complete. Their identities remain
unresolved under this concrete checkpoint even when individual concrete jobs succeed or refuse;
Section 17.14 supplies separate certificates for the selected whole regions. Within the finite
job queue, pending, completed and refused jobs form an exact partition. A refused job retains
both its payable and its unexecuted commands; it is a completed assessment of that particular
instruction sequence, not proof that every alternative has failed.
Finally, reuse depends on what was asked and what information was available. The saved
record names the source revision, clause, actor’s observation map, available actions, horizon,
query, precision and method. A changed scope refuses reuse under the old record. An earlier
query can still use its earlier explicitly supplied scope, preserving the distinction between later
knowledge and information available at the time. Those bindings do not authenticate the source
or establish when a real manager received it. Source admission, extension beyond the selected
symbolic domains, efficient refinement and durable recovery require their own evidence.3
17.14
Proving a result for an entire rate region
The second coupon gives a useful test of what a simulation has actually established. Suppose the
company drew its once-only facility at the first date. After the operating receipt and repayment,
cash available for the second coupon is 5.25 + R −I1. A payment succeeds exactly when
I1 + I2 ≤5.25 + R.
(17.16)
At I1 = I2 = 6, a receipt of 6 leaves only 5.25 before the payment, so the coupon remains
unpaid. A receipt of 7 leaves 6.25 and permits payment, with 0.25 left over. A simulator that
happens to test only the latter case has learned nothing about the former. Even a large set of
