physical probability model can estimate frequency but requires a risk adjustment to price claims
unless special assumptions apply.
A test with different physical and pricing probabilities should produce different expected losses
while preserving the declared price. The report should refuse an unlabeled probability when
the measure is ambiguous. This is a semantic type check with direct financial consequences.
25.14
A numerical tolerance followed by a legal threshold
A solver treats a constraint violation of 10−6 as acceptable. A downstream routine interprets
the approximate value as exact compliance with a strict contractual threshold. The numerical
convention has silently become a legal conclusion.
The result should carry an error interval or tolerance. If the interval crosses the threshold,
classification is unresolved unless another calculation determines the sign. The contract’s
inclusive or strict comparison remains unchanged.
A test at equality and on both sides by less than the solver tolerance reveals this interaction.
Exact rational cases can establish intended boundary semantics. Floating-point production
methods then need an explicit policy for translating their error into a classification.
25.15
A language model followed by evidence admission
A language model can propose a plausible missing journal or summarize a contract. Those
outputs are interpretations or hypotheses until supported. If they are inserted into the evidence
store as facts, subsequent inference can become circular: the model later cites its own generated
explanation.
The admission layer should retain source anchors and distinguish extracted text, normalized
interpretation and generated suggestion. Unsupported suggestions can be useful search leads or
candidate templates. They should not narrow the family merely because the text is fluent.
A test should submit a plausible but unanchored maturity date. The system should preserve it
as a proposed assumption or reject factual admission. If a genuine source later supports it, the
new assertion can narrow the family with traceable provenance.
25.16
A user interface followed by decision interpretation
An interface may display one attractive central path while hiding unresolved regions. Users
can reasonably interpret it as the forecast even when the calculation produced a family. The
presentation then overstates certainty without any numerical bug.
The default display should identify conditional paths, ranges, measures and unresolved cases in
proportion to their importance. It should not label an arbitrary witness most likely. A central


PAGE 209
CHAPTER 25.
FAILURES AT THE JOINS BETWEEN METHODS
187
estimate needs a stated selection rule or probability model.
A useful review asks whether the reader can identify the history driving a shortfall, the
assumption that could remove it and the status of the computation. If those answers require
searching internal logs, the explanation is insufficient even if the underlying data are preserved.
25.17
Composition contracts
Each boundary should specify input meaning, units, timing, information, allowed uncertainty
and failure behavior. A contract engine returns obligations and payoffs; an accounting engine
returns postings under a basis; a policy engine returns admissible actions; an inference engine
returns families or measures.
These interfaces should be tested end to end with the worked cases. A local adapter can be
mechanically correct while mapping the wrong concept. The test must compare the resulting
economic and accounting consequences, not only schema validity.
Version compatibility also matters. A changed rule can alter event order or measurement.
Results should identify the exact component versions and invalidate dependent caches. A stable
interface name does not establish semantic compatibility across versions.
25.18
A complete rate, covenant and ownership path
An exact combined example shows why the financing steps must be dated. Two possible
companies each start with cash 16 and loan face 100. One pays a fixed coupon of 5; the other
pays 7 after the specified floating-rate reset. Each then makes an operating payment of 6. A
covenant tests cash of at least 5 immediately after these payments and before financing. Breach
forbids new borrowing but permits an equity cure. Management requires closing cash 8. These
are assumed contract terms, not a claim about a standard covenant.
The fixed-rate company has cash 16 −5 −6 = 5, meets the covenant and needs 3. A candidate
debt issue with maximum face 5, conditional solvency probability 4/5, recovery 2 and gross
risk-free return 21/20 has proceeds 88/21. Holding that quoted per-face price fixed for this
conditional stress, issuing face 315/88 raises exactly 3. The floating-rate company has cash
16 −7 −6 = 3, breaches the covenant and needs 5. Debt is unavailable; even an improper
borrowing override at the maximum face would raise only 88/21 < 5.
Suppose the permitted equity cure has fee 1, pre-issue economic equity value 20 and 100
incumbent shares. A gross subscription of 6 provides net cash 5. The post-issue equity value is
25, new investors own 6/25, and the common share price is 19/100. Their 600/19 new shares
have value 6, while incumbents retain value 19. Both companies finish with cash 8, but their
new debt and ownership differ. Merging them on closing cash would conceal those differences.
Testing the covenant only after the cure would erase the earlier breach and wrongly admit the
borrowing alternative.
This example holds quoted financing prices and the pre-issue equity valuation fixed to expose
event ordering and allocation. It does not solve the feedback from financing to default and prices.
A source correction introduces another interaction: withdrawing an unsupported fixed-rate
classification can reopen the floating-rate history, invalidating an earlier single-history result.
If management later receives a rate notice, its admissible policies can change even when the
analyst’s reported balances do not. Shared computations must therefore depend on evidence
version, actor information, contracts and the requested outcomes, as well as current cash.


PAGE 210
CHAPTER 25.
FAILURES AT THE JOINS BETWEEN METHODS
188
25.19
Executing promises, carrying amounts and ownership
The combined example can be executed through the existing typed company runtime once
its state distinguishes contractual face from carrying amount. For the fixed-rate history, the
authored initial-measurement convention posts cash of 3 and a liability carrying amount of 3,
while contractual face increases by 315/88. The closing liability carrying amount is 103 and
outstanding face is 9115/88. Subsequent effective-interest measurement and settlement require
further methods; substituting carrying amount for the promise would lose the very exposure
the example is intended to preserve.
For the floating-rate history, subscription proceeds of 6 increase cash and contributed capital;
the direct issue cost of 1 reduces both. The typed state retains 100 incumbent shares and
600/19 new shares. Under this explicitly synthetic posting convention, both histories report
assets of 112. The fixed history has liabilities of 103 and book equity of 9; the floating history
has liabilities of 100 and book equity of 12. These book amounts differ from the economic
equity values used to price the subscription. No reporting-standard admission follows from the
balanced journals.
The reference adapter executes coupon payment, operations, covenant observation and financing
in that order, using the unchanged common company executor. It rejects borrowing after
the floating-rate breach, proceeds inconsistent with the supplied per-face quote, a zero issue
price, duplicate issuance and payment without sufficient cash. A mutation that omits the
share-count update still produces balanced accounts and completes the accounting path; the
independent ownership comparison detects the error. Accounting conservation alone therefore
cannot validate a financing method.
The same runtime also executes the earlier information example. Starting with cash 6, a fixed
coupon 5 leaves 1, while a floating coupon 7 cannot be paid. A hedge costing 1 caps the coupon
at 5. An informed manager hedges only the floating history and retains cash 1 or 0; a manager
who must act identically in both histories can hedge both and retain 0. Withdrawing the earlier
fixed-only evidence reopens the payment-refusal branch. The negative cash in the algebraic
shortfall calculation denotes unmet funding; the runtime refuses the payment atomically and
keeps the opening cash, rather than recording an impossible settlement. A missed-payment
event and any ensuing default process would need separate declared methods.
25.20
Prioritizing interaction tests
The highest-value interactions are those that can create cash, remove obligations, leak in-
formation or drop adverse histories. They should be tested early because they can make
every downstream chart look plausible. Positive-recovery pricing, settlement timing, ownership
conversion and joint-history preservation are examples.
Other interactions can be staged according to the active company and query. A bank capital
rule may be irrelevant to a simple industrial-company fixture but essential to a bank funding
scenario. The full mission retains these domains without forcing every first implementation to
solve all of them at once.
The catalogue remains extensible. Each newly discovered failure should identify the target,
actual behavior, mechanism, discriminating case and repair. That practice turns failures into
durable knowledge and prevents the same convenient shortcut from reappearing under another
module name.


PAGE 211
Chapter 26
A fully solved risky-debt model
before any neural approximation
A small structural model can make the financing equations completely transparent. This
chapter solves one with two future asset states. It is deliberately restrictive: one period,
one creditor, no tax, no fees initially, no bankruptcy costs, no interim distributions and risk-
neutral pricing with zero risk-free rate. These assumptions isolate the relationship among
promised repayment, proceeds, default, recovery and equity value. They are not a general
corporate-finance specification.
26.1
Assets and claims
At the final date, assets available to creditors and shareholders are 60 in the low state and
140 in the high state, each with probability one half. A debt contract promises face F ≥0.
The creditor receives the smaller of face and available assets. Shareholders receive the positive
residual.
D(A, F) = min(F, A),
(26.1)
E(A, F) = max(A −F, 0).
(26.2)
For every state, D(A, F)+E(A, F) = A. This conservation relation is exact under the stipulated
priority and absence of costs. Default occurs when assets are insufficient for the promise, with
equality treated as full payment.
Debt proceeds at issuance are the expected creditor payoff because the pricing rate is zero and
risk neutrality is assumed. Economic equity value is the expected shareholder payoff. Therefore
P(F) = 1
2 min(F, 60) + 1
2 min(F, 140),
VE(F) = 100 −P(F).
(26.3)
The expected asset value 100 is allocated between the claims. Face does not equal proceeds
once default becomes possible.
26.2
Solve the price schedule by regions
For 0 ≤F ≤60, debt is paid in both states, so P(F) = F. For 60 < F ≤140, the creditor
receives 60 in the low state and F in the high state, so P(F) = 30 + F/2. For F > 140, the
189


PAGE 212
CHAPTER 26.
A FULLY SOLVED RISKY-DEBT MODEL
190
creditor receives all assets in both states, so proceeds remain 100.
P(F) =





F,
0 ≤F ≤60,
30 + F/2,
60 < F ≤140,
100,
F > 140.
(26.4)
The function is continuous but its slope changes. Promising another unit raises proceeds by one
in the safe region, by one half in the intermediate region and by zero beyond the highest asset
payoff. An unlimited face amount does not create unlimited funding.
Equity value is 100 −F in the first region, 70 −F/2 in the second and zero in the third. These
are economic values under the model. They are not a balance-sheet measurement rule. The
model has not specified accounting carrying amounts.
26.3
A funding need of 80
Suppose an admitted transaction requires gross proceeds of 80. This need lies above the safe-debt
capacity 60. Solve 30 + F/2 = 80, giving face 100. In the low state the lender receives 60 and
shareholders zero. In the high state the lender receives 100 and shareholders 40.
Expected lender payoff is 80, matching proceeds. Expected shareholder payoff is 20. The
promised yield is 100/80 −1 = 25 per cent, although the risk-free rate is zero. That promised
yield compensates for the modeled default loss; it is not the realized return in both states.
The low-state recovery is 60, or 60 per cent of face. If the model instead specifies recovery as a
fixed fraction of face independent of available assets, it is a different recovery model and may
violate conservation at some face values. Recovery assumptions cannot be swapped silently.
26.4
A funding need of 110
No face value raises 110 in this model because expected distributable assets are only 100 and the
creditor cannot receive more than assets. A root finder searching for P(F) = 110 should report
no solution under the model, or a validated infeasibility conclusion if its method establishes it.
A numerical optimizer that pushes face to a large upper bound has not solved the funding
problem. Reporting that bound as issued debt would invent proceeds. The flat price region is a
useful test of solver behavior when increasing the promise no longer raises cash.
The company could still have an alternative outside this model: new equity, collateral from
another entity, a guarantee or a changed investment plan.
The no-solution conclusion is
conditional on the admitted financing channel and asset process. It is not proof that the real
company has no option.
26.5
Fees and net cash
Add a fixed issuance fee of 5 paid at inception. To net 80, gross proceeds must be 85. Solving
30 + F/2 = 85 gives face 110. Net cash is 80, expected creditor payoff 85, and expected equity
payoff 15 under the unchanged asset distribution.
If the fee instead equals five per cent of gross proceeds, required gross proceeds are 80/0.95 =
1600/19. Face is 2(1600/19 −30) = 2060/19, about 108.4211. The two cost structures give
different promises.


PAGE 213
CHAPTER 26.
A FULLY SOLVED RISKY-DEBT MODEL
191
If the fee is itself paid out of final assets rather than at inception, the asset payoff to claims
changes and pricing must be recomputed. Merely subtracting the fee from current proceeds
while also reducing final assets would charge it twice. The event dates determine which equation
it enters.
26.6
Recovery costs
Suppose default destroys 10 of assets before creditor recovery. For 60 < F ≤140, low-state
recovery becomes 50 and high-state payment remains F, so P(F) = 25 + F/2. Raising 80 now
requires face 110.
At face 110, expected shareholder payoff is 15 and expected creditor payoff 80. Their sum is 95,
below expected assets 100 by expected default cost 5. The conservation relation now includes
the cost recipient or destroyed value: creditor payoff plus shareholder payoff plus default cost
equals assets in each state.
A test that insists creditor plus shareholder value always equals 100 would be wrong after
adding bankruptcy costs. Invariants must follow the model’s resource accounting. Conversely, a
model with no costs that loses value unexplained has a defect.
26.7
Endogenous investment changes the price
Suppose management can choose between project S with assets 100 in both states and project
R with assets zero or 180 equally likely. Expected assets are 100 for S and 90 for R. Let face be
80. Under S, creditor payoff is 80 and equity payoff 20. Under R, creditor expected payoff is 40
and equity expected payoff 50.
Shareholders prefer R if they choose after debt proceeds are fixed and maximize their residual
value. Total expected asset value is lower under R. A lender anticipating R prices face 80
at 40, not 80. Pricing as though S will be chosen while allowing shareholders to choose R is
inconsistent.
A covenant restricting project choice, a different security structure or a commitment can change
the feasible policy. The model must say whether project choice is committed before issuance,
observed by lenders or chosen afterward. Timing determines the financing equilibrium.
26.8
A committed project comparison
If management commits credibly to S before issuance, face 80 raises 80. If it commits to R, face
80 raises 40. To raise 80 under R, face must satisfy F/2 = 80 while F ≤180, giving face 160.
Equity expected payoff is then 10.
The comparison is now between different promises financing the same proceeds under different
committed projects. S leaves equity value 20; R leaves 10. Under the stated objective and no
other benefits, S is preferred ex ante. This differs from the post-issuance asset-substitution
choice at fixed face.
The example demonstrates why a structural model must distinguish issuance decisions from
later policy. A single optimization over all variables can accidentally grant commitment that
the actor does not possess. A sequential model must encode which choices are binding when
the lender prices the debt.


PAGE 214
CHAPTER 26.
A FULLY SOLVED RISKY-DEBT MODEL
192
26.9
A simple equity alternative
Suppose the company can instead sell a fraction u of the final asset claim for fair value under
the original 60/140 distribution, with no fees. The price is 100u. Raising 80 requires selling 80
per cent, leaving existing owners expected value 20.
This matches the expected residual value after issuing face 100 debt in the frictionless original
example, but the state payoffs differ. With equity financing, old owners receive 12 in the low
state and 28 in the high state. With debt financing, they receive zero and 40. Equal expected
value does not imply equal risk or incentives.
Adding taxes, costs, control rights or different investor preferences can change the comparison.
Those mechanisms must be specified rather than inferred from the equality in this simplified
benchmark. The benchmark is valuable because it identifies exactly which additions drive a
difference.
26.10
Unknown asset distribution
Suppose reports do not identify the low-state asset payoff and it may be 40 or 60, while the
high payoff remains 140 and probabilities remain one half by assumption. For a promised face
100, proceeds are 70 or 80. A funding plan requiring 80 succeeds only in the second history.
If lenders know the asset type privately, they offer different prices across histories. If lenders
share the analyst’s uncertainty and use a prior, pricing requires that prior and the information
structure. Averaging proceeds without stating who knows the type can misrepresent both the
offer and management’s choice.
An unweighted robust funding calculation asks whether a face can raise the required cash under
every admitted pricing history. At face 120, proceeds are 80 in the low-asset history and 90 in
the other. But the higher promise changes shareholders’ payoffs. A robustly feasible financing
plan need not maximize expected owner value under a selected prior.
26.11
Learning before issuance
A verified asset report arriving before issuance can identify the low-state payoff and narrow the
price schedule. The same report arriving afterward cannot retroactively change settled proceeds,
although it may affect secondary-market value or future refinancing.
The simulator should retain issuance-time information for the original price and current infor-
mation for later valuation. Repricing the original cash receipt after new information would
rewrite history. A market-value update is a separate measurement, not a new issuance event.
This distinction is particularly important in backtests of debt pricing. Using later realized
recovery information to set the earlier issue price creates look-ahead. The model can use that
information to evaluate the forecast, but not as an original input.
26.12
Two creditors, policy consistency and financing refusal
In a finite one-period model, policy consistency can be checked by enumeration. Specify a
candidate policy π, the distribution of the terminal estate under that policy, the priority of
each claim, and a pricing measure and discount factor. Allocate the estate once, calculate each


PAGE 215
CHAPTER 26.
A FULLY SOLVED RISKY-DEBT MODEL
193
lender’s expected discounted receipt, and use those receipts as candidate issue proceeds. At
those fixed quoted proceeds, compare the issuer’s payoffs from every feasible policy. Retain the
candidate only when its conjectured policy is a best response and all funding and participation
constraints hold. Ties must be retained. If no pure-policy pair passes, report that result
for the declared class; neither a balancing loan nor an assumed mixed equilibrium repairs it
automatically.
For a concrete local example, existing senior debt has face 40 and newly issued junior debt has
face 80. A project costs 60. The safe policy produces estate 100; the risky policy produces
estate 20 or 220 with equal probabilities. Both pricing and shareholder valuation use the stated
risk-neutral probabilities and gross discount factor 21/20. Absolute priority in this illustration
gives receipts
(s, j, e) =
 min(A, 40), min((A −40)+, 80), (A −120)+
.
(26.5)
Thus the safe estate pays (40, 60, 0), and the risky estates pay (20, 0, 0) and (40, 80, 100). Each
allocation sums to its estate. The candidate junior proceeds are 400/7 under the safe policy
and 800/21 under the risky policy.
At a posted quote P, existing shareholders must subscribe 60 −P to cover the investment.
Their net value from policy π is E[eπ]/(21/20) −(60 −P). They prefer risk at either quoted
price. Consequently, the safe-policy quote is inconsistent with their best response: if risk is
chosen, junior lenders receive present value 800/21 after advancing 400/7, a shortfall of 400/21.
The risky quote is consistent within the pure-policy class, but requires shareholder cash 460/21.
If shareholders have only 20, the project remains unfunded. The safe quote would need less
cash, but its incentive problem remains. Even an enforceable safe-policy commitment would
still need shareholder participation: here its zero terminal equity receipt does not cover their
positive subscription. Liquidity, incentives, lender valuation and voluntary participation are
distinct restrictions.
The same separation applies to restructuring. Given estate 70 and creditor outside values 50
and 20, an offer 45/25 conserves value but the senior creditor refuses. An offer 50/20 meets
both constraints. A mandatory fee 1 reduces the available estate to69 and makes simultaneous
participation impossible under these stated outside values. This is a feasibility result. Selecting
a unique offer requires bargaining rights or another decision rule; it does not follow from total
value conservation.
26.13
Why positive surplus can still end in liquidation
Hart and Moore’s retained 1991 working paper makes the distinction between productive value
and credible repayment particularly clear. The entrepreneur can withdraw human capital, the
creditor can seize specified assets, and renegotiation follows an explicit offer game. Returns and
liquidation values are deterministic, private savings have a particular seizure treatment, and one
creditor controls the assets (Hart and Moore, 1991, Sections 2–3 and Appendix A1–A4). Under
those assumptions, Lemma 1 derives the creditor’s continuation reservation value and requires
both future nonrepudiation and cash feasibility. The bargaining rule determines a result that
cannot be obtained merely by balancing accounts.
In their Example 1, take two remaining operating receipts of 2 and 26 and liquidation rents of 8
in each period. The creditor’s reservation value is 16, while the final credible payment is at
most 13. The entrepreneur therefore needs to provide at least 3 before the final date. The first
operating receipt provides only 2, so private wealth w ≥1 is necessary. At w = 1, an immediate
payment of 1 followed by payments 2 and 13 satisfies the cash and repayment conditions. At
w < 1, it cannot. Liquidation occurs despite operating value 28 exceeding liquidation value 16


PAGE 216
CHAPTER 26.
A FULLY SOLVED RISKY-DEBT MODEL
194
(Hart and Moore, 1991, Example 1, printed p. 16). The reference calculation reproduces this
boundary with exact rational amounts. It is a useful counterexample to assuming that positive
total surplus guarantees refinancing or restructuring.
Their repayment-path analysis also reinforces the distinction between an observed payment
and its contractual explanation. A long-term contract and rolling short contracts can support
the same path under the paper’s conditions; the latter may additionally require access to
outside refinancing (Hart and Moore, 1991, Proposition 2 and printed pp. 27–28). A future
market closure can therefore distinguish histories that looked equivalent before the shock. For
a multi-creditor company, the analyst must additionally specify priority, control and consent
rights, collateral and intercreditor restrictions. The single-creditor result supplies neither an
equity-issuance model nor a general legal restructuring procedure.
26.14
Reference-solver requirements
The finite model has a closed-form piecewise price schedule, so a numerical method can be
checked at safe, risky, boundary and flat-price regions. Required points include face zero, 60,
100, 140 and above 140. Funding roots should be checked for needs below 60, between 60 and
100, exactly 100 and above 100.
A neural approximation can fit the schedule, but average error is not the only criterion. It
should preserve monotonicity, upper funding capacity and boundary classification within stated
error. A small positive price slope beyond 140 can falsely imply that an arbitrarily large face
eventually raises 110. Downstream feasibility is the decisive check for that use.
A smooth approximation around the kinks can be useful for training, but final results should be
evaluated against the original payoff. The source literature on learned economic solvers provides
candidate algorithms; this exact model provides a local target-specific test. No training result
is claimed here.
26.15
How the example answers the financing concern
Debt issuance is not a choice of a balance-sheet number. It is a contract whose cash price
depends on future payoff and policy. Equity issuance is not merely negative dividends; it
allocates future claims and potentially control. The finite model makes those relations explicit
before numerical approximation.
For AccDSL, an external offer can be accepted as a conditional input, while an endogenous
financing module can solve a stated pricing-policy problem.
Both routes must keep face,
proceeds, recovery, owner claims and information dates separate. This provides a defensible
starting point for extending the supplied lecture rather than importing its conflicting convention
into a larger simulator.
26.16
Conversion also changes the accounting question
The economic financing model specifies who receives cash and who chooses the project. Ac-
counting for those events requires additional distinctions. In the supplied contingent-conversion
extension, a creditor receives 120 shares alongside 40 existing shares. The resulting 75 per cent
voting fraction explains the extension’s assumed economic control rule. It does not by itself
establish the date control begins for accounting, the holder’s reporting perimeter, or the amount


PAGE 217
CHAPTER 26.
A FULLY SOLVED RISKY-DEBT MODEL
195
to recognise in its accounts.
The following analysis uses Australian standards as a conditional design case. It does not select
a reporting basis for an actual company. The retained editions and their application periods
matter: the inspected AASB 10 compilation covers annual periods beginning in 2026 before
2027, and the inspected AASB 3 compilation covers starts from 1 July 2026 before 2027. The
synthetic issue and conversion occur in 2027 and 2028, so a complete applicable edition and
entity-scope chain remains necessary. The source paragraphs identify the questions that must
be answered, while the missing facts remain unresolved.
26.16.1
Control can begin before the shares arrive
Under AASB 10, control requires power over the investee, exposure or rights to variable returns,
and the ability to use that power to affect the returns. Power concerns existing rights giving
the current ability to direct the activities that significantly affect those returns. Majority votes
support that conclusion in the circumstances specified by the standard, but the votes must be
substantive and contrary rights or arrangements can matter (Australian Accounting Standards
Board, 2026c, paras. 6–18, B22–B28, B35–B37).
This is a question about rights and relevant decisions, not just a percentage.
A lender’s
protective covenant can restrict a borrower without giving the lender power over its activities.
Conversely, an apparently contingent decision right is not automatically protective. If the
activities that significantly affect returns arise only after a particular event, the right to direct
those activities can matter before the event occurs (Australian Accounting Standards Board,
2026c, paras. B26–B28, B53).
Potential voting rights introduce the same timing issue into a convertible instrument. AASB 10
requires consideration of substantive potential rights, including those arising from convertibles,
options and forwards. Whether rights are substantive depends partly on whether they can be
exercised when decisions about the relevant activities need to be made (Australian Accounting
Standards Board, 2026c, paras. B24, B47–B50). It follows that the simulator needs a control
assessment before conversion as well as after it. Setting the first control date equal to the
share-issue timestamp would conceal this question.
The acquisition date for an in-scope business combination is the date control is obtained.
It can precede or follow the legal closing date when the relevant facts support that timing.
Consolidation similarly begins when control is obtained (Australian Accounting Standards
Board, 2026c, para. 20) (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026h, paras. 8–9). Thus the
economic conversion, the rights assessment and the accounting acquisition date require distinct
records linked by evidence. A date cannot be supplied merely to make the annual schedule
continuous.
26.16.2
Two control assessments do not determine the intervening history
Suppose the investor controls the company at both observation dates. Control may have
continued throughout, or it may have been lost and later regained. Likewise, two observations
of no control permit an intervening acquisition followed by a disposal. An unknown earlier
assessment must preserve both acquisition and continued-control possibilities. The reporting
period therefore needs a history of changes and evidence about the intervals between them; an
empty event list does not establish that no change occurred.
The accounting consequences differ. Changes in ownership while control continues are equity
transactions under the inspected AASB 10 paragraphs.
Loss of control instead requires


PAGE 218
CHAPTER 26.
A FULLY SOLVED RISKY-DEBT MODEL
196
the specified derecognition, retained-interest measurement, treatment of accumulated other
comprehensive income and resulting gain or loss (Australian Accounting Standards Board,
2026c, paras. 20, 23, 25, B96, B98–B99). A later return to control raises a new acquisition
assessment. The same closing voting fraction cannot select among these histories.
A boundary comparison exposed this distinction in the first conditional reference: unknown
prior control incorrectly selected an acquisition route, and a known loss omitted the loss-of-
control dependencies. The successor preserves all nine combinations of controlled, not controlled
and unknown endpoints, together with possible intervening changes. Each interval remains
unresolved until additional evidence narrows it. Its history classes preserve the alternatives
relevant to these dependency questions; they do not enumerate every possible event sequence.
A supplied measurement-reference string likewise identifies a dependency without establishing
the measurement itself.
26.16.3
The holder’s investment and the group’s assets are different reports
In separate financial statements, AASB 127 permits investments in subsidiaries, joint ventures
and associates to be accounted for at cost, under AASB 9, or using the equity method, subject
to its requirements and consistent treatment within each investment category. Certain required
fair-value treatments carry through to the separate statements (Australian Accounting Standards
Board, 2022c, paras. 3–10, 11A). Choosing a cost policy does not provide the initial cost amount.
The earlier price paid for a convertible claim cannot silently become the post-conversion
measurement without the relevant transaction and measurement analysis.
For consolidated reporting, a parent normally consolidates controlled subsidiaries, with specified
exemptions and exceptions. An investment entity generally measures qualifying subsidiary
investments at fair value through profit or loss, while a non-investment-entity subsidiary
providing services related to its parent’s investment activities can fall within the consolidation
exception to that exception (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026c, paras. 4–4B,
27–33). The investor’s entity status, the subsidiary’s activities and the complete exemption
conditions therefore belong among the required facts. None is determined by the 75 per cent
fraction.
Consolidation combines the relevant assets, liabilities, income, expenses and cash flows, elimi-
nates the parent’s investment against the relevant subsidiary equity, and removes intragroup
transactions and balances.
It also requires consistent policies and appropriate reporting
dates (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026c, paras. B86–B93). Potential rights used
in a control assessment do not automatically set the proportion allocated to non-controlling inter-
ests: the standard separately addresses existing ownership and instruments that already provide
access to ownership returns (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026c, paras. B89–B91).
This creates a concrete implementation consequence.
The existing selected group-period
reference assumes full ownership and the same supplied control edges and membership at
opening and closing; it supplies no non-controlling-interest method. A company acquired during
that period violates the assumption. A correct continuation must distinguish the period before
control, the acquisition measurements and the period consolidated afterward, rather than force
the new subsidiary into the opening group or omit its acquisition effects.
26.16.4
A controlled project is not automatically a business combination
Obtaining control does not settle whether the acquired set is a business. AASB 3 distinguishes
an in-scope business combination from acquiring an asset or group of assets, a common-control
combination, and specified investment-entity acquisitions. An asset-group acquisition under


PAGE 219
CHAPTER 26.
A FULLY SOLVED RISKY-DEBT MODEL
197
its scope paragraph does not create goodwill (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026h,
paras. 2–3).
The optional concentration test can establish that an acquired set is not a business when
substantially all of the gross-asset fair value is concentrated as specified. Failing that test does
not establish that the set is a business; the further assessment remains necessary. The underlying
analysis requires inputs and a substantive process, with different criteria depending on whether
outputs are present (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026h, paras. B7A–B12D). A
project described by a terminal cash payoff supplies neither the workforce and process evidence
nor the fair-value composition needed to decide the question.
For an in-scope business combination, the acquisition method requires identifying the acquirer
and control date, recognising and measuring the identifiable assets, liabilities and non-controlling
interest, and determining goodwill or a bargain-purchase gain. The goodwill calculation uses the
specified acquisition-date components, including any relevant previously held interest (Australian
Accounting Standards Board, 2026h, paras. 4–10, 32–33). The synthetic funding price, a 5
per cent pricing discount and a balanced four-party journal do not supply these missing
measurements. They remain separate economic evidence.
26.16.5
Keep dated authority and missing facts visible
The inspected AASB 18 consequential amendments change specified AASB 3 presentation
and disclosure references, add title-related footnotes in AASB 10 and AASB 127, and alter
accompanying AASB 10 guidance. They do not in that instrument rewrite the core control,
business-definition or separate-policy paragraphs used here (Australian Accounting Standards
Board, 2024a, Appendix D, pp. 57, 70, 101–102). That bounded observation cannot establish
that no other amendment applies.
AASB 2024-4 defers specified sale-or-contribution amendments involving an investor and its
associate or joint venture until annual periods beginning in 2028, with earlier application
permitted. It is not a general acquisition-of-control rule (Australian Accounting Standards
Board, 2024b, paras. 6–11). AASB 2026-3 changes a downstream associate/joint-venture fair-
value election, while AASB 2026-4 contains entity-specific presentation and application changes
and references AASB 2026-2 (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026d, p. 5) (Australian
Accounting Standards Board, 2026f, paras. 1–3). Completing that entity and edition chain is
still required before actual 2027 or 2028 admission.
The repaired conditional dependency reference consequently records supplied, dated assessments
of rights, control, reporting scope and acquisition type. Unknown endpoints retain alternative
control histories; missing measurements stay named requirements. It distinguishes a separate
cost-policy choice from evidence of cost, and a positive supplied control assessment from actual
source-backed admission. Its current results authorize no posting. This is the useful conclusion
for scenario design: unresolved accounting alternatives must remain linked to the rights, dates,
entities and measurements that could distinguish them, instead of being collapsed into whichever
journal first balances.1

correctness or complete standards coverage.

26.17
A control history must fit the reporting method
A supplied history of control can answer the continuity question without yet supplying a method
for consolidated reporting. The existing selected group-period calculation supports a stable
perimeter and full ownership. A conditional history must therefore be checked against that
scope before the simulator uses the calculation. Matching opening and closing control edges
cannot replace the intervening history.
The checked connection carries the original history request, actor, exact interval and source
identities into the group-report request. It identifies the same parent and subsidiary in both. It
also requires an explicit relation between the history’s reporting basis and the book-composition
measurement basis. Similar labels do not establish that those two premises mean the same
thing.
Time precision matters here too. The book period uses calendar dates, whereas the control history
can use exact instants. The reference requires an explicit fixed-offset calendar interpretation
and compares the resulting midnight boundaries exactly. A partial-day acquisition cannot be
rounded into whole-period control merely because the annual report’s date fields contain no
time of day.
The outcome depends on the whole interval. Missing coverage, conflicting evidence or unresolved
actor availability leave the control-history requirement unresolved. Continuous absence of control
stays outside this parent–subsidiary reporting route, even if an ownership record says 100 per
cent. A gain or loss requires changing-perimeter methods; this includes a loss followed by
regain when the opening and closing assessments are identical. Continuous control with partial
ownership requires non-controlling-interest methods beyond the present full-ownership reference.
Only continuous supplied control together with a separate full-ownership premise fits the existing
method’s control and ownership scope. Even that result is a candidate for further report checks.
It supplies no missing legal-book report, acquisition measurement or accounting authority, and
the scope check itself produces no consolidated statement. The original report can still refuse a
request whose control scope fits but whose other inputs are absent or incompatible.
This separates two sources of failure. If a valid unchanged-period fixture is refused, the existing
report or its inputs need diagnosis. If a history contains a mid-period acquisition and the
stable-perimeter report refuses it, the refusal exposes a missing financial method. The next
work must specify acquisition or loss accounting, dated member and flow allocation, ownership
and non-controlling interests, and the required measurements. A source-history check cannot
manufacture those amounts or authorize a journal merely because its identities and dates
match.2
26.18
Acquiring control changes the measurement and the pe-
riod
Suppose the financing arrangement eventually gives the creditor control of the debtor. The
debtor’s legal accounts do not thereby become the creditor’s accounts, and the new shares do
not supply an acquisition-date balance sheet. Consolidated reporting now needs to explain
which assets and liabilities enter the group, at what amounts, and from which instant the
acquired company’s income belongs in the group result. Multiplying its annual statement by a
2The conditional history-to-group connection, complete expected routes and unchanged/changed-perimeter
baseline checks are retained in data/accounting-master/control-group-boundary-20261027/. The selected compari-
son covers 23 cases and 15 refusal controls. Every scope result withholds source, accounting, posting and report
authorization; it is preparation for financial-method composition.

199
75 per cent shareholding would answer none of those questions. It would both omit part of the
controlled company’s activity and bring in activity from before control.
The smallest continuation considered here is one gain of control in an ordinary business
acquisition, with a single reporting currency, aligned reporting dates and no subsequent loss
of control or change in ownership rights during the selected interval. These are limits of the
first method. A purchase of assets that is not a business, a combination under common control,
an investment-entity exception and separate financial statements require their own treatments.
The business assessment and acquirer identification therefore precede the acquisition calculation.
The acquisition date follows the assessed date of obtaining control, rather than the share-issue
date by definition (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026h, paras. 2–9).
26.18.1
The acquisition balance needs its own measurements
Consider the consolidated acquisition worksheet at that date. Let A be the aggregate recognised
acquired assets and L the aggregate assumed liabilities, both expressed as positive magnitudes in
the same currency unit. Their amounts follow the applicable recognition and measurement rules,
including item-specific exceptions; an unchanged legal carrying amount is not automatically the
required acquisition amount. Let T denote measured consideration transferred, Q measured
non-controlling interests, and P the acquisition-date fair value of a qualifying previously held
equity interest. Under the ordinary business-acquisition branch, the excess described in AASB 3
paragraph 32 is
E = T + Q + P −(A −L).
(26.6)
The calculation compares the consideration and interests in the acquired business with its
recognised net assets. A positive excess can determine goodwill only when the route and
measurements support that recognition. A negative excess requires the reassessment specified
in paragraphs 34–36 before a bargain-purchase gain is recognised. Computing max(E, 0) and
putting the remainder into profit would skip that requirement (Australian Accounting Standards
Board, 2026h, paras. 10–21, 24–36).
The distinction between T and P matters particularly in the conversion example. A creditor
may hold debt immediately before obtaining shares. That debt is not, merely because it converts,
the previously held equity interest addressed by paragraph 42. Nor does the issuer’s transfer of
a carrying amount from debt to equity determine the holder’s acquisition consideration. The
contract and its financial-instrument treatment must establish what is transferred and which
effects belong to the business combination. Pre-existing relationships and separate transactions
require their own assessment (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026h, paras. 42, 51–53
and B50–B53). The face amount, an economic price and a carrying amount cannot silently fill
the same measurement slot.
Non-controlling interests introduce a further measurement choice. For qualifying present owner-
ship interests, a proportional-share election gives Q = q(A −L), where q is the non-controlling
fraction. A fair-value election instead requires a distinct measurement of Q (Australian Ac-
counting Standards Board, 2026h, para. 19). Substituting the proportional expression into
equation (26.6) gives
E = T + P −(1 −q)(A −L).
(26.7)
For a conditional 75 per cent ordinary interest, q = 1/4 and the acquired net-asset contribution
is 3(A −L)/4. This algebra does not establish that a particular preference share or other right
qualifies for the election. With full ownership, q = 0 and no non-controlling interest remains. An
unspecified prior interest does not become P = 0; that zero requires an explicit no-prior-equity
premise under the selected route.


PAGE 222
CHAPTER 26.
A FULLY SOLVED RISKY-DEBT MODEL
200
26.18.2
Only the controlled interval contributes subsidiary results
After the acquisition worksheet is established, consolidation incorporates the controlled com-
pany’s income and expenses from the date control is obtained. Those subsequent amounts follow
the acquisition-date basis: for example, depreciation uses the acquired asset amount recognised
in the consolidated statements (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026c, para. 20 and
B86–B88). A legal-book expense may therefore need a group adjustment even when the legal
journal itself is correct. The simulator must preserve the legal books and put the adjustment in
the separate consolidation worksheet.
Write R for the subsidiary’s legal profit over the exact post-control interval, and D for the
signed additional expense required to express that interval on the group basis. Positive D
reduces profit; negative D increases it. Let O be the legal other comprehensive income over the
same interval and U its signed group adjustment. All four quantities have the same currency
unit. The adjusted subsidiary contribution is
π = R −D,
ω = O + U.
(26.8)
This is a period and measurement reconciliation, not a forecast or a rule for dividing an annual
total. A whole-year R cannot be prorated by elapsed months unless an independently supported
method justifies that approximation. A journal recorded only by calendar date also needs an
ordering premise when control changes within that date; rounding the control instant would
conceal the ambiguity.
Under the deliberately limited assumption of constant ordinary present ownership rights, the
owners’ and non-controlling shares of π are (1 −q)π and qπ, and similarly for ω. Actual
allocation must consider the relevant rights and the potential-voting-rights exception; the
fraction of issued shares is not a universal allocation rule (Australian Accounting Standards
Board, 2026c, B89–B95). Let V be distributions to non-controlling interests, expressed as a
positive reduction, and M the other signed, supported changes in those interests within this
limited branch. Their selected reconciliation is then
Nclose = Q + q(R −D) + q(O + U) −V + M.
(26.9)
Starting with measured acquisition-date NCI, the equation adds its share of adjusted profit
and OCI and then accounts for direct changes. A sufficiently large loss may make the closing
amount negative; clipping that deficit at zero would contradict the attribution requirement
(Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026c, B94). The equation cannot establish whether
the supplied M or adjustment population is complete. Ownership transactions with continued
control, and a later loss of control, need their separate methods rather than an unexplained
entry in M.
26.18.3
Cash and revisions retain their own dates
Acquisition also changes the cash included in the group. The conversion of debt to equity is
a noncash transaction, but the acquired company may already hold cash. Its cash enters the
consolidated perimeter even when the conversion itself creates no receipt or payment. The
method must retain cash consideration, acquired cash and noncash consideration separately,
with the applicable acquisition cash presentation net of acquired cash.
Acquisitions and
disposals are not netted against one another merely because both change control (Australian
Accounting Standards Board, 2026b, paras. 39–44B). Cash transfers before control are not
retrospectively intragroup flows; transfers afterwards require supported counterpart matching
before elimination.


PAGE 223
CHAPTER 26.
A FULLY SOLVED RISKY-DEBT MODEL
201
The acquisition worksheet must also distinguish new information about conditions at the
acquisition date from later events. Provisional amounts and measurement-period adjustments
follow their own rules, including the one-year maximum, rather than a general permission to
rewrite the past (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026h, paras. 45–50). A revised
measurement can therefore have an acquisition-date effect and a later information-availability
date. The earlier actor view must remain reproducible, using the temporal distinctions developed
in Chapter 17.
26.18.4
A transaction date does not select an edition
The conditional AASB analysis preserves several dates because they answer different questions.
For example, the retained AASB 2026-2 compilation was prepared on 21 October 2026, has
a compilation date of 31 December 2027, and generally applies to annual reporting periods
beginning on or after 1 July 2029, with earlier application permitted under its terms. These
dates cannot be substituted for one another. Its amendments to AASB 3 and AASB 10 concern
application of the Conceptual Framework; entity classification and elections remain necessary
inputs (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026e, paras. 3, 18–19, 25; compilation details).
The inspected interaction with AASB 2026-4 also imposes a specific AASB 18 condition for
early use of that amendment (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026f, paras. 3, 25).
Other branches remain visible. AASB 20’s Appendix D adds a business-combination treatment
for regulatory assets and liabilities, with its own application timing and an additional dependency
when applied before AASB 18. The retained appendix alone does not establish the entity’s
eligibility or the complete transition treatment (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2026g,
Appendix D, amendments to AASB 3).
AASB 1061’s Appendix C changes tier wording
relevant to AASB 10; it does not supply the full Tier 3 scope or measurement rules (Australian
Accounting Standards Board, 2026a, Appendix C, amendment to AG1). Similarly, the inspected
sale/contribution amendment chain affects a particular loss-of-control branch, rather than
replacing every acquisition method (Australian Accounting Standards Board, 2024b, paras. 6–8).
The dated authority matrix retains these alternatives and exact paragraph anchors. A future
transaction’s policy must be checked against its entity, annual period, elections and then-current
applicable requirements.
26.18.5
What the selected reference establishes
The conditional expression reference binds the original control-history request and the exact
gain-of-control instant before constructing these relations. Each amount remains an explicitly
named unknown, with its role, currency, unit, timing, measurement basis and source identity.
Future or unknown source availability remains unresolved for the selected actor. Missing assets,
consideration, tax effects or adjustment methods cannot be supplied by the fact that an equation
balances.
Independent coefficient maps check both NCI elections, qualifying prior equity, the full-ownership
limit and the post-control reconciliations. Adverse requests test prior debt labelled as equity, pre-
control profit leakage, sub-day rounding, inconsistent currency or ownership, source alteration
and attempted insertion of an unmeasured face value. All source, accounting, posting and
report authorization remains withheld.3

The next financial-method comparison must expand the aggregate unknowns into an identified
item population, preserve every legal account, and reconcile a complete dated acquisition
worksheet and period report against independently authored expectations. That comparison
must include consideration and separate transactions, acquisition-basis expenses, NCI, cash,
eliminations, taxes and revisions. The expressions developed here identify the measurements
that comparison needs; they do not replace it.
